Table 1
Foreign Exchange Gains and Losses
1. Q: What is the purpose of an SOP for realized and unrealized foreign exchange gains and losses?
A: Show answer
The purpose of SOP 034 is to establish an auditable process for selecting exchange rates, converting foreign currency transactions, calculating settlement differences, remeasuring outstanding monetary balances, preparing journals, reconciling results, and reporting foreign exchange gains and losses. The procedure connects accounting policy decisions with operational controls, source-rate evidence, approvals, and system processing. A mathematically correct calculation does not, by itself, establish that the correct rate, accounting classification, or authorization was used. The supplied document is explicitly a fictional demonstration, not an adopted organizational policy. Its example entity, transactions, exchange rates, journal references, approvals, and test outcomes must not be treated as actual evidence. Before implementation, confirm the applicable accounting framework, legal requirements, functional currency, rate-source policy, account mappings, and authorized responsibilities for the actual NGO.
Study instruction:
- "Review the SOP's purpose, scope, assumptions, and adoption conditions before using any example as an operational procedure."
Key point:
- The SOP establishes controls for measurement, posting, reconciliation, and reporting.
- A fictional example is not evidence of organizational approval or applicable accounting treatment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
2. Q: What is the difference between realized and unrealized foreign exchange gains and losses?
A: Show answer
A realized foreign exchange gain or loss generally arises when a foreign currency transaction is settled and the amount received or paid differs from the relevant carrying amount immediately before settlement. An unrealized gain or loss generally arises when an eligible outstanding foreign currency monetary item is remeasured at a reporting date and its functional-currency carrying amount changes. Realized and unrealized describe different stages of a transaction's measurement history; they do not necessarily determine separate recognition destinations under every accounting framework. Both calculations must follow the applicable accounting requirements and organizational policy. The distinction is especially important when a receivable or payable was revalued in an earlier period. The subsequent settlement calculation must start from its latest recognized carrying amount, rather than treating the entire difference from initial recognition as a new realized gain or loss. Reporting classifications and disclosures must also be verified against the applicable framework.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an outstanding foreign currency receivable before settlement with the same receivable after cash collection, including any previous revaluation."
Key point:
- Unrealized differences arise from eligible remeasurement; realized differences generally arise upon settlement.
- Previous recognized differences must be included when determining the remaining settlement gain or loss.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Currency and Financial Reporting
3. Q: Which accounting authority should determine the treatment of foreign exchange gains and losses?
A: Show answer
The entity must first identify the accounting framework that applies to its financial statements and reporting purpose. If the relevant framework is IPSAS, consult the applicable version of IPSAS 4, The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates, alongside the directly relevant UN IPSAS Finance Manual guidance where appropriate. However, the UN Finance Manual describes UN-specific policies and operational processes; it should not automatically be treated as the adopted policy of an independent NGO. An Indonesian foundation must separately assess the applicable Indonesian financial reporting requirements, legal obligations, donor conditions, and organizational accounting policy. The documented decision should identify the authoritative source, applicable version, effective date, affected transactions, functional currency, measurement method, presentation consequences, reviewer, and approver. Any unresolved question about framework applicability or exchangeability should remain explicitly on hold instead of being converted into an assumed accounting rule.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a source register identifying the governing framework, authoritative text, effective version, applicability decision, and approval evidence."
Key point:
- Applicable accounting authority takes precedence over example-workbook logic.
- UN-specific practices and donor reporting rules must not automatically be applied as universal accounting requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Accounting Policies and Financial Reporting
- IPSASB — Improvements to IPSAS Accounting Standards, Volume 10
4. Q: How should functional currency and presentation currency be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Functional currency is the currency of the primary economic environment in which an entity operates, determined using the criteria of its applicable accounting framework. Presentation currency is the currency used to present the financial statements. The two currencies may be the same or different. Transaction measurement in an entity's functional currency is not automatically equivalent to translating a complete set of financial statements into a separate presentation currency. A policy should document the functional currency, presentation currency, relevant reporting perimeter, and approved accounting treatment. A donor's requirement to report a project budget in USD does not, by itself, establish the NGO's functional currency or authorize changes to its ledger. If functional and presentation currencies differ, the entity must separately evaluate the requirements for translating financial statements and any foreign operations, including the applicable treatment of resulting differences.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one foreign currency transaction from the functional-currency ledger to a donor report and identify any separate presentation-currency conversion."
Key point:
- Functional-currency accounting and presentation-currency translation are different processes.
- Donor reporting requirements do not automatically determine the currency basis of the general ledger.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Functional Currency and Presentation Currency
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Treasury and Foreign Exchange Processes
5. Q: How should a foreign currency transaction be initially recognized in the functional-currency ledger?
A: Show answer
Under the applicable foreign currency accounting requirements, an entity generally translates a foreign currency transaction into its functional currency using the exchange rate at the transaction date. The transaction date is determined by the recognition requirements for the underlying transaction; it should not be selected automatically from the date on which an employee happens to enter the invoice. The accounting file should preserve the foreign currency, amount, transaction date, approved exchange-rate observation, quote convention, conversion calculation, resulting functional-currency amount, supporting document, and journal reference. Where the entity's authorized policy permits an approximation, such as an average rate, confirm that the approach is allowed and reasonably represents the relevant transaction-date rates. Maintain a clear distinction between the rate used for initial recognition, a later settlement rate, and a subsequent reporting-date closing rate. The actual entity's policy and effective standards must be verified before these general principles are operationalized.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a foreign currency invoice through its recognition date, approved rate, calculation, supporting document, and posted journal."
Key point:
- Initial recognition uses the applicable transaction-date conversion basis.
- Rate selection must be supported by an approved policy and traceable evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Currency Transactions
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Sources and Treasury Processes
6. Q: Why must foreign currency monetary items be distinguished from non-monetary items?
A: Show answer
Monetary items generally involve a right to receive, or an obligation to deliver, a fixed or determinable number of currency units. Examples can include cash, bank balances, trade receivables, and trade payables. Eligible monetary items denominated in foreign currencies are generally retranslated at the applicable closing rate at the reporting date under the relevant framework. Non-monetary items follow different requirements depending on their measurement basis. A non-monetary asset measured at historical cost in a foreign currency is generally not retranslated at every reporting date solely because the exchange rate has changed. A non-monetary item measured at fair value may require translation using the rate at the date its fair value was measured. The precise classification and resulting treatment must be established from the governing standards. A system must therefore classify the underlying item before sending it into the monetary revaluation process.
Study instruction:
- "Classify cash, receivables, payables, historical-cost equipment, and fair-value items, then document the applicable measurement rule for each."
Key point:
- Currency denomination alone does not make every asset or liability eligible for monetary revaluation.
- Classification and measurement basis must be verified before the calculation runs.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Monetary and Non-Monetary Foreign Currency Items
7. Q: Which exchange rate should be used at initial recognition, settlement, and the reporting date?
A: Show answer
The appropriate exchange rate depends on the accounting event and the governing framework. Initial recognition generally uses the rate applicable at the transaction date. Settlement measurement uses the applicable settlement transaction information and compares it with the relevant carrying amount immediately before settlement. Reporting-date remeasurement generally uses the applicable closing rate for eligible outstanding monetary items. These rates must not be interchanged simply because one is more convenient or more readily available in a spreadsheet. An organization may maintain separate rates for accounting, treasury, donor budgeting, tax, or statutory reporting, provided each rate's purpose, authority, and application are documented. The rate register should identify the currency pair, rate type, relevant date and timestamp, quotation units, source, effective period, and approval status. If the accounting event or rate basis is ambiguous, hold the affected calculation until the policy question is resolved.
Study instruction:
- "Create a transaction timeline showing the initial recognition rate, any reporting-date closing rate, and the eventual settlement basis."
Key point:
- The applicable rate depends on the accounting event and authorized measurement policy.
- Donor, tax, treasury, and ledger rates cannot be substituted for one another without an approved basis.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Currency Translation and Measurement
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange Rates and Treasury Operations
8. Q: How should the hierarchy for approved foreign exchange rate sources be established?
A: Show answer
The hierarchy should identify which source is permitted for each currency pair, rate type, accounting purpose, and relevant date. Depending on the approved policy, sources might include a recognized market-data provider, an official operational rate, an authorized bank quote, or an evidenced rate actually obtained in a permitted local conversion. A source's existence does not establish that it is suitable for the transaction in question. The policy should explain how unavailable, delayed, corrected, or conflicting observations are handled. It should also specify source ownership, permitted access, independent review, approval, retention, and change control. The organization must assess whether its selected source is appropriate under its accounting framework rather than copying UN-specific exchange-rate arrangements without authorization. A rate used for a historical transaction must remain traceable to the approved observation and policy version that governed its use.
Study instruction:
- "Build a rate-source hierarchy for the organization's currencies and document the permitted purpose, evidence, reviewer, and fallback for each source."
Key point:
- A valid-looking market quote is not automatically an approved accounting rate.
- Each source must be approved for its particular use and applicable policy.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Sources and Treasury Operations
9. Q: What information must be retained for each exchange-rate observation?
A: Show answer
Each observation should have a unique identifier and preserve the raw rate information, source identity, currency pair, rate type, quotation convention, quoted value, publication or effective date, timestamp and time zone where available, retrieval time, precision, and applicable approval. The record should also identify whether the rate was published directly, manually entered, or derived through a documented conversion formula. If a source revises a previously published observation, preserve both the original and revised versions and record the impact assessment. Duplicate observations should be identified through a controlled key rather than deleted silently. A downstream calculation should reference the observation actually used, not only a mutable cell containing a rate. These controls help a reviewer reconstruct why a particular functional-currency amount was calculated and whether subsequent corrections were properly handled.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect one rate observation record and verify that the source, currency pair, date, quote units, version, and downstream calculation can be independently reproduced."
Key point:
- Rate lineage must connect original evidence to each calculation and posting.
- Corrected observations must preserve their historical versions and affected records.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Master Data
10. Q: How should currency-pair direction, quotation convention, and rounding be controlled?
A: Show answer
The calculation model must explicitly state what the rate means. For example, a rate of IDR 16,000 per USD 1 expresses functional-currency units for each foreign-currency unit when IDR is the functional currency and USD is foreign. The conversion of USD 5,000 at that rate gives IDR 80,000,000 before any required rounding or other adjustment. An inverse quote cannot be used as though it were the direct quote; the transformation must be explicit and validated. Store foreign amounts and exchange-rate values at appropriate precision, apply the documented rounding convention at the prescribed stage, and preserve unrounded results where needed for review. Binary floating-point calculations should not be used as the authoritative representation when exact decimal arithmetic is required. Test direct and inverse rates, zero or negative values, excessive precision, and mismatched currency pairs.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a foreign currency amount using both the stated quote and its inverse, then demonstrate how a quotation-direction error changes the result."
Key point:
- Currency units and quotation direction must be explicit.
- Precision and rounding rules must be controlled, tested, and reproducible.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Master Data
11. Q: What controls prevent an incorrect or stale exchange rate from entering the calculation process?
A: Show answer
The rate validation process should confirm source approval, currency-pair compatibility, quote direction, relevant date, rate type, freshness, permitted precision, positive value, and completeness of evidence. It should also identify duplicate records and suspicious changes from prior observations. A plausibility threshold can be useful as an alert, but it must be based on an approved control methodology and should not be confused with a legitimate market quote. A rate that passes arithmetic validation may still be inappropriate for the intended accounting purpose. Any failed validation must produce a clear exception record identifying the affected currency pair, transactions, calculation run, responsible owner, and required action. If there is no approved fallback for the applicable date and purpose, the affected process should remain on hold until authorized evidence and review are obtained.
Study instruction:
- "Test a rate-validation checklist using a valid observation, an inverted pair, a stale observation, a duplicate, and a materially unusual rate."
Key point:
- Validation must test both data integrity and policy applicability.
- Missing or invalid rate evidence must not be silently replaced with a convenient value.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Master Data and Valuation
12. Q: How is a realized foreign exchange gain or loss calculated when a receivable or payable is settled?
A: Show answer
The calculation compares the functional-currency amount associated with settlement with the carrying amount of the foreign currency item immediately before settlement, after incorporating all relevant previously recognized remeasurements and adjustments. For a receivable, collecting more functional-currency value than the carrying amount generally creates a settlement gain; collecting less generally creates a loss. For a payable, the direction depends on the functional-currency amount needed to settle the obligation relative to its carrying amount. Transaction fees and bank charges should be identified separately so they are not automatically classified as exchange losses. The settlement schedule should show the foreign currency units settled, current carrying amount, settlement date and rate evidence, actual functional-currency cash movement, calculated difference, and journal reference. Any residual foreign currency amount must remain open if the settlement is partial.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate a receivable settlement gain and a payable settlement gain independently using carrying amounts immediately before settlement."
Key point:
- Settlement differences are calculated from the latest relevant carrying amount.
- Fees, FX differences, and remaining open balances must be distinguished.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Exchange Gains and Losses
13. Q: How does the fictional EUR receivable example demonstrate the prevention of double counting?
A: Show answer
The supplied SOP describes a fictional EUR receivable with an initial functional-currency value of IDR 170,000,000 and an earlier reporting-date revaluation gain of IDR 5,000,000. After revaluation, its carrying amount is IDR 175,000,000. The later collection produces IDR 176,500,000, resulting in an additional settlement gain of IDR 1,500,000 relative to the latest carrying amount. The cumulative gain across the full lifecycle is IDR 6,500,000: the earlier IDR 5,000,000 revaluation gain plus the later IDR 1,500,000 settlement gain. The settlement journal debits cash for IDR 176,500,000 and credits the receivable for IDR 175,000,000, with the remaining IDR 1,500,000 credited to the relevant FX gain account under the approved accounting mapping. These amounts are synthetic and are included only to illustrate the calculation logic.
Study instruction:
- "Reperform the fictional EUR receivable calculation and verify that the cumulative gain equals the sum of the earlier revaluation gain and the settlement difference."
Key point:
- The post-revaluation carrying amount becomes the settlement comparison basis.
- Previous FX gains must not be recognized a second time upon collection.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Currency Gains and Losses
14. Q: What can be learned from the fictional USD payable example?
A: Show answer
The supplied SOP illustrates a USD 4,000 payable initially recognized at IDR 15,800 per USD, producing a liability of IDR 63,200,000. At the fictional reporting date, a closing rate of IDR 16,100 results in a carrying amount of IDR 64,400,000 and an increase in the liability of IDR 1,200,000, representing an exchange loss under the illustrated assumptions. The later settlement occurs at IDR 16,050, requiring IDR 64,200,000 in cash. Relative to the IDR 64,400,000 liability immediately before settlement, the cash payment produces a settlement gain of IDR 200,000. The cumulative effect is a net loss of IDR 1,000,000, calculated as the IDR 1,200,000 revaluation loss less the IDR 200,000 settlement gain. All rates and amounts are fictional and must not be treated as real market data or a mandatory accounting example.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the USD payable example from initial recognition through revaluation and settlement, checking the liability and FX result after each stage."
Key point:
- A payable's exchange loss can be partly offset by a later settlement gain.
- The cumulative result must reconcile across the full transaction lifecycle.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Foreign Currency Measurement
15. Q: How should partial foreign currency settlements be handled?
A: Show answer
A partial settlement reduces only the foreign currency quantity that has actually been settled and matched to the underlying obligation. The settlement worksheet should identify the original open item, quantity or amount settled, residual quantity, carrying amount allocated to the settled portion, settlement value, realized FX difference, and remaining balance. The allocation should follow a consistent method supported by the applicable accounting policy and system design. The unsettled portion continues to be tracked and remains subject to subsequent measurement if it is an eligible monetary item. A system must not close an entire invoice merely because a partial payment was recorded. It must also prevent the same settlement from being included twice in the calculation. Bank fees, withholding, credit notes, disputes, or deductions must be analyzed separately to determine their impact on settlement quantity and value.
Study instruction:
- "Create a partial-settlement worksheet that reconciles the original foreign currency amount to the amount settled and the remaining open balance."
Key point:
- Settlement closes only the matched portion of the underlying item.
- Residual balances remain subject to the applicable monitoring and revaluation rules.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Workflows
16. Q: How should outstanding foreign currency monetary balances be revalued at period end?
A: Show answer
Begin with a complete and approved population of outstanding foreign currency monetary items as of the reporting date. Confirm that each item is still open, identify its foreign currency amount and existing functional-currency carrying amount, obtain the applicable closing rate, calculate the required translated amount, and determine the difference from the current carrying amount. Reconcile the population to the general ledger and supporting subledgers before posting. The process should exclude items that have already been settled, items incorrectly classified as monetary, and duplicate records. It should also identify items with missing documentation or unresolved rate-source issues. The calculation method and recognition destination must reflect the applicable accounting framework. A reporting-date revaluation should be reproducible from retained input data, policy versions, rate observations, calculation results, and independent review evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a reporting-date population of eligible open monetary items and independently recalculate the closing carrying amount for a sample."
Key point:
- Period-end revaluation applies to eligible monetary items that remain outstanding.
- Completeness, classification, closing-rate evidence, and reconciliation are release prerequisites.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Valuation and General Ledger Processes
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Currency Measurement
17. Q: How should an unrealized foreign exchange revaluation journal be prepared and reviewed?
A: Show answer
The journal should reflect the change between the eligible monetary item's current carrying amount and its properly calculated reporting-date amount. For example, if an eligible foreign currency receivable increases in functional-currency value, the debit and credit accounts depend on the applicable accounting treatment and approved account mapping. The journal preparer must document the calculation, account selection, reporting period, currency, rate observation, reference to the open item, and the reason for the difference. The reviewer should verify the population, arithmetic, classification, rate evidence, period, debit-credit balance, and consistency with the approved policy. If the accounting system reverses revaluation entries in a subsequent period, the reversal mechanism and next-period treatment must be verified rather than assumed. The posting must not proceed while material exceptions or required approvals remain unresolved.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a sample unrealized FX journal to the open item, closing rate, recalculation, approved account mapping, reviewer sign-off, and posting reference."
Key point:
- A journal must be supported by the approved calculation and correct accounting mapping.
- Reversal and subsequent-period treatment must follow documented policy and system configuration.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Adjustments and Period-End Processes
18. Q: How can an organization prevent double counting between revaluation gains or losses and settlement gains or losses?
A: Show answer
The control should link each original foreign currency item to its entire measurement history. The system should retain initial recognition, previous revaluation adjustments, partial settlements, corrections, and the latest carrying amount. When settlement occurs, the calculation should use the carrying amount allocated to the settled units immediately before settlement. The previously recognized revaluation difference should not be recognized again merely because the item has now been paid or collected. Reconciliation should compare the cumulative effect of initial recognition, previous measurement changes, settlement differences, and any subsequent correction. A duplicate-settlement check and a revaluation-population check help prevent the same item from being processed incorrectly in both workflows. Any unresolved difference between the lifecycle schedule and general ledger must remain an open exception until independently explained and resolved.
Study instruction:
- "Build a transaction lifecycle reconciliation showing initial recognition, every revaluation, all settlements, and the cumulative recognized FX result."
Key point:
- The same economic FX difference must not be recognized twice.
- Settlement calculations must use the latest applicable carrying amount.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls
19. Q: How are non-monetary assets measured at historical cost treated at reporting dates?
A: Show answer
A non-monetary asset measured at historical cost in a foreign currency is generally translated using the applicable rate at the original recognition date and is not retranslated at every reporting date merely because the exchange rate has changed. This is different from the reporting-date remeasurement of eligible monetary assets and liabilities. For example, historical-cost equipment purchased in USD should not automatically enter a routine monetary revaluation run. Other accounting adjustments, such as depreciation, impairment, or a required remeasurement under another policy, may still affect the accounting value but do not make the item an ordinary monetary balance. The organization must identify the underlying asset's measurement basis and the requirements of its applicable accounting framework. System classification controls should block historical-cost non-monetary items from being revalued by an indiscriminate exchange-rate routine.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the reporting-date treatment of a foreign currency receivable and historical-cost equipment, documenting why only the qualifying item enters the monetary revaluation process."
Key point:
- Historical-cost non-monetary items are not automatically retranslated at the closing rate.
- The asset's measurement basis determines the appropriate procedure.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Currency Measurement
20. Q: How should a non-monetary item measured at fair value be treated when foreign currency is involved?
A: Show answer
A non-monetary item measured at fair value in a foreign currency generally uses the exchange rate applicable at the date on which the fair value was measured, subject to the applicable accounting framework. The entity must therefore identify the relevant valuation date, approved valuation evidence, measurement basis, and associated currency rate. It should not mechanically apply the reporting-date closing rate merely because the accounting close is taking place. The resulting exchange effect may follow the recognition destination associated with the underlying fair-value change under the applicable requirements. The journal and reporting presentation must not be inferred solely from whether the exchange difference is informally called realized or unrealized. A policy should explain how the valuation date and exchange-rate evidence are connected, who reviews them, and how the result is reconciled to the relevant asset or liability record.
Study instruction:
- "Select one foreign currency fair-value item and trace its valuation date, exchange-rate evidence, accounting calculation, and recognition destination."
Key point:
- A fair-value item's translation basis is linked to the applicable valuation date.
- Currency differences must be considered with the underlying measurement and recognition requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Fair Value and Foreign Currency Measurement
21. Q: How should foreign-operation translation differences be distinguished from transaction and settlement differences?
A: Show answer
Translating the financial statements of a foreign operation or translating an entity's financial statements into a presentation currency is conceptually different from settling an individual foreign currency receivable or remeasuring a monetary payable. Foreign-operation translation may involve assets, liabilities, income, and expenses under specific framework requirements. The resulting exchange differences can have a prescribed recognition and presentation treatment that differs from ordinary transaction gains and losses. A system should therefore maintain separate classifications and reporting paths for transaction-level exchange differences and translation differences. Donor budget conversions or management reporting conversions should also remain identifiable as separate reporting processes. The organization must determine the applicable presentation and disclosure requirements before configuring financial statement mapping or reporting aggregation. An exchange difference must not be classified as a settlement event merely because it arose during a reporting period.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an individual payable settlement with translation of a foreign operation, identifying the underlying event, calculation basis, and reporting treatment."
Key point:
- Transaction settlement, monetary-item revaluation, and foreign-operation translation are different accounting processes.
- Reporting classifications must follow the governing framework rather than a generic FX label.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Currency Translation and Financial Reporting
- IPSASB — Improvements to IPSAS Accounting Standards, Volume 10
22. Q: What should happen when the required exchange rate is missing, delayed, or unavailable?
A: Show answer
The process should identify the missing or unavailable observation and determine which currencies, items, transactions, periods, and reports are affected. The responsible rate owner should preserve the error or source snapshot, document attempts to obtain the required information, and check whether an explicitly approved fallback is available for the same purpose, date, currency pair, and rate hierarchy. A previous business-day rate, bank quote, approximate average, or manually entered value must not be substituted automatically. Where no applicable approved alternative exists, the affected measurement, journal, or close certification should remain on hold. The incident record should state its owner, due date, supporting evidence, decision authority, and the conditions required to resume processing. Following recovery, reperform the affected calculations, reconcile the resulting journals, and independently confirm that the issue is closed with evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Simulate an unavailable closing rate and document the approved investigation, fallback decision, escalation, and post-recovery verification."
Key point:
- A missing rate is a controlled exception, not permission to guess.
- Processing resumes only when the applicable rate basis and evidence are approved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Master Data and Treasury Processes
23. Q: How should unusual exchange-rate movements and rate outliers be investigated?
A: Show answer
A significant rate movement should trigger verification rather than an automatic override or rejection. Check the source, effective date, publication time, currency pair, quote convention, market context, prior observations, and whether the value reflects a genuine change or a data-quality problem. A rule that flags values outside a configured interval should be identified as an analytical control, not as evidence that the actual rate must fall within that interval. The reviewer should distinguish plausible volatility from a mismatched pair, decimal error, stale observation, duplicated feed, or corrected publication. For material exceptions, preserve the original and candidate records, quantify the affected transactions and journals, document the investigation, and obtain the required decision. Synthetic plausibility ranges in the supplied SOP are test fixtures only and must not be used as live market tolerances.
Study instruction:
- "Investigate a synthetic rate outlier and document the tests that distinguish genuine volatility from a wrong quote or invalid source record."
Key point:
- Outlier alerts initiate investigation; they do not establish the correct accounting rate.
- Test thresholds must not be presented as actual market evidence or approved operational limits.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Sources and Valuation
24. Q: How should a currency that is not readily exchangeable be handled?
A: Show answer
A restriction on exchangeability requires analysis under the applicable standard and the entity's actual circumstances. The organization must determine whether the relevant currency can be exchanged for another currency at the measurement date, for the specified purpose, through a permitted mechanism and in the required quantity. It should document available sources, legal and practical restrictions, potential alternatives, and the effects on measurement and disclosure. A convenient market quote or the rate used in a prior period must not be selected without an authorized basis. The matter may require specialist accounting advice, especially when an official rate and the rate available for permitted transactions diverge materially. The supplied SOP's instruction to hold unresolved cases is a control safeguard; the precise accounting treatment must be established from the effective authoritative requirements rather than assumed from the example.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a documented analysis of an exchangeability restriction, including the measurement date, permitted exchange mechanisms, evidence, and approval requirements."
Key point:
- Exchangeability must be assessed for the relevant currency, purpose, date, and quantity.
- Do not substitute a convenient rate without authoritative support and appropriate review.
Source links:
- IPSASB — Improvements to IPSAS Accounting Standards, Volume 10
25. Q: Under what conditions should a manual exchange-rate override be permitted?
A: Show answer
A manual override should be allowed only when the applicable policy explicitly permits it and the required evidence, scope, and authority are documented. The request should identify the original rate observation, proposed replacement, reason, currency pair, transaction population, date, affected accounts and reports, intended duration, and potential downstream effects. A competent independent reviewer should assess the proposal and an authorized approver should approve it under a verified delegation. The implementer should not authorize their own override or alter historical evidence. The process should retain both original and replacement values, test results, approval records, and an expiry or review condition where applicable. If the override is outside policy or lacks required evidence, it must be rejected or held. Subsequent calculations and reports must use the authorized result with traceable references.
Study instruction:
- "Review a proposed override and verify its justification, scope, reviewer, approver, effective period, test results, and audit trail."
Key point:
- Overrides require an explicit policy basis, independent review, and authorized approval.
- Original rate evidence and the full change history must be preserved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls and Approvals
26. Q: What should an end-to-end month-end foreign exchange close process include?
A: Show answer
An effective close process begins with an approved reporting period and a complete population of foreign currency transactions and outstanding monetary balances. Accounting and Treasury should confirm rate-source availability, current rate observations, eligible items, settlements, residual open balances, and required revaluations. The preparer then generates calculations and proposed journals, while an independent reviewer checks population completeness, rate evidence, quote direction, arithmetic, account mappings, and exceptions. After approval, the authorized posting process records the journals. Finance subsequently reconciles subledgers, general ledger balances, settlement records, revaluation results, and reporting outputs. Any open difference is assigned to a responsible owner with a due date and action plan. Period-end certification should occur only when required evidence and controls are complete and material exceptions have been resolved or formally escalated under an authorized policy.
Study instruction:
- "Map the monthly FX close from source-data collection through revaluation, review, posting, reconciliation, exception resolution, and certification."
Key point:
- The close must link rate evidence, population control, calculation, journal posting, and final reconciliation.
- Unresolved material exceptions must not be concealed by a close-status flag.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Period-End Processes
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Treasury and Foreign Exchange Processes
27. Q: How can the organization prove that all eligible monetary items were included in the revaluation run?
A: Show answer
The organization should construct the revaluation population from authoritative ledger and subledger data, reconcile its control totals, and validate classification and settlement status. Each included item should have an identifiable source transaction, currency, open amount, carrying amount, required reporting date, and applicable rate observation. Items excluded from the process should have a documented reason, such as being settled, classified as non-monetary, or held because the required evidence is incomplete. The reviewer should compare the eligible population against the relevant control accounts and investigate omissions, unexpected duplicates, unmatched records, and changes in the population since the prior run. A zero difference in total values does not prove completeness if offsetting errors exist; therefore, both aggregate reconciliation and transaction-level testing are necessary. The final population, exclusion list, calculation results, and review evidence must be retained.
Study instruction:
- "Reconcile the eligible foreign currency population to ledger controls and test a sample of included and excluded items against source documents."
Key point:
- Population completeness must be demonstrated before the FX run is released.
- Aggregate totals alone cannot identify all missing or duplicate transactions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Close Processes
28. Q: How should segregation of duties operate in the foreign exchange measurement and posting workflow?
A: Show answer
Responsibilities should be allocated so that one individual does not control source administration, policy decisions, preparation, review, approval, system implementation, posting, and final verification without appropriate safeguards. A rate administrator may capture observations, a preparer may calculate FX differences, a reviewer may independently examine the evidence and result, and an authorized approver may authorize posting under the organization's delegation. System access must match each role's responsibilities and be reviewed periodically. Where the number of staff makes full segregation impractical, compensating controls should be defined, such as independent review of all material entries and separate examination of bank and ledger reconciliations. Internal Audit should remain independent of operational preparation and approval. A job title alone is not proof of delegated authority; approval must be linked to a current, documented authorization record.
Study instruction:
- "Create a responsibility matrix for rate administration, calculations, approval, posting, and verification, and identify any incompatible access combinations."
Key point:
- Preparation, independent review, approval, and posting require clear ownership.
- Any limitation in segregation must be addressed by documented compensating controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Financial Process and Journal Controls
29. Q: How should FX journals, account mappings, and corrections be governed?
A: Show answer
The accounting system should use approved mappings for the relevant transaction type, account, currency, reporting purpose, and recognition category. Realized settlement differences, unrealized revaluation differences, transaction fees, foreign-operation translation differences, and other adjustments should not be merged into one generic account unless the applicable accounting requirements and approved chart of accounts support that presentation. Each journal must contain a unique reference, reporting period, source calculation, debit-credit amounts, currency, account codes, applicable project or donor dimensions, preparer, reviewer, approver, and posting reference. Corrections must preserve the original posting history and link the adjustment or reversal to the affected entry. The organization should validate mappings against the governing framework and reporting requirements before release. A balanced journal is necessary but not sufficient: the transaction population, classification, rate, amount, and authorization must also be correct.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a sample FX journal and trace its calculation, account mapping, dimensions, approval evidence, posting reference, and any correction history."
Key point:
- Journals must be both mathematically balanced and correctly classified.
- Corrections must preserve the original record and the reason for the change.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Entries and Journal Workflows
30. Q: How should foreign exchange differences be reconciled to donor budgets, tax calculations, and statutory reporting?
A: Show answer
Accounting, donor budget, tax, and statutory calculations may use different rules, dates, rates, or reporting units. The organization must identify the requirements governing each output and document how the separate calculations connect. For example, a donor may require an expenditure report converted at a contractually specified rate, while the general ledger uses the rate required by its accounting policy. The donor conversion must not silently rewrite the underlying ledger measurement. Similarly, tax calculations must follow the applicable tax requirements rather than automatically use the accounting closing rate. A reporting bridge should identify the source ledger balance, the separate rate basis, conversion calculation, adjustment or classification, supporting agreement or regulation, reviewer, and output reference. Differences between reports should be explained and reconciled rather than forced to zero through an unsupported adjustment.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a reconciliation bridge between the ledger FX result and a donor or tax report, documenting the separate rate basis and explaining each difference."
Key point:
- Accounting, donor, and tax conversion methods must remain distinguishable.
- Reporting differences require an evidenced bridge and must not be hidden by changing ledger values.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Foreign Currency Translation
31. Q: How can Excel and Power Query support foreign exchange calculations and reconciliation without determining the accounting policy?
A: Show answer
Power Query can collect transaction files, rate observations, open-item schedules, and ledger exports, then standardize fields, filter records, merge approved reference data, flag potential duplicates, and create a repeatable calculation population. Excel tables and formulas can support controlled calculations, reconciliation schedules, and exception reporting. However, data transformation and arithmetic cannot establish that the accounting framework, rate source, transaction date, or measurement classification is correct. Those decisions must remain governed by approved policy and competent accounting review. Each refresh should retain its source references, extraction date, input counts, control totals, transformation version, and exceptions. Where source columns change or a transformation produces an unexpected output, the process should stop or flag the issue rather than generate a silently incorrect report. The final model should support reproducibility and independent calculation testing.
Study instruction:
- "Design a controlled Power Query flow using transaction data, rate observations, and ledger balances, then reconcile record counts and monetary totals before and after transformation."
Key point:
- Power Query supports repeatable data acquisition and transformation.
- Software output does not replace accounting policy validation or independent review.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Foreign Currency Accounting Principles
32. Q: How should Excel tables, master data, and validation controls be designed for an FX register?
A: Show answer
Use structured tables with explicit columns for transaction identifiers, entity, ledger, source system, source document, counterparty, transaction class, recognition date, foreign currency, foreign amount, functional currency, rate type, rate value, rate date and time, source observation, quotation convention, conversion method, calculation result, carrying amount, accounting classification, and journal or reconciliation references. Required fields should be validated before a calculation or posting is authorized. Controlled lists should be used for currency codes, transaction types, statuses, and approved rate categories. Source evidence and approval records should be referenced rather than replaced by informal free text. Where possible, data should be stored in its proper type and immutable transaction IDs should be retained. Validation messages and exception reports should identify what is missing and who must correct it. Changes to the data structure need independent testing so that refreshes do not silently change accounting outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Create an FX transaction table and rate master with required fields, controlled statuses, unique identifiers, and validation tests for missing or inconsistent data."
Key point:
- Standardized master data strengthens matching, reconciliation, and repeatability.
- Required fields and controlled values help prevent invalid transactions from reaching posting.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Power Query Data Preparation and Transformation
33. Q: How can automated FX processing prevent duplicate revaluation runs or journal postings?
A: Show answer
Each processing run should have a unique identifier derived from the approved entity, ledger, reporting period, valuation date, source population, policy version, and relevant calculation configuration. The system should detect whether an equivalent run has already been posted or whether inputs have materially changed since the previous run. Repeating the same calculation should not create duplicate journals merely because a user retried the process after a timeout or application error. If corrected inputs require a new run, preserve the earlier run, document the differences, calculate the impact, and follow the approved reversal or correction procedure. The system should distinguish a validation-only run from an authorized posting run and verify the permitted status transition. Independent testing should demonstrate that identical inputs do not create a second posting and that a legitimate revised run is linked to its predecessor.
Study instruction:
- "Test a repeated FX run with unchanged inputs, followed by a formally approved run with corrected inputs, and verify that the ledger history remains traceable."
Key point:
- Repeating an unchanged process must not duplicate a journal.
- A revised run needs an identifiable version, documented impact, and authorized correction pathway.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
34. Q: What should happen when a previously published exchange rate is revised after the period has been processed?
A: Show answer
The organization should preserve the original observation and append the corrected observation with its publication information, reason for revision, and relationship to the previous version. It should then identify every affected transaction, calculation run, journal, reconciliation, donor report, and financial reporting period. The accounting owner must determine, under the applicable framework and correction policy, whether the change requires an adjustment, a formal correction, disclosure, or another action. The correction must not be implemented by overwriting the old rate or silently replacing historical reports. A change assessment should record materiality, affected amounts, responsible persons, reviewer, approval, testing, and communication to recipients of prior reports where applicable. Closure requires evidence that the approved correction was implemented and that downstream balances and reports were re-reconciled.
Study instruction:
- "Simulate a rate publication correction and identify its impact on historical transactions, journals, reconciliations, and previously distributed reports."
Key point:
- Rate revisions must preserve the original observation and its lineage.
- Historical changes require impact assessment and an authorized correction route.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Adjustments and Reconciliation
35. Q: How should foreign exchange exceptions be recorded, escalated, and closed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a centralized register for issues such as missing rates, mismatched currency pairs, incorrect quote direction, incomplete transactions, duplicate processing, unusual rate movements, unsupported overrides, failed journals, and unexplained reconciliation differences. Each record should include a unique issue ID, date, affected entity and period, currency pair, impacted population, potential financial effect, severity, responsible owner, due date, required action, reviewer, and evidence reference. Statuses such as DRAFT, REVIEW, APPROVED, ACTIVE, HOLD, REJECTED, SUPERSEDED, and CLOSED should have defined meanings and authorized transition rules. An item must not be marked closed merely because it was carried forward to the next reporting period. Closure should require correction or an approved disposition, retesting where relevant, independent review, and evidence that the affected accounts and reports reconcile. Recurring exceptions should be analyzed for underlying process failures.
Study instruction:
- "Build an FX exception register and define the evidence required to progress an issue from identification to approved closure."
Key point:
- Every exception requires an owner, deadline, status, and documented resolution.
- Closure must be supported by evidence and any required independent retesting.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Control Processes
36. Q: Which performance indicators can management use to monitor the foreign exchange control process?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include the percentage of eligible open items revalued, number of missing or stale rates, number and value of manual overrides, number of duplicate-run alerts, failed conversion count, unresolved reconciliation difference, age of open reconciliation items, frequency of source corrections, and number of journals posted without complete evidence. Each indicator needs a defined formula, data source, reporting period, denominator where applicable, owner, threshold, and escalation rule. For example, revaluation coverage should compare the number or value of eligible items processed against the complete eligible population; counting only successful items without a reliable denominator can overstate performance. Management should examine trends, investigate recurring causes, and assign corrective actions. Dashboard values must be validated against ledger records and source evidence before being used to certify that a period's controls operated successfully.
Study instruction:
- "Design a monitoring dashboard with controlled KPI definitions and independently reconcile its output to the underlying FX registers and close evidence."
Key point:
- KPIs require reliable definitions, complete populations, and traceable source data.
- A dashboard supports management monitoring but does not replace detailed accounting review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Financial Controls
37. Q: What audit evidence should be retained for an FX measurement and settlement cycle?
A: Show answer
The evidence package should connect the source transaction to initial recognition, approved rate observation, conversion calculation, subsequent revaluations, settlements, journals, reconciliations, approvals, exceptions, and the final reporting output. It should include immutable identifiers, relevant document versions, source timestamps, calculation parameters, the applicable accounting-policy version, reviewer and approver records, and posting references. Rate files, workbooks, scripts, and reports should be retained under the organization's approved retention and access policy. A final output should be stored separately from a working file so that later edits cannot silently change the record of what was reviewed or reported. The audit trail must also distinguish factual evidence from assumptions or fictional examples. Backup and recovery controls should be periodically tested to ensure evidence is accessible when required for audits, donor reviews, or management investigations.
Study instruction:
- "Select one FX balance and assemble the complete audit trail from source evidence through calculation, approval, journal posting, reconciliation, and final reporting."
Key point:
- Every material FX balance needs a traceable path from source to reported amount.
- Retention, version control, access, and recovery must support future verification.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Documentation References
38. Q: How should FX calculation logic and its implementation be tested before adoption?
A: Show answer
Testing should cover normal transactions and negative cases that must fail safely. Examples include a valid transaction-date conversion, a valid rate observation, a mismatched currency pair, inverse quotation used incorrectly, missing date or time-zone evidence, zero or negative rate, stale observation, duplicate rate, unsupported average rate, unapproved fallback, historical-cost non-monetary item incorrectly included in revaluation, incomplete population, duplicate posting, unbalanced journal, and settlement based on the wrong carrying amount. Tests should verify both the calculation result and whether the workflow appropriately accepts, rejects, or holds the item. Expected results must be determined independently from the same production calculation logic. Separate implementation testing from assessment of the accounting framework and source applicability. Successful local calculation tests do not prove independent document-schema validity, successful operation in Microsoft Word or the finance system, complete visual inspection, or formal organizational adoption.
Study instruction:
- "Review the test inventory and execute independent positive and negative fixtures, recording expected outcomes, actual results, evidence, and unresolved acceptance gates."
Key point:
- Testing must cover calculations, source validity, access, posting, reconciliation, and exception handling.
- A successful test suite does not automatically establish accounting-policy approval or organizational adoption.

- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
39. Q: What should Finance do if a closing rate is missing immediately before the reporting deadline?
A: Show answer
Finance should identify the affected open items and quantify the scope of the potential reporting impact. The rate owner should investigate the approved source, capture evidence of the missing observation, and check whether the policy authorizes a fallback for the exact currency pair, date, and purpose. The preparer must not insert the prior month's rate solely to meet the deadline. The accounting owner should evaluate materiality, timing, and reporting consequences, then escalate the matter to the authorized decision-maker. If the required evidence or authority is unavailable, keep the affected process on hold and communicate the risk to management. Once an authorized rate basis is established, recalculate affected balances, retest journals, reconcile the ledger, and preserve the decision and all supporting evidence. Any deadline extension or alternative reporting procedure must be verified under the applicable rules.
Study instruction:
- "Work through a missing-closing-rate scenario and document the escalation, approved fallback assessment, period-close impact, and evidence needed to resume."
Key point:
- Deadline pressure does not authorize an unsupported rate substitution.
- The exception must be documented, escalated, and resolved through an approved process.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 11 — Exchange-Rate Source Processes
40. Q: What should Finance do when a donor requests an exchange rate different from the ledger rate?
A: Show answer
Finance should first determine the rate basis specified in the donor agreement or reporting instruction, including the applicable date, methodology, currency, and reporting purpose. The ledger measurement should continue to follow the applicable accounting framework and approved accounting policy. If the donor requires a different reporting conversion, create a separate bridge showing the ledger amount, donor rate, conversion calculation, donor-reporting amount, and the reason for the difference. Review the donor clause and document whether the requested treatment applies to budgeting, expenditure reporting, settlement, or another purpose. Do not rewrite the original accounting transaction merely to make the donor report agree. The bridge must reconcile to the source ledger and retain evidence of the contractual or reporting requirement and the reviewer's approval. Any uncertainty about eligible exchange-rate treatment should be referred to the responsible grant and accounting owners.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a donor-rate reconciliation using an illustrative ledger transaction and document the agreement clause and conversion method governing the report."
Key point:
- Donor reporting conversion does not automatically replace accounting measurement.
- Separate reporting calculations must reconcile to the ledger and be supported by applicable terms.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Reporting and Foreign Currency Translation
41. Q: How should the FX procedure be reviewed and transitioned from a controlled draft to live organizational use?
A: Show answer
Before adoption, the organization should confirm its legal entity, applicable financial reporting framework, functional and presentation currencies, approved rate sources, account mappings, settlement rules, tax and donor interfaces, system configuration, and record-retention requirements. The policy owner must review the proposed accounting treatment against authoritative standards and organizational requirements. Technical owners should then test the actual calculation logic, integrations, permissions, journal posting, reconciliation, and report generation using approved test data. Independent reviewers must examine the results and verify that all critical acceptance requirements have been met. The document should be assigned a version, effective date, owner, reviewer, approver, training plan, and controlled distribution list only after the required approvals are recorded. Any unresolved applicability, evidence, schema-validation, application-operation, or implementation issue must be disclosed rather than marked as passed. Operational adoption requires actual authorized approval; the fictional references in the supplied example do not satisfy that requirement.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an adoption checklist that distinguishes policy approval, source validation, testing, technical release, training, and formal authorization."
Key point:
- Draft completion, technical testing, and organizational adoption are separate acceptance gates.
- No live accounting treatment should be activated while required authority or evidence remains unresolved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Accounting Policy and Financial Reporting
42. Q: How can recurring FX errors be investigated and prevented from happening again?
A: Show answer
When an error is detected, identify the affected transaction population, original source, rate observation, calculation version, journal, account mapping, and reports. Determine whether the cause was an incorrect rate, wrong currency pair, missing date, misclassification, duplicate processing, incomplete settlement, invalid fallback, inaccurate mapping, unauthorized override, or weak review. Quantify the impact on financial statements, donor reports, tax calculations, and open balances where relevant. Prepare a correction proposal that follows the applicable accounting policy, preserve the original record, obtain required approval, post the authorized adjustment, and independently re-reconcile affected balances. The incident register should record root cause, impact, action owner, due date, evidence, reviewer, and closure status. Preventive actions may include improving source validation, controlled inputs, automated duplicate detection, change approval, training, and independent testing. Closure requires evidence that the correction worked and that the recurring risk has been addressed.
Study instruction:
- "Investigate one historical or synthetic FX error from discovery through impact analysis, correction, independent reconciliation, root-cause documentation, and preventive action."
Key point:
- Correcting the journal is not enough if the underlying control failure remains.
- Recurrence prevention requires root-cause analysis, assigned actions, and evidence-based closure.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger, Reconciliation, and Financial Control Processes
