Table 3
Property, Plant and Equipment and Capital Expenditure Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an integrated property, plant and equipment (PPE) and capital expenditure standard operating procedure (SOP)?
A: Show answer
An integrated PPE and capital expenditure SOP establishes the controls governing an asset from the initial business request through budgeting, authorization, procurement handoffs, receipt, accounting assessment, registration, depreciation, physical verification, transfer, impairment, disposal, and financial reporting. It defines responsibilities, required evidence, approval gates, exception handling, and the relationship between accounting and operational asset management. The supplied SOP 036 example uses a fictional organization and explicitly identifies itself as a demonstration rather than an adopted policy. Its fictional values, source records, approvals, useful lives, thresholds, and test outcomes must not be treated as evidence of actual policy or transactions. An organization should adapt the framework only after confirming its legal obligations, financial reporting framework, internal policies, donor agreements, delegation of authority, and operating environment. The SOP should complement, rather than replace, separate procurement, accounts payable, payment, tax, security, insurance, and general-ledger procedures.

Study instruction:
- Map the complete lifecycle of one asset and identify the SOP or control owner responsible for every transition.

Key point:
- The SOP governs the asset lifecycle but does not independently authorize spending, payment, journal posting, or disposal.
- The supplied example is fictional and must not be represented as an approved organizational policy.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
2. Q: What is the difference between capital expenditure and property, plant and equipment recognition?
A: Show answer
Capital expenditure (CapEx) generally describes spending intended to acquire, construct, improve, or replace long-lived assets. PPE recognition is an accounting decision that depends on the applicable reporting framework and the facts of the transaction. A project can receive CapEx budget approval without every associated cost qualifying for capitalization. For example, the purchase of equipment may qualify as PPE, while routine servicing, staff training, general administration, or abnormal waste may require expense recognition. Conversely, an asset may qualify for recognition even when the purchase was funded by a donor or paid directly by a third party. The SOP must maintain separate decisions for business need, budget authorization, procurement, asset recognition, payment, and journal approval. Each decision should have its own evidence and approval authority. Budget labels, invoice descriptions, purchase orders, and project codes are useful inputs, but they do not independently determine accounting classification.

Study instruction:
- Compare a proposed equipment purchase, a routine repair, and a qualifying improvement, documenting the separate budget and accounting decisions.

Key point:
- An approved CapEx budget does not automatically establish PPE recognition.
- Accounting classification must follow the applicable framework and documented facts.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
3. Q: Which accounting framework and authoritative requirements should govern PPE accounting in an NGO?
A: Show answer
The organization must first establish which financial reporting framework is legally and operationally applicable. Depending on the entity, this may involve the applicable Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards, an adopted IFRS-based framework, public-sector standards, or another permitted framework. Mandatory legislation takes precedence within its scope, while approved accounting policies implement the applicable requirements. Donor agreements may impose additional contractual requirements for eligibility, ownership, safeguarding, reporting, or disposal, but those terms do not automatically override accounting recognition and measurement principles. Tax depreciation and insurance valuations likewise serve different purposes from financial accounting. The supplied SOP appropriately places source applicability and framework verification before operational release. Its reference to illustrative IFRS-like principles is not evidence that IFRS or any other specific framework applies to an actual NGO. Record the selected framework, relevant standard and paragraph, edition, effective date, entity scope, and approval before configuring actual accounting rules.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a source applicability register showing the applicable framework, controlling authority, supporting documents, effective date, and reviewer approval.

Key point:
- Verify the reporting framework before adopting accounting parameters or conclusions.
- Donor rules, tax rules, insurance values, and financial accounting must remain clearly distinguished.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- Indonesian Institute of Accountants — Official Website
4. Q: How should an NGO determine whether an expenditure qualifies for PPE recognition?
A: Show answer
Apply the recognition requirements of the governing accounting framework to the item's substance. Under the IFRS model, an item of PPE is generally a tangible asset held for use in operations, rental, production, service delivery, or administration and expected to be used over more than one reporting period. Recognition requires probable future economic benefits and a reliably measurable cost. Public-sector frameworks may express the benefits criterion in terms that also address service potential. The supplied UN Finance Manual describes control, service potential or benefits extending beyond one year, reliable measurement, and the relevant capitalization threshold among its PPE recognition considerations. These criteria must be applied together with the actual framework and organizational policy. The reviewer should examine what the NGO controls, how the item will be used, its expected service period, the unit of account, and the available cost evidence. Items that fail capitalization criteria may still require expense recognition, inventory treatment, or inclusion in an operational stewardship register.

Study instruction:
- Assess a sample of equipment purchases against control, intended use, expected service period, measurement reliability, and the applicable capitalization policy.

Key point:
- Recognition depends on the applicable accounting criteria, not merely purchase intent.
- Items that are not capitalized may still require operational tracking.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
5. Q: How should an NGO establish a capitalization threshold and unit-of-account policy?
A: Show answer
The capitalization threshold determines the monetary level at which qualifying items may be recognized as PPE under the approved policy. The unit-of-account rule determines what constitutes the item being assessed, such as an individual asset, an integrated system, a significant component, or a group of items that must be evaluated together. These decisions must comply with the applicable framework and be approved for the organization. A fictional threshold in an example workbook must never be adopted without verification. The policy should specify the applicable amount, currency, asset categories, effective date, aggregation rules, relevant exceptions, and approval authority. It should also explain the treatment of integrated systems and purchases made through multiple invoices. Artificially splitting one economically integrated purchase into smaller transactions to evade the threshold should be prohibited. Low-value items may remain subject to stewardship and inventory controls even when expensed rather than capitalized.

Study instruction:
- Test the policy against an individual low-value item, a group purchase, and several invoices that together form one integrated system.

Key point:
- Threshold and unit-of-account rules must be approved, effective-dated, and consistently applied.
- Transaction splitting must not be used to manipulate capitalization decisions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
6. Q: What information should be included in a capital expenditure request before an NGO makes a commitment?
A: Show answer
The request should document the business or program need, intended users, proposed asset class, quantity, unit of account, estimated cost, alternatives considered, budget availability, funding source, donor restrictions, expected service period, maintenance implications, operational risks, proposed location, and intended custodian. Where relevant, include technical specifications, lifecycle costs, installation needs, and the justification for replacement rather than repair. Finance should assess the budget and accounting implications, while the budget owner and authorized approver decide within their delegated authority. Donor or grant personnel should verify funding eligibility and any required prior consent. The request must be approved before a commitment is made, subject to the organization's authorized emergency procedures. Approval evidence should identify the approver, date, amount or scope, currency, funding source, applicable delegation, and conditions. A completed request supports decision-making; it does not replace procurement, contract, receipt, payment, or accounting approvals.

Study instruction:
- Review a sample CapEx request and identify missing evidence, unverified funding conditions, and approvals needed before commitment.

Key point:
- Business justification, budget authorization, and procurement are distinct control stages.
- No commitment should rely on an unsupported budget assumption or expired delegation.
7. Q: Why must CapEx approval be separated from procurement, receipt, payment, and journal approval?
A: Show answer
Each approval answers a different question. CapEx approval confirms that the proposed investment is justified and authorized within the budget. Procurement approval confirms that the supplier selection and contracting process comply with purchasing rules. Receipt and technical acceptance establish what was delivered and whether it meets agreed requirements. Payment approval verifies the payable, supporting documents, and payment authority. Accounting review determines the appropriate classification, measurement, and period, while journal approval controls posting to the ledger. Disposal approval addresses the separate decision to relinquish an asset. Combining these decisions into a single undifferentiated approval creates a risk that an authorized purchase will be treated as fully approved at every subsequent stage. The SOP should identify the documents, responsible roles, and system statuses for each gate. Missing evidence at one stage should stop the dependent action without falsely closing other stages as complete.

Study instruction:
- Draw a workflow distinguishing budget authorization, purchasing, receipt, accounting review, payment, posting, and disposal, then test whether any approval improperly substitutes for another.

Key point:
- One approval does not authorize every subsequent asset transaction.
- Each gate requires evidence appropriate to its own purpose.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
8. Q: How should responsibilities, delegations, and segregation of duties be designed for PPE transactions?
A: Show answer
The SOP should identify separate responsibilities for requesting expenditure, confirming budget, purchasing, receiving, assessing accounting treatment, maintaining asset records, reviewing journals, approving transactions, handling proceeds, and testing controls. The person who requests an asset should not be the sole approver of the purchase or the sole verifier of its existence. Custodians should not unilaterally approve their own physical-verification results. Users who implement approved master-data changes should not be the only people testing or authorizing those changes. Where staffing limitations prevent full segregation, management should document compensating controls, such as independent post-transaction review, dual approval, physical spot checks, or reconciliations by an uninvolved reviewer. Delegations must be valid for the relevant entity, transaction type, monetary limit, date, and currency. Internal Audit should retain independence from the design and operation of controls it subsequently audits.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a responsibility matrix for an asset purchase and identify conflicts between requester, custodian, approver, payment executor, and independent reviewer.

Key point:
- Authorization and review must be appropriate to the transaction and independent where necessary.
- Compensating controls must be documented and evidenced when full segregation is impracticable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
9. Q: How should the cost of newly acquired PPE be measured initially?
A: Show answer
Under the applicable accounting framework, initial measurement commonly starts with purchase price after trade discounts and rebates, together with eligible directly attributable costs necessary to bring the asset to the location and condition required for intended operation. Non-refundable taxes may form part of cost, whereas recoverable taxes generally require separate treatment under the applicable rules. Some frameworks also require an initial estimate of dismantling, removal, or site-restoration obligations when the recognition criteria are met. Costs must be supported by invoices, contracts, delivery records, installation evidence, and allocation schedules. The SOP should distinguish eligible costs from routine repairs, general administration, training, abnormal waste, and costs incurred after the asset is already ready for its intended use, subject to the applicable framework. Complex allocations should identify the method, source data, reviewer, and rationale. The accounting conclusion must not be determined by whether a cost was included in the original budget.

Study instruction:
- Reconstruct an asset's cost from the supplier invoice and supporting installation documents, separating eligible and non-qualifying cost components.

Key point:
- Capitalized cost must be supported by the applicable recognition and measurement rules.
- Unsupported or non-qualifying amounts must not be capitalized merely to increase the asset balance.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
10. Q: How should an NGO distinguish routine repairs from capital improvements and component replacements?
A: Show answer
Routine maintenance and repairs generally maintain the asset's existing condition rather than create a separately recognizable improvement. They are normally expensed as incurred under the applicable framework. Expenditure may qualify for capitalization when it satisfies the relevant recognition requirements, such as providing additional service potential, replacing a significant component, or extending the useful life in circumstances permitted by the framework. The assessment should describe the work performed, the original asset's condition, the expected benefit or service potential, the component affected, and the reason the expenditure qualifies. If a replacement component is capitalized, the carrying amount of the replaced component may need to be derecognized under the governing standard. The reviewer should not rely solely on descriptions such as "upgrade," "renovation," or "project improvement" in an invoice. Mixed invoices should be split into supported cost categories using a documented method.

Study instruction:
- Compare an annual maintenance contract, a minor repair, and a significant equipment replacement, then document the accounting conclusion for each.

Key point:
- The nature and accounting criteria of the work determine whether expenditure is capitalized.
- Component replacement requires consideration of the replaced component's remaining carrying amount.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
11. Q: How should donated, donor-funded, or third-party-paid equipment be assessed for recognition and control?
A: Show answer
The organization should determine whether it controls the equipment, what rights and obligations apply, and which recognition and measurement rules govern the transaction. Direct payment by a donor does not, by itself, establish that the equipment is outside the organization's asset records. Equally, physical custody or use does not prove legal ownership or control. The finance and grants teams should examine donation agreements, purchase contracts, delivery evidence, handover records, title documents, donor restrictions, and any right to return or transfer the asset. Initial measurement must follow the applicable reporting framework, including its rules for non-exchange acquisitions or assets received for nil or nominal consideration. The asset should also be flagged in the donor or third-party register when needed to monitor restrictions. Where title, control, measurement, or consent remains uncertain, record the issue and hold the affected accounting or operational decision pending appropriate review.

Study instruction:
- Trace a donor-paid equipment item from the donor agreement through delivery, control assessment, asset-register entry, and restriction monitoring.

Key point:
- The cash payment route does not independently determine recognition.
- Donor consent, ownership, control, and accounting measurement require separate evidence-based assessments.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
12. Q: What checks are required when equipment is delivered and technically accepted?
A: Show answer
Receiving personnel should compare the delivery with the approved purchase order or contract, inspect the item, record quantity and identifying details, document damage or missing components, and obtain evidence of acceptance where required. Finance should match the receiving evidence to the invoice, contractual cost, asset description, funding approval, and accounting assessment. Donor-related restrictions and ownership terms should be checked before concluding that the organization has unrestricted rights over the asset. Disputed or incomplete deliveries should remain in an exception status and should not be treated as fully accepted solely because an invoice arrived. The receiving process must preserve the identities of the people who inspected and accepted the goods and the dates on which these activities occurred. Where installation, calibration, or commissioning remains necessary, the asset may need to remain outside the available-for-use population until the applicable recognition and depreciation triggers are substantiated.

Study instruction:
- Select a purchase and match the approved order, delivery note, technical acceptance, invoice, asset identification, and exception records.

Key point:
- Delivery, invoice receipt, technical acceptance, and availability for use are distinct events.
- Disputed items require a documented resolution before dependent accounting decisions are finalized.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
13. Q: How should construction in progress be recorded and transferred to completed PPE?
A: Show answer
Construction in progress (CIP) represents qualifying costs relating to assets that are not yet ready for their intended use. The organization should maintain a project-level record of approved scope, budget, contracts, invoices, progress evidence, accumulated cost, outstanding commitments, and unresolved technical or contractual issues. Only costs that meet the applicable capitalization criteria should be included. When completion and available-for-use conditions are met, the organization should assemble commissioning evidence, reconcile project costs, confirm the intended asset components, and transfer qualifying balances from CIP to the appropriate PPE classes. The transfer must be supported by an authorized journal and a traceable asset-register entry. Avoid double capitalization by ensuring costs are transferred once and that the corresponding CIP balance is cleared or explained. Any residual balance must be investigated rather than written off or transferred automatically without support.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile one CIP project from approved commitments through accumulated costs, commissioning, asset recognition, and the closing CIP balance.

Key point:
- CIP is not a substitute for final asset classification or commissioning evidence.
- Completed asset transfers must be supported, approved, and reconciled to prevent duplicate balances.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
14. Q: When should depreciation begin and how should the organization determine the available-for-use date?
A: Show answer
Under IAS 16, depreciation begins when an asset is available for use, meaning it is in the location and condition necessary to operate as intended by management. Other applicable frameworks may contain requirements that must be checked before implementation. The trigger is not automatically the invoice date, payment date, purchase-order date, or the date someone first uses the item. The organization should retain commissioning reports, installation completion records, technical acceptance, operational readiness evidence, and any required approvals. The SOP must specify how the date is assessed and who independently reviews it. If an asset is ready for use but idle, the applicable standard may still require depreciation. Land and assets under construction have distinct considerations, so they should not be assigned routine depreciation parameters without checking the governing framework. Missing or conflicting evidence of availability for use should be escalated before the depreciation run is finalized.

Study instruction:
- Compare invoice, delivery, installation, commissioning, and actual-use dates for one asset; document which date satisfies the applicable policy.

Key point:
- Depreciation timing must follow the governing recognition and depreciation requirements.
- An invoice or payment date alone does not prove that an asset was available for use.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
15. Q: How should an NGO establish and review useful lives, residual values, depreciation methods, and components?
A: Show answer
The organization should use estimates and methods that reflect the applicable accounting framework, intended use, expected service potential, consumption pattern, physical condition, maintenance plan, technological obsolescence, and relevant legal or contractual constraints. Significant components of an asset may need separate depreciation when their useful lives or consumption patterns differ materially. The SOP should identify the approved parameter owner, asset classes, depreciation convention, residual-value treatment, component rules, and effective dates. Changes in useful life, residual value, or depreciation method should be supported by current evidence and handled according to the governing standard's rules for accounting estimates or policies. Historical parameter values must remain traceable. A spreadsheet default or ERP configuration is not authority to adopt an estimate. Independent review should confirm both the original parameters and any later changes before they are used in the depreciation calculation.

Study instruction:
- Review depreciation parameters for a vehicle, IT equipment, and a building component; explain the evidence supporting each estimate and identify any required reassessment.

Key point:
- Depreciation parameters require an accounting basis, documented estimates, and appropriate approval.
- Significant components and changes in estimates must be handled under the applicable framework.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
16. Q: What should be included in the fixed asset register?
A: Show answer
The register should contain an immutable asset identifier and, where appropriate, a unique physical tag or serial number. Other fields should ordinarily include asset class, description, manufacturer or model where relevant, acquisition date, cost, funding source, location, custodian, title or control evidence, donor restrictions, available-for-use date, useful life, residual value, depreciation method, accumulated depreciation, impairment, carrying amount, physical-verification status, condition, and supporting-document references. The register should link to approved additions, transfers, component changes, disposals, and other material events. Required fields should be controlled so incomplete records cannot be certified as final. Sensitive personal information should be limited to what is necessary, and permissions should reflect job responsibilities. A tag confirms identification only; it does not prove ownership, existence, correct valuation, or accounting treatment. The register should preserve historical information instead of overwriting material changes without an audit trail.

Study instruction:
- Compare the register fields with the accounting, custody, donor, depreciation, and reporting requirements, then list missing fields and propose validation rules.

Key point:
- The asset register links accounting balances to identified physical or controlled assets.
- A tag or register entry is not a substitute for evidence of existence, title, valuation, or approval.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
17. Q: How should asset identifiers, tagging, and changes to asset records be controlled?
A: Show answer
Each asset should receive a stable identifier that is not reused after retirement or disposal. Physical tags or serial numbers should be recorded where applicable, and duplicates should be flagged for investigation. Changes to location, custodian, class, status, restriction, depreciation parameters, or other material fields must be authorized and retained as dated changes with a named user, reason, evidence, and approval. Historical acquisition cost and previously approved values must not be overwritten merely to make the current register look consistent. The organization should distinguish a corrected master-data field from an accounting adjustment, because changing a description or location does not by itself justify changing the ledger. System controls should reject duplicate identifiers and preserve the previous value when changes are made. Reconciliation should identify records that lost their historical references or that have been edited outside the authorized workflow.

Study instruction:
- Test whether a duplicate asset tag is rejected and whether a custodian change preserves the previous location, custodian, date, and approval.

Key point:
- Asset identity must remain stable throughout the lifecycle.
- Material changes require an authorized and retrievable history.
18. Q: How should an NGO conduct physical verification of PPE and investigate missing assets?
A: Show answer
Physical verification should use an approved population and a documented cutoff date. Independent counters should inspect or otherwise verify assets using an appropriate method, recording the identifier, observed location, custodian, condition, and evidence of sighting. Depending on risk, verification may involve complete counts, risk-based selection, independent inspection, or other approved procedures. Results should be reconciled with the register and investigated when an asset cannot be located or when its location, custodian, identity, or condition differs from the recorded data. A custodian's statement or spreadsheet confirmation alone may be insufficient to conclude that a missing asset has been verified. Suspected theft, damage, or loss should trigger the relevant incident, security, insurance, legal, and management procedures. Any write-off or impairment decision requires the applicable accounting assessment and authorization; an unresolved count difference must not simply be removed from the register to make the reconciliation balance.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile a sample of physical assets to the register and investigate one simulated missing item using independent evidence and a documented escalation path.

Key point:
- Physical verification requires evidence and appropriate independence.
- Missing or mismatched assets remain open until investigation and authorized resolution are documented.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
19. Q: How should asset transfers, temporary loans, off-site use, and custodian changes be managed?
A: Show answer
Every movement should be assessed against ownership, donor restrictions, operational purpose, security risk, insurance arrangements, and any required authorization. The transfer record should identify the asset, original and new location, former and new custodian, effective date, return date if applicable, purpose, approval, and acknowledgement of handover. Loans and off-site use may require additional contractual or insurance checks. Donor-funded equipment may be subject to restrictions on transfer or changes in use, so the relevant agreement must be checked before permission is granted. An approved operational transfer usually changes custody or location information but does not automatically change the asset's cost or carrying amount. Accounting changes should follow a separate assessment of the facts and governing policy. The register should retain an effective-dated history so management can reconstruct where the asset was and who was responsible for it at any point.

Study instruction:
- Trace a temporary field-office equipment loan and confirm consent, authorization, custody handover, expected return, actual return, and history in the register.

Key point:
- Asset movements need permission, custody evidence, and traceable records.
- A location change does not automatically justify an accounting adjustment.
20. Q: How should impairment indicators, damage, obsolescence, theft, and loss be investigated?
A: Show answer
An NGO should identify indicators that an asset's carrying amount may no longer be recoverable or supportable under the applicable accounting framework. Relevant facts may include physical damage, technological obsolescence, reduced service potential, prolonged non-use, unexpected changes in demand, or an incident involving loss or theft. These events should trigger an assessment rather than an automatic journal. The assessment should distinguish repair costs, impairment, derecognition, insurance recovery, and incident reporting. A valuation or other measurement basis should be supported by reliable evidence and reviewed independently. The authorized approver should consider the accounting treatment, donor restrictions, insurance conditions, and implications for financial reporting. A theft or loss case remains open until the required investigation and accounting decisions are supported. Any impairment reversal must also follow the applicable framework; the SOP must not assume that all previous impairment losses may be reversed.

Study instruction:
- Work through a damaged asset scenario, identifying the evidence for repair, impairment, insurance, incident reporting, and any derecognition decision.

Key point:
- Damage and obsolescence trigger investigation, not an automatic accounting entry.
- Impairment, insurance, and write-off decisions must follow separate requirements and approved evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 36 Impairment of Assets
21. Q: When should PPE be derecognized and how should disposal gains or losses be calculated?
A: Show answer
Derecognition is considered when an asset is disposed of or when the applicable framework's conditions for removing the asset from the financial statements are met. Before disposal, the organization should verify ownership, donor restrictions, title, condition, proposed method, approvals, valuation where needed, and any required external consent. The disposal record should include the asset identifier, reason, authorization, date, proceeds, evidence of transfer or sale, and the status of associated custody and insurance records. Financial accounting should remove the applicable gross cost and accumulated depreciation or impairment and calculate the resulting gain or loss using the framework's requirements. A practical calculation often compares net proceeds with carrying amount, subject to the governing rules. The asset register, general ledger, donor or third-party register, and proceeds record must be reconciled. Disposal gains should not be confused with ordinary revenue where the applicable standard requires different presentation.

Study instruction:
- Recalculate the gain or loss on an illustrative disposal and reconcile the asset register, carrying amount, authorization, proceeds, bank evidence, and journal.

Key point:
- Disposal requires authorized evidence and complete accounting derecognition.
- The gain or loss must be supported by the asset's carrying amount and the applicable proceeds rules.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
22. Q: How should PPE accounting, tax depreciation, donor reporting, and insurance records be reconciled without conflating them?
A: Show answer
These records may use different measurement bases, eligibility requirements, or reporting purposes. Financial depreciation is determined by the applicable financial reporting framework and approved accounting policy. Tax depreciation follows applicable tax legislation and current tax guidance. Donor reporting is governed by funding agreements and eligibility terms, while insurance schedules use policy definitions and valuation requirements. Operational registers may retain low-value equipment for custody even when it is expensed in the financial accounts. The organization should maintain explicit mappings among these records and explain legitimate differences rather than forcing all values to agree. A reconciliation should identify the amount, basis, period, reason for difference, owner, and evidence. Tax depreciation should never be substituted automatically for financial depreciation. Likewise, inclusion in a donor budget does not necessarily mean the cost is eligible for reimbursement or should be capitalized.

Study instruction:
- Compare one asset's financial depreciation, tax depreciation, insurance value, and donor-reported amount, explaining the basis for each difference.

Key point:
- Financial accounting, tax, donor eligibility, and insurance operate under different rules.
- Differences should be documented and reconciled where relevant, not eliminated by forcing a single value.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- Indonesian Ministry of Finance Regulation No. 72 of 2023
- Directorate General of Taxes — Depreciation and Amortization Guidance
23. Q: What journal entries and ledger controls are required across the PPE lifecycle?
A: Show answer
The accounting team should document the expected journal treatment for acquisitions, eligible cost allocations, CIP accumulation, transfers into completed PPE, depreciation, impairment, component replacements, disposal, and any supported corrections. The exact accounts and recognition entries must be based on the applicable accounting policy and transaction facts. For example, recording an invoice in accounts payable is not necessarily the same as recognizing an asset, and recording a CIP transfer must not duplicate the asset's original cost. Journal preparation should identify the asset or project, accounting period, currency, supporting documents, debit and credit amounts, preparer, reviewer, and approval. Posting must follow the organization's authorized general-ledger workflow. A journal entry does not by itself demonstrate physical existence, donor consent, or receipt of goods. After posting, reconcile the register with relevant GL accounts and investigate missing, duplicate, or misclassified entries before certification.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a journal walkthrough for an equipment purchase, depreciation run, CIP transfer, and final disposal, tracing every entry to its supporting records.

Key point:
- Every material PPE entry should be traceable to a supported accounting decision.
- Journal posting and asset-register maintenance must be reconciled and independently reviewed.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
24. Q: How should the fixed asset register, general ledger, and construction-in-progress balances be reconciled?
A: Show answer
Establish a monthly or otherwise approved reconciliation between the detailed asset register and the relevant PPE, accumulated depreciation, impairment, and CIP general-ledger balances. The reconciliation should separately address opening balances, additions, disposals, transfers, depreciation, impairment, foreign exchange where relevant, and approved adjustments. Each difference should be classified, assigned to a responsible owner, supported with evidence, and resolved or formally escalated. Timing differences can be legitimate, but must be explained and tracked until cleared. The reconciliation should also identify assets appearing in the register but absent from the ledger, ledger balances with no corresponding asset support, and CIP projects that should have been transferred or closed. A zero difference is not sufficient if the population is incomplete or the underlying data is unreliable. Independent review should confirm the population, calculations, unresolved items, and evidence before the period-end certification is signed.

Study instruction:
- Construct an asset-register-to-GL reconciliation and investigate sample differences involving a missed addition, duplicate transfer, and unposted depreciation journal.

Key point:
- Reconciliation must test completeness and accuracy, not only arithmetic agreement.
- Unresolved differences require owners, deadlines, and documented follow-up.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
25. Q: How should duplicate invoices, duplicate asset records, and artificially split purchases be detected?
A: Show answer
Duplicate controls should operate at both transaction level and asset-register level. Invoice controls should compare supplier identity, invoice number, date, amount, currency, purchase order, and other available identifiers. Asset controls should compare asset ID, physical tag, serial number, description, location, and acquisition references. Artificial splitting requires an assessment of related invoices and items that form one integrated purchase or project under the unit-of-account policy. Automated matching can identify candidates but should not automatically delete or merge records when there is a possibility of legitimate duplication of identifiers across suppliers or related components. Exceptions should show the matched records, comparison fields, risk, reviewer decision, and supporting evidence. Any correction must preserve the original record and the audit trail. The SOP should also test how duplicate records affect capitalization thresholds, depreciation, and the reconciliation between PPE and the ledger.

Study instruction:
- Create a test population with duplicate invoices, repeated serial numbers, and multiple invoices for one integrated system, then document which items require review.

Key point:
- Multiple matching fields provide stronger duplicate detection than a single reference number.
- Automated detection should identify candidates for review, not remove valid records without evidence.
26. Q: How should accounting-policy changes, master-data changes, and system configuration changes be controlled?
A: Show answer
Changes must have a documented reason, authoritative basis, impact assessment, approval, effective date, testing, implementation evidence, and retained previous version. Policy changes may affect recognition thresholds, asset classes, depreciation methods, useful lives, residual values, component rules, or disposal procedures. Master-data changes affect particular records, while system configuration changes may alter how many transactions are processed. These change types should be distinguished because their approval levels and testing requirements differ. The implementation team must identify affected assets, open projects, prior calculations, future depreciation, reports, and outstanding exceptions. Historical accounting records must not be silently rewritten merely because a new parameter is introduced. When a change is not approved or its effective date is uncertain, the affected decision should remain on hold. After implementation, verify that the applied configuration matches the approved specification and that the results are reproducible.

Study instruction:
- Simulate a change to a useful-life parameter and document source authority, approval, effective date, sample testing, impact assessment, and preserved history.

Key point:
- Material changes require controlled approval and independent testing.
- Previous settings and results must remain traceable after implementation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
27. Q: What source and evidence hierarchy should be applied when documents conflict or an approval cannot be verified?
A: Show answer
Use a controlled hierarchy that distinguishes mandatory law, the applicable financial reporting framework, approved organizational policy, delegation of authority, donor agreements, transaction contracts, system records, and explanatory or training material. The correct hierarchy depends on each source's scope; no single document automatically resolves every question. For example, a donor agreement may govern whether a restricted asset can be disposed of, while the accounting framework governs recognition and derecognition. A system-generated status may provide evidence of processing but does not replace an underlying approval required by policy. The source register should record the issuing authority, document title, exact version or paragraph, date of issue, effective date, verification status, applicable scope, owner, and approval. Conflicting, outdated, incomplete, or inaccessible sources should be escalated to the designated policy owner or legal/accounting adviser. Do not treat a fictional identifier or placeholder as an authoritative source.

Study instruction:
- Examine a conflicting donor condition, internal policy, and accounting requirement, then record the question each source can and cannot resolve.

Key point:
- Source authority and applicability must be verified for each decision.
- Unverified or conflicting requirements should trigger a documented hold rather than an invented conclusion.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
28. Q: How should the NGO handle a capitalization decision when the threshold, reporting framework, or required approval is missing?
A: Show answer
A missing or unapproved parameter should not be replaced with a convenient assumption. The preparer should identify the affected transaction, determine which decision depends on the missing information, preserve available evidence, and assign a policy owner to resolve the issue. The asset may be operationally recorded for safeguarding purposes while its accounting conclusion remains unresolved, provided that the provisional status is explicit and the treatment is otherwise authorized. It must not be falsely marked as fully approved, capitalized, depreciating, or finally reconciled when the mandatory prerequisite is not satisfied. The exception log should record the missing requirement, risk, owner, interim control, escalation date, deadline, decision authority, and closure evidence. If an applicable rule requires a formal accounting conclusion before proceeding, the organization must obtain that decision rather than allowing the software to infer it.

Study instruction:
- Test a transaction with an empty capitalization threshold and identify which workflow actions must be blocked, which provisional records are permitted, and who can authorize closure.

Key point:
- Missing mandatory parameters must remain visible exceptions.
- A provisional operational record is not proof of a final accounting conclusion.
29. Q: How should an NGO document exceptions and corrective actions throughout the PPE lifecycle?
A: Show answer
The exception register should assign a unique reference to each material issue and identify the affected asset or project, issue type, date detected, expected control, actual condition, financial or operational exposure, responsible owner, target date, interim safeguard, escalation level, reviewer, and closure evidence. Examples include missing ownership documents, incomplete commissioning, unapproved depreciation parameters, unidentified physical assets, donor consent gaps, unreconciled balances, and unauthorized changes. Root-cause analysis should distinguish individual error from process design, training, system configuration, incomplete source data, or unclear authority. Closing an exception requires evidence that the issue was addressed and any necessary accounting, register, donor, security, insurance, or reporting updates were completed. Repeated issues should lead to changes in controls or training rather than repeated temporary corrections. Exceptions must not be closed simply because an item has been carried forward to the next period.

Study instruction:
- Review three sample exceptions and test whether each has a clear owner, risk assessment, target date, escalation route, corrective action, and independently supported closure.

Key point:
- Exception management must continue until the root cause and required consequences are addressed.
- Repeated exceptions should inform process improvements and management oversight.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
30. Q: What reconciliation and disclosures should support PPE reporting at month-end and year-end?
A: Show answer
Period-end procedures should reconcile additions, disposals, transfers, accumulated depreciation, impairment, and closing carrying amounts by asset class with the underlying records and general ledger. Finance should investigate movements that appear inconsistent with the approved budget, procurement and receiving records, depreciation schedules, or donor-restricted asset lists. Year-end reporting under the applicable framework may require information about measurement bases, depreciation methods, useful lives or rates, gross carrying amounts, accumulated depreciation and impairment, and a reconciliation of movements during the period. The exact disclosure requirements depend on the framework and should be confirmed against the standard that applies to the NGO. The supplied UN Finance Manual demonstrates PPE presentation and note disclosures for the UN's reporting model, but its particular reporting choices are not automatically mandatory for a separate organization. Keep evidence of the calculation, reviewer, unresolved items, and approval behind the reported figures.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a PPE movement reconciliation from opening cost and accumulated depreciation to closing carrying amount, then map each movement to the proposed financial-statement note.

Key point:
- Period-end reconciliation must connect ledger movements to detailed asset evidence.
- Disclosures must follow the framework applicable to the reporting entity.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment
31. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, or VBA support PPE controls without determining the accounting treatment automatically?
A: Show answer
A controlled workbook can consolidate purchase records, asset registers, depreciation schedules, CIP projects, physical-verification results, and exception logs. Power Query can standardize source data, combine approved input files, normalize identifiers, flag missing fields, and identify potential duplicates. Excel formulas can calculate depreciation and carrying amounts based on approved and validated parameters. VBA can prepare workflow outputs, create reports, export controlled PDFs, and record certain operational events if appropriately designed. However, automation does not establish that the accounting policy is correct, the asset exists, a donor has consented, or an approval is legally valid. Calculations should be tested against independently prepared examples, while access, formula changes, source refreshes, exceptions, and generated outputs should be controlled. The software workflow must also preserve the distinction between draft calculations, reviewed results, approved entries, and actual posted transactions.

Study instruction:
- Design a sample asset register and exception report, then independently verify its calculations and test missing identifiers, duplicate items, and unapproved parameters.

Key point:
- Technology improves consistency and traceability but cannot substitute for accounting judgment and authorization.
- Automated outputs must be independently tested and reconciled to approved source evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
32. Q: How should testing and acceptance criteria be designed before a PPE workflow or software configuration is released?
A: Show answer
Define test cases from the approved policy and risk assessment before implementation. Tests should cover normal transactions and negative cases, such as a missing capitalization threshold, an expired delegation, duplicate asset identifiers, absent commissioning evidence, unsupported cost components, invalid depreciation parameters, missing donor consent, and unexplained reconciliation differences. Each test should specify the preconditions, input data, expected result, actual result, evidence, test owner, reviewer, and status. A failed prerequisite should prevent protected actions from proceeding. Synthetic data can demonstrate that arithmetic or validation logic behaves as designed, but does not establish that production data or actual accounting balances are correct. The SOP 036 example itself distinguishes executed local checks from unverified tests and blocked external validation. A release decision must therefore reflect what was actually tested, what remains outstanding, and who has the authority to approve implementation.

Study instruction:
- Create a test matrix with both positive and negative cases, run the tests against a controlled sample, and classify every result as passed, failed, blocked, or not run.

Key point:
- A test intention is not the same as an executed test result.
- Release must not be certified while mandatory acceptance gates remain unverified or blocked.
33. Q: How should migration of an existing asset register into a new system be controlled?
A: Show answer
Before migration, establish the source population, data dictionary, asset identity rules, opening balances, depreciation history, current custody information, restriction fields, supporting documents, and the scope of the destination system. Reconcile record counts and material totals before transformation. Define how missing identifiers, duplicate tags, invalid dates, unsupported values, and inconsistent classifications will be treated. Retain the original source file or a controlled snapshot, migration mapping, transformation rules, exception report, approval, and evidence of post-migration verification. The organization should compare cost, accumulated depreciation, impairment, net carrying amount, and relevant register attributes before and after migration. Differences must be explained and authorized rather than overwritten or hidden by recalculation. The acceptance decision should remain open until critical mismatches are resolved and the responsible reviewer confirms the migrated population is sufficiently complete and accurate.

Study instruction:
- Simulate a migration with missing asset IDs and a cost mismatch, then document the reconciliation and approval required before release.

Key point:
- Migration must preserve identity, history, and accounting integrity.
- Unexplained changes to asset values or populations block reliable acceptance.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
34. Q: How should access rights, records, retention, and backups be managed for an asset-control system?
A: Show answer
Access should be based on business need and the principle of least privilege. The organization should define who can request assets, amend master data, approve expenditure, change depreciation parameters, process journals, perform verification, authorize disposal, and access sensitive records. Access reviews should identify obsolete accounts, conflicting duties, unnecessary administrative privileges, and external access that is no longer required. Retained evidence should include source documents, approvals, asset movements, calculations, reconciliation records, exceptions, and relevant change logs, subject to applicable legal, contractual, donor, and organizational retention requirements. Backups should be protected against unauthorized alteration, and restoration procedures should be tested periodically. The system should not store passwords, authentication tokens, or unnecessary personal information in ordinary shared registers. A backup that has never been tested does not prove that data can be recovered.

Study instruction:
- Review a sample user-access matrix and test whether asset transactions, approvals, logs, retained evidence, and recovery procedures meet the organization's control requirements.

Key point:
- Access and records must support accountability without exposing unnecessary sensitive information.
- Backup and recovery arrangements require evidence of actual testing.
35. Q: Which indicators should management monitor to assess whether PPE and CapEx controls are operating effectively?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include the percentage of additions with complete supporting evidence, the number of assets missing identifiers, the value and age of unreconciled balances, the number of open physical-verification exceptions, the proportion of CIP projects overdue for review, unapproved parameter changes, late depreciation journals, disposal records awaiting closure, and the proportion of required approvals completed before commitment. Each indicator requires a defined population, calculation method, owner, reporting frequency, target or tolerance, and escalation rule. Management should distinguish the number of issues from their financial or operational severity. For example, one missing high-value asset may present greater risk than many minor data-quality issues. Performance measures should be reconciled to underlying registers and records rather than relying solely on manually updated dashboard statuses. Trends and recurring exceptions should lead to specific corrective actions and subsequent testing.

Study instruction:
- Define five PPE control indicators and verify each result against the source register, reconciliation, or approval records for a sample period.

Key point:
- KPIs must have clear definitions, reliable source data, and accountable owners.
- Management reporting should trigger investigation and corrective action rather than merely report activity.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
36. Q: What evidence is required before an NGO adopts a draft PPE and CapEx SOP as an official procedure?
A: Show answer
Adoption requires verification that the procedure reflects the organization's legal form, applicable accounting framework, current source requirements, donor obligations, internal policies, delegation of authority, and actual operating processes. The policy owner should validate each parameter, including capitalization thresholds, asset categories, useful lives, depreciation methods, effective dates, approval limits, and retention requirements. Relevant process owners should confirm that the proposed controls can be performed and evidenced by available personnel and systems. The organization should test templates, workflows, calculations, exception handling, reconciliation, permissions, and reporting. Approval must come from the authorized governance or delegated authority, with the approved version, effective date, training plan, implementation responsibilities, and periodic review date recorded. A draft cannot be treated as adopted merely because it is complete, formatted, or successfully generated by software.

Study instruction:
- Build an adoption checklist comparing the draft SOP with verified source requirements, real operating evidence, tested controls, designated owners, and formal approval records.

Key point:
- Draft completion and formal policy adoption are separate events.
- Actual applicability, parameters, approval, implementation, and control testing must be evidenced before release.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- Indonesian Institute of Accountants — Official Website
37. Q: How should an NGO assess whether a fictional worked example or synthetic test result can be relied upon as operational evidence?
A: Show answer
Separate fictional demonstration data from actual organizational evidence. A synthetic asset, donor agreement, approval ID, balance, depreciation amount, or test result can illustrate the intended control logic, but it cannot prove that an actual asset exists, that a legal entity owns it, that donor consent has been obtained, or that a production system is correctly configured. The supplied SOP 036 explicitly warns that its entity, asset data, donor restrictions, useful lives, thresholds, balances, calculations, approvals, and implementation history are invented. Its calculated examples may be useful for testing arithmetic, validation, and workflow design, but those checks must not be represented as confirmation of actual accounting records or formal acceptance. The organization should replace fictional placeholders with verified documents, approved parameters, actual transaction samples, and appropriately authorized decisions before operational adoption.

Study instruction:
- Identify fictional values, approval references, and test outcomes in the supplied example, then specify what real evidence would be required to replace each one.

Key point:
- Synthetic tests demonstrate design behavior, not actual compliance or ownership.
- All real policy and transaction conclusions require independently verified evidence.
38. Q: How should staff training and handover support continued compliance with the PPE SOP?
A: Show answer
Training should explain the asset lifecycle, recognition criteria, capitalization policy, required forms, evidence standards, approval responsibilities, segregation of duties, depreciation rules, physical-verification process, disposal requirements, and escalation procedures. It should also clarify which decisions belong to Finance, Procurement, Grants, Receiving, Operations, Treasury, Legal, and authorized management. Training should use realistic cases covering both routine transactions and exceptions, such as a missing commissioning record or donor-restricted asset. A new staff member should be able to locate the approved SOP, identify the current policy parameters, understand how a transaction is reviewed, and find prior approved examples without relying exclusively on another employee's memory. Training attendance alone is not proof of competence; practical exercises, review of completed forms, and follow-up testing can help establish whether users apply the procedures correctly.

Study instruction:
- Design a handover exercise requiring a new team member to process a simulated asset request, explain the accounting decision, reconcile the record, and escalate an unresolved exception.

Key point:
- Training should build demonstrated competence, not merely document attendance.
- Handover materials must point users to the current approved procedures and parameter sources.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
39. Q: What should happen if the approved asset policy conflicts with a donor agreement or applicable legal requirement?
A: Show answer
Do not resolve the conflict by silently applying whichever instruction is easiest. Identify the exact requirement, source, applicable entity, asset, transaction, date, and area of conflict. Determine whether the issue concerns accounting recognition, funding eligibility, ownership, permitted use, transfer, disposal, tax compliance, or delegated authority, because each may be governed by different requirements. Refer the matter to the responsible accounting policy owner, grants or legal team, and authorized management as appropriate. Preserve the evidence and record any temporary controls needed to prevent an unauthorized commitment, movement, or disposal. The organization must determine the accounting and operational consequences separately, document the decision, and update the relevant procedures if necessary. A donor condition does not automatically replace the financial reporting framework, and internal policy cannot override mandatory applicable law.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a proposed disposal of donor-restricted equipment and identify the approvals, legal interpretation, accounting treatment, and evidence needed before the transaction proceeds.

Key point:
- Conflicting requirements require documented interpretation and authorized resolution.
- Accounting treatment and donor or legal compliance must each be addressed within their appropriate scope.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
40. Q: What are the most important acceptance gates before a PPE transaction or asset-control process is certified as complete?
A: Show answer
A complete workflow should confirm that the underlying source and policy are applicable; business and funding approvals are valid; the procurement, delivery, and acceptance evidence is sufficient; recognition, cost, classification, and depreciation parameters are supported; the asset register and general ledger are updated consistently; donor, tax, insurance, and operational requirements have been addressed where relevant; and required independent reviews have been completed. For transfers or disposals, the corresponding authorization and custody or proceeds evidence must also exist. Material exceptions must be resolved or formally held in accordance with the organization's approved policy. Certification should identify the period, population, preparer, reviewer, version, unresolved items, and evidence of approval. A successful spreadsheet calculation, a saved form, a zero reconciliation difference, or a generated report is not enough on its own. Final status should reflect actual evidence and executed controls rather than an intention to complete them later.

Study instruction:
- Use one completed PPE transaction to test every acceptance gate and record which requirements are passed, blocked, outstanding, or not applicable.

Key point:
- Completion requires evidence across the accounting, operational, authorization, and reconciliation stages.
- A process must not be certified as fully complete while mandatory approval or evidence requirements remain unresolved.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
