Table 4
Intangible Assets and Software Accounting Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an intangible-assets and software standard operating procedure?
A: Show answer
An intangible-assets SOP establishes a controlled process for identifying, evaluating, recognizing, measuring, maintaining, amortizing, assessing for impairment, and derecognizing intangible assets and software-related arrangements. For an international NGO, it should connect Finance, IT, Procurement, Legal, Grants, project managers, and authorized management. The objective is to ensure that accounting decisions are supported by enforceable rights, technical evidence, contractual terms, an applicable accounting framework, and documented approvals. A successful implementation makes each material decision traceable from the original request through the contract, recognition assessment, asset register, general ledger, and financial reporting. The supplied document is explicitly a fictional demonstration, not an adopted organizational policy. Its examples, thresholds, useful lives, approval references, and system configurations must not be treated as actual organizational decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the objective of each lifecycle stage and distinguish the supplied fictional example from an approved organizational SOP."

Key point:
- Accounting decisions must be supported by evidence, applicable authority, and documented approval.
- A demonstration document cannot establish an organization's accounting policy or legal rights.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
2. Q: Which transactions and activities should fall within the scope of the SOP?
A: Show answer
The scope should cover separately acquired software licenses, qualifying internally generated software, identifiable intellectual-property rights, qualifying development outputs, and assessments of SaaS, hosting, configuration, and customization arrangements. It should also cover asset-register maintenance, amortization, impairment, subsequent expenditure, transfers, disposal, expiry, and financial reporting. However, the SOP must not replace procurement authorization, contract review, cybersecurity, software development governance, project management, tax procedures, donor reporting, general-ledger posting, or period-end closing procedures. These activities require defined interfaces. For example, Procurement supplies the executed contract, IT validates implementation and operational readiness, Legal reviews relevant rights, and Finance determines accounting classification under the applicable framework. The final SOP must identify which department owns each handoff and what evidence must be transferred before the next stage begins.

Study instruction:
- "Map each covered activity to its responsible department, adjacent SOP, required evidence, and completion condition."

Key point:
- Accounting assessment and operational authorization are related but separate responsibilities.
- Every process handoff needs an identifiable owner and acceptance criteria.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
3. Q: How should an NGO determine which accounting framework and intangible-asset rules actually apply?
A: Show answer
Establish the reporting entity, jurisdiction, applicable accounting framework, authoritative requirements, and effective dates before applying recognition or measurement rules. Confirm whether the organization reports under IPSAS, an applicable national accounting framework, IFRS-based requirements, or another prescribed basis. Then obtain the relevant current standards, authorized internal accounting policies, capitalization policy, and any requirements applicable to the reporting entity. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides useful IPSAS-based guidance, but certain procedures, thresholds, useful lives, and system instructions are specific to the United Nations and its Umoja environment. They must not automatically be transferred to an independent NGO. Contract terms, donor conditions, and applicable legislation may create additional requirements. Maintain a source-applicability register recording each authority, its relevance, the responsible reviewer, the effective date, and any unresolved interpretation.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an accounting-framework decision record and identify which standards, internal policies, and contractual requirements govern the organization."

Key point:
- The applicable framework must be established rather than inferred from NGO status.
- UN-specific policies and thresholds are not universal NGO requirements.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards — IAI official publications
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
4. Q: What qualifies as an intangible asset, and why is an invoice labelled "software" insufficient evidence?
A: Show answer
Under IAS 38, an intangible asset is an identifiable non-monetary asset without physical substance. The label on an invoice does not establish that the organization controls a resource meeting the asset definition or the recognition requirements. A software invoice might represent a perpetual license, a limited-term right, cloud access, maintenance, implementation services, training, or a mixture of these items. Each component may require different accounting treatment. The reviewer must determine what has actually been acquired, which rights have transferred, what benefits or service potential are controlled, and whether the cost can be measured reliably. For an IPSAS-reporting entity, the applicable IPSAS requirements must be used instead of automatically applying IAS 38. The assessment and conclusion should identify the specific item being evaluated rather than classifying the whole supplier invoice based on its description.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a perpetual software license, a SaaS subscription, and a training invoice to identify the underlying resource or service acquired."

Key point:
- The substance of the acquired rights and services determines the accounting assessment.
- An invoice description alone does not prove intangible-asset recognition.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
5. Q: How should identifiability, control, and future benefits or service potential be assessed?
A: Show answer
The assessment should establish whether the resource is identifiable, whether the entity controls the relevant rights or resource, and whether the applicable recognition criteria are satisfied. Under IAS 38, identifiability can arise because an asset is separable or because it arises from contractual or other legal rights. Control involves the ability to obtain the relevant future economic benefits and restrict others' access to those benefits. For NGOs, the assessment should also consider the applicable public-sector framework and whether the resource provides service potential. Inspect the executed license, ownership or assignment clauses, permitted users, duration, renewal provisions, transfer restrictions, termination rights, and supplier dependencies. Technical documentation should identify the actual deliverable, while Legal confirms the enforceability of relevant rights where necessary. If essential rights or control evidence is missing, record an unresolved assessment and prevent unsupported capitalization.

Study instruction:
- "Apply identifiability and control criteria to a software license and an internally developed application, citing the rights and technical evidence supporting each conclusion."

Key point:
- Recognition requires evidence about the resource and the applicable accounting criteria.
- Missing contractual or control evidence requires resolution before a positive conclusion is approved.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
6. Q: What recognition criteria should be checked before software expenditure is capitalized?
A: Show answer
Apply the recognition requirements of the organization's confirmed accounting framework. Under IAS 38, recognition requires that the item meets the definition of an intangible asset, that the relevant future economic benefits are probable, and that cost can be measured reliably. Other frameworks may express or implement the requirements differently. Accordingly, Finance should document the resource or right, evidence of control, expected benefits or service potential as applicable, reliable cost measurement, and any additional framework-specific conditions. Budget approval, donor approval, a supplier invoice, technical completion, or a management statement cannot independently prove that all recognition criteria are satisfied. The recognition decision should identify the evidence reviewed, outstanding uncertainties, and the authorized approver. If a criterion remains unresolved, the transaction should remain on hold for accounting classification or receive the appropriate treatment required by the applicable framework rather than being capitalized automatically.

Study instruction:
- "Complete a recognition checklist using the applicable standard and explain why each criterion is supported or remains unresolved."

Key point:
- Recognition follows the applicable accounting criteria, not the existence of expenditure alone.
- Every material recognition decision needs documented evidence and review.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
7. Q: How should an externally acquired software license be evaluated and initially measured?
A: Show answer
First establish the rights obtained and whether the arrangement produces a recognizable intangible asset under the confirmed framework. If the criteria are met, determine initial measurement under that framework and identify the directly attributable costs that qualify for inclusion. Potential evidence includes the executed agreement, supplier invoice, license certificate, purchase order, proof of delivery, technical acceptance, implementation records, and approved cost breakdown. Separate the license or asset acquisition from subscriptions, routine support, training, maintenance, and other services where required. Verify the contractual term and any restrictions relevant to useful life and amortization. Finance should prepare the measurement schedule, reconcile its total to procurement and project records, and submit it for independent review. Capitalization must not be justified simply because the organization paid a large amount or obtained donor funding for the purchase.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one acquired software arrangement from contract and invoice through recognition analysis, initial measurement, approval, and the proposed accounting entry."

Key point:
- Initial measurement depends on the applicable framework and the costs eligible for recognition.
- Service components must not be included in an asset without a supported basis.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
8. Q: How should donated or nominal-cost intangible assets be assessed?
A: Show answer
Determine what the organization actually receives, whether rights are transferred, and whether the asset meets the recognition requirements of its accounting framework. Obtain the donation or transfer agreement, evidence of enforceable rights, restrictions, valuation support where required, acceptance records, and documentation of the date control begins. The applicable framework determines the relevant initial-measurement treatment; the organization must not assume that every donated license is measured at zero cost or automatically at fair value without checking the governing requirements. Separately consider donor restrictions, conditions, and reporting obligations. These may affect operational use, disclosures, or other accounting consequences without necessarily changing the assessment of whether the resource meets the asset definition. Record the accounting conclusion independently from the donor-eligibility and legal-rights assessments, and retain the underlying evidence with the asset record.

Study instruction:
- "Review a hypothetical donated software license and identify the documents needed to evaluate recognition, measurement, donor restrictions, and the start date of control."

Key point:
- Donated assets require the applicable framework's recognition and measurement rules.
- A donation label does not prove unrestricted use or enforceable software rights.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
9. Q: How should research expenditure be distinguished from development expenditure for internally generated software?
A: Show answer
Separate the project into identifiable activities and evaluate them using the criteria in the applicable accounting framework. Under IAS 38, research-phase expenditure is expensed, while development expenditure can be capitalized only when the specified development-recognition criteria are demonstrated. Relevant evidence may include feasibility assessments, approved development plans, project-stage approvals, technical validation, documented resource availability, commercial or service-use intentions, and reliable cost records. The project name, management optimism, a committed grant, or an approved budget does not establish that the recognition criteria were met. Where research and development phases cannot be distinguished under IAS 38, the standard's treatment for the expenditure must be considered. Finance must preserve the actual qualifying date so costs from an earlier non-qualifying phase are not capitalized retrospectively without a permitted basis.

Study instruction:
- "Classify a sample software-development project into research, development, and operational stages, and identify the evidence supporting the capitalization start date."

Key point:
- Research and development expenditure may have different accounting treatment.
- The qualifying development date must be supported by contemporaneous evidence.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
10. Q: What should a development phase-gate assessment establish before capitalization begins?
A: Show answer
A phase gate is a documented decision point that assesses whether the project has met the development-recognition requirements and whether it is authorized to proceed. The control should identify the applicable criteria, evidence, evaluation date, project scope, cost records, unresolved technical or legal issues, reviewer, and authorized decision-maker. Depending on the applicable framework, evidence may address technical feasibility, intention and ability to complete, availability of resources, expected benefits or service potential, and reliable measurement of attributable expenditure. The gate must not be passed merely because a project reaches a planned milestone or receives payment. If criteria are demonstrated only later, the accounting assessment must distinguish earlier expenditure from expenditure incurred after qualification. A failed or incomplete gate should create an explicit hold, with a named owner and resolution requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Design a development gate record with criterion-by-criterion evidence, qualifying date, cost cutoff, review, and approval fields."

Key point:
- Development capitalization needs evidence that the applicable criteria have been met.
- The phase-gate approval must identify the date and costs eligible for consideration.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
11. Q: Which development costs may qualify for capitalization, and how should the amount be determined?
A: Show answer
Identify expenditure directly attributable to creating, producing, and preparing the qualifying asset for its intended use, applying the actual requirements of the governing framework. Potentially relevant items include supported development-employee costs, qualifying contractor invoices, and other permitted directly attributable costs. Eligibility must be evaluated by cost type, development stage, and connection to the asset. Finance should reconcile project timesheets, vendor invoices, approved change requests, purchase orders, and allocation calculations to the project ledger. Unsupported estimates, unrelated general overhead, abnormal rework, training, maintenance, and routine operational expenditure must not be included automatically. The treatment of each category depends on the applicable standard and facts. Record the decision for every material cost category, identify excluded items, and ensure the same expenditure is not capitalized in more than one asset or duplicated across hardware, software, and implementation records.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a cost-classification schedule that separates potentially eligible development expenditure from excluded or unresolved items and reconciles both groups to the project ledger."

Key point:
- Only qualifying costs supported by the applicable framework and evidence may enter the asset cost.
- Reconciliation must prevent unsupported costs and duplicate capitalization.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
12. Q: How should training, data migration, hosting, maintenance, and general overhead be evaluated?
A: Show answer
These cost categories must be assessed separately rather than capitalized simply because they relate to a software implementation. Training commonly relates to preparing staff to use a system, while hosting may provide an ongoing service; data migration, customization, maintenance, overhead, and rework depend on their precise nature and the applicable accounting requirements. Obtain detailed supplier invoices, statements of work, time records, deliverable descriptions, and acceptance evidence. Where one invoice covers multiple services, request a reasonable and documented breakdown instead of assigning the entire charge to software. Consider whether the expenditure creates a controlled identifiable asset, relates to an existing recognized asset, provides a service over time, or must be expensed under the applicable rules. Unsupported allocation assumptions should remain unresolved until reviewed and approved.

Study instruction:
- "Break down a mixed implementation invoice into cost categories and document the evidence and framework-based conclusion for each category."

Key point:
- Related expenditure does not automatically become part of software asset cost.
- Mixed invoices require evidence-based component analysis and allocation.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- Configuration or Customisation Costs in a Cloud Computing Arrangement — IFRS Foundation
13. Q: How should capitalization thresholds be established and applied consistently?
A: Show answer
The organization must verify whether its governing framework requires or permits a capitalization threshold and how that policy should operate. Any threshold must be formally approved, supported by relevant requirements and materiality considerations, and applied consistently. The policy should define the unit of account, treatment of related components, handling of coordinated purchases, effective date, and process for exceptions. Staff must not split a single integrated project into smaller purchases to avoid a threshold or combine unrelated items solely to qualify for capitalization. The UN Finance Manual gives specific examples of thresholds for the UN reporting environment; those values are not automatic requirements for other NGOs. The asset recognition criteria still need to be satisfied, even when expenditure exceeds a policy threshold. Threshold calculations and overrides should be documented, reviewed, and traceable to the approved policy version.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a single integrated software project with several unrelated license purchases and explain how the approved unit of account and threshold policy apply."

Key point:
- Thresholds must be approved for the relevant entity and framework.
- Meeting a monetary threshold does not replace the substantive recognition criteria.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
14. Q: How should a perpetual license, a time-limited software license, and a SaaS subscription be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Examine the rights the contract grants rather than relying on the product name or payment frequency. A perpetual or time-limited license may provide identifiable rights that require an intangible-asset assessment. A SaaS subscription may instead grant access to supplier-controlled software over a period, which is generally a service arrangement under the IFRS Interpretations Committee's described fact pattern. Contract terms may nevertheless require further analysis if they convey control of software or another qualifying right. Inspect ownership, decision-making rights, hosting arrangements, renewal clauses, access restrictions, termination provisions, and deliverables. Separate software rights from implementation, support, hosting, and maintenance services where appropriate. The conclusion must follow the applicable framework and the actual agreement, and the final accounting record should explain why the arrangement was classified as an asset, service, lease, prepayment, or a combination as relevant.

Study instruction:
- "Compare three hypothetical contracts and identify the contractual clauses that affect the classification of rights and services."

Key point:
- Payment structure and product labels do not determine accounting classification.
- SaaS and license arrangements require a rights-based contractual assessment.

Source links:
- Customer's Right to Receive Access to the Supplier's Software Hosted on the Cloud — IFRS Foundation
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
15. Q: How should SaaS subscription fees and prepaid access be accounted for under the applicable framework?
A: Show answer
For the IFRS SaaS fact pattern addressed by the 2019 IFRIC agenda decision, the customer that receives only future access to supplier-controlled software generally receives a service over the contract term rather than a software intangible asset at commencement. If payment is made before the service is received, the amount may represent a prepayment that is recognized and released as the service is received. The conclusion must not be applied without checking whether the actual contract grants additional rights or another type of arrangement exists. Document contract duration, the service period, payment dates, renewal options, access rights, supplier responsibilities, and any distinct deliverables. Establish a prepaid-service schedule where necessary and reconcile releases to the applicable period. Do not capitalize the subscription merely because the supplier calls it a software license or because the charge recurs annually.

Study instruction:
- "Use an illustrative annual SaaS payment to distinguish the payment date, service period, prepayment balance, and service expense under the applicable framework."

Key point:
- A payment for future software access may represent a prepaid service rather than a software asset.
- Classification depends on contract rights and the relevant accounting authority.

Source links:
- Customer's Right to Receive Access to the Supplier's Software Hosted on the Cloud — IFRS Foundation
- Configuration or Customisation Costs in a Cloud Computing Arrangement — IFRS Foundation
16. Q: How should configuration and customization costs in cloud-computing arrangements be evaluated?
A: Show answer
Distinguish configuring the supplier's existing software from developing or obtaining additional code or another identifiable output. Under the IFRS Interpretations Committee's 2021 agenda decision, configuration or customization of supplier-controlled SaaS does not automatically create an intangible asset for the customer. The customer assesses the nature of the output, whether it is separately identifiable, whether the customer controls it, and whether the applicable recognition criteria are met. If no intangible asset is recognized, service timing and prepayment requirements must be considered under the applicable IFRS requirements. The arrangement may require analysis of whether implementation services are distinct or connected with the service of receiving software access. Obtain technical descriptions, statements of work, intellectual-property clauses, ownership provisions, and delivery evidence. For an IPSAS or other reporting entity, verify whether the relevant rules produce the same treatment before adopting this analysis.

Study instruction:
- "Review a cloud implementation statement of work and identify whether each deliverable is a setting, a supplier service, or potentially controlled additional code."

Key point:
- Configuration and customization expenditure is not automatically capitalizable.
- The output, rights, service timing, and applicable framework determine treatment.

Source links:
- Configuration or Customisation Costs in a Cloud Computing Arrangement — IFRS Foundation
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
17. Q: Which contract clauses are most important when assessing software rights and accounting control?
A: Show answer
Review the clauses concerning ownership, intellectual property, license scope, user limits, source-code access, modification rights, sublicensing, exclusivity, restrictions on use, termination, renewal, data access, transferability, and supplier dependency. These clauses help identify what the organization has acquired, how long the rights last, whether the deliverable is separately identifiable, and what happens if the arrangement ends. Legal should confirm the interpretation of material or uncertain contractual rights, while IT should verify the technical description of the arrangement. Finance then evaluates the accounting implications using the applicable framework. Contractual rights and technical evidence must be consistent; a specification describing customer-owned code should not be accepted without reviewing the assignment provisions. Keep the signed agreement and approved assessment linked to the asset record or the service arrangement record.

Study instruction:
- "Create a contract-review checklist and test whether the accounting conclusion is supported by the executed rights and deliverable provisions."

Key point:
- Contract wording can materially affect whether software rights are controlled by the organization.
- Legal, technical, and accounting conclusions should be connected but independently evidenced.

Source links:
- Customer's Right to Receive Access to the Supplier's Software Hosted on the Cloud — IFRS Foundation
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
18. Q: How should Procurement, IT, Legal, Grants, and Finance coordinate a software acquisition?
A: Show answer
Procurement manages the authorized purchasing process and supplier documentation; IT evaluates architecture, security, technical deliverables, access, and readiness; Legal reviews contractual rights and obligations; Grants assesses donor restrictions, funding eligibility, and budget conditions; and Finance makes the accounting assessment and records the approved classification. These responsibilities should be connected by a standardized handoff checklist. For example, payment authorization must not be confused with accounting recognition, and technical acceptance does not independently prove that all intangible-asset criteria have been met. The checklist should record the transaction identifier, supplier, contract version, deliverables, project and funding codes, responsible owners, required reviews, and unresolved issues. Missing evidence should generate an exception rather than permit the workflow to proceed through an undocumented assumption.

Study instruction:
- "Map the acquisition workflow from request to payment and asset-register entry, identifying the evidence supplied at every departmental handoff."

Key point:
- Cross-functional responsibilities require explicit evidence and acceptance criteria.
- Payment approval, technical acceptance, donor approval, and accounting recognition are distinct decisions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
19. Q: How should roles and segregation of duties be designed for intangible-asset accounting?
A: Show answer
Identify separate responsibilities for initiating a request, confirming rights, validating technical completion, preparing the accounting analysis, reviewing the calculation, approving recognition, creating the asset record, posting journals, and reconciling the ledger. The same person should not be able to establish an unsupported asset, approve their own assessment, change critical parameters, and clear the resulting reconciliation without independent oversight. Where limited staffing prevents full separation, document compensating controls such as independent review of source evidence, approval by an authorized manager, and post-transaction reconciliation by another employee. Access rights should reflect each person's role and delegated authority. Maintain an approved role matrix, delegation register, user-access record, and review evidence. Consultant participation does not eliminate management's responsibility for the organization's own approvals and financial reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a responsibility matrix and identify any combination of duties that could allow an employee to bypass an essential control."

Key point:
- Material recognition and lifecycle decisions require appropriate independent review.
- Compensating controls must be documented and demonstrably performed.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
20. Q: What evidence is required before a software asset assessment can be marked complete?
A: Show answer
Evidence will vary by arrangement but may include the approved request, executed contract, license terms, rights or assignment clauses, supplier invoices, detailed cost allocation, project milestones, technical acceptance, available-for-use evidence, approval record, accounting calculation, and proposed general-ledger mapping. Internally generated assets additionally require project-stage records and evidence supporting the applicable development criteria. The reviewer must confirm that the documents refer to the same transaction, deliverable, period, and contract version. A narrative explanation without underlying evidence should not close a material control requirement. If documentation is incomplete, the record should specify exactly what is missing, who must obtain it, when it is due, and whether accounting recognition, posting, or release must remain on hold. Retain both the conclusion and the source evidence used to reach it.

Study instruction:
- "Select a software acquisition and verify that every mandatory recognition criterion can be traced to a dated and identifiable supporting record."

Key point:
- Evidence sufficiency must be demonstrated, not assumed.
- Unresolved evidence gaps require an explicit hold, owner, and resolution date.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
21. Q: What should the software-asset request and approval workflow contain?
A: Show answer
The workflow should start with a unique request identifier and capture the business need, proposed deliverable, funding source, project code, supplier or development team, estimated expenditure, contract reference, intended use, expected operational date, and responsible owner. It should then route the request through the appropriate technical, procurement, legal, grant, and accounting assessments. Material recognition decisions must use the applicable criteria and an authorized reviewer; the workflow should not treat a request or budget authorization as proof of an asset. The record should track status, required evidence, approval dates, unresolved questions, and links to the final accounting assessment. Status transitions such as requested, evidence review, independent review, approval pending, approved, on hold, returned, and rejected need defined conditions. Any change to a previously approved material assumption should trigger a documented reassessment.

Study instruction:
- "Create a workflow for a hypothetical software acquisition and specify the conditions required before each status transition can occur."

Key point:
- Workflow status must represent verifiable completion of defined control requirements.
- Approval of a purchase does not automatically approve capitalization.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
22. Q: What information should the intangible-asset register contain?
A: Show answer
The register should enable identification, measurement, lifecycle monitoring, reconciliation, and reporting of every recorded asset. Useful fields include asset ID, description, class, project, funding source, supplier or development project, contract reference, recognition date, available-for-use date, cost, accumulated amortization, impairment, carrying amount, useful life, amortization method, residual value where applicable, custodian, status, and linked evidence. The exact requirements depend on the organization's framework and policies. System controls should prevent duplicate IDs, invalid asset classes, impossible dates, unsupported negative values, and unauthorized changes. Distinguish assets under development from operational assets and track disposals and expiry explicitly. The register must reconcile to the general ledger and applicable reporting outputs. Adding a row to a spreadsheet is not itself proof that the accounting decision is valid or approved.

Study instruction:
- "Design an asset-register data dictionary that defines every field, its source, validation rules, owner, and reconciliation purpose."

Key point:
- The register is a controlled accounting record, not merely an inventory list.
- Data integrity and reconciliation are essential to reliable asset reporting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
23. Q: How should initial asset cost, cost allocation, and duplicate charging be controlled?
A: Show answer
The cost schedule should identify each component included in the proposed asset value, the reason for its inclusion, its supporting evidence, the applicable accounting requirement, and the allocation method where costs are shared. Reconcile the schedule to invoices, approved timesheets, project records, and the general ledger. Shared expenditure must use a reasonable and consistently applied allocation basis that reflects the supported benefit or applicable cost attribution requirements. Ensure that one cost is not duplicated across software, hardware, service arrangements, project expenditure, and multiple asset records. Verify arithmetic, currencies, dates, and rounding, then independently recalculate material amounts. The accounting assessment must distinguish eligible asset cost from expenses, prepayments, commitments, and unresolved expenditure. Any correction should identify the original amount, revised amount, reason, approval, and effects on the related records and reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a multi-component supplier invoice to the proposed asset cost and show how excluded items, allocations, and duplicate charges are handled."

Key point:
- Asset cost needs a reproducible calculation supported by source documents.
- Duplicate charging and unsupported cost allocations must be identified before posting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
24. Q: When should amortization begin for a recognized software asset?
A: Show answer
Determine the date on which the asset is available for use under the applicable accounting framework. This is not necessarily the purchase order date, invoice date, payment date, contract signature date, or first user login. Relevant evidence may include deployment approval, successful testing, technical acceptance, completion certificates, user-readiness confirmation, and the absence of outstanding conditions that prevent intended use. The responsible technical owner should confirm operational status, while Finance independently evaluates the accounting consequence and approved start date. Assets still under development should not be treated as operational merely because they have incurred cost, although the applicable framework's specific requirements must be checked. Record the available-for-use date and supporting evidence in the asset register and verify that the amortization schedule uses the approved period and convention consistently.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a software invoice date with testing, acceptance, and operational-readiness dates and justify the proposed amortization start date."

Key point:
- Amortization timing depends on the asset's supported available-for-use status under the applicable framework.
- Technical readiness and accounting approval must be documented.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
25. Q: How should useful lives, amortization methods, and residual values be established?
A: Show answer
Determine the applicable framework's requirements and select a useful life that reflects the expected pattern and duration of the asset's benefits or service potential, subject to relevant contractual and legal limits. Consider license expiry, vendor support, expected technological obsolescence, replacement plans, product roadmaps, security requirements, and the asset's actual intended use. Evaluate the appropriate amortization method and residual value under the governing rules and approved policy. Do not copy the useful life of another software product or apply a tax life automatically to financial reporting. Where specialized or high-value assets require a different life, retain the technical and accounting rationale and required approval. The UN Finance Manual contains illustrative classifications and useful-life ranges for the UN environment; an NGO must establish its own applicable requirements rather than treat those ranges as universal.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the expected life of a licensed application with its contractual term, vendor-support horizon, and replacement plan, then document a justified estimate."

Key point:
- Useful-life estimates must reflect the asset's circumstances and applicable framework.
- UN-specific examples should not be copied into another entity's policy without authorization and assessment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
26. Q: How should an amortization schedule be prepared and reconciled?
A: Show answer
The schedule should start from the approved asset cost, available-for-use date, useful life, amortization method, residual value where applicable, and relevant period conventions. It should calculate the current-period expense, accumulated amortization, and carrying amount while respecting any framework-specific requirements for impairment, disposal, or partial-period treatment. Check that the scheduled charges do not exceed the depreciable or amortizable amount and that changes are supported by approved assumptions. Use reliable decimal arithmetic and explicitly document rounding rules. Independently recalculate sample assets and compare schedule totals with the general ledger and asset register. Exceptions such as negative carrying values, missing start dates, life expiry, or amortization after derecognition must be flagged for review instead of silently corrected by a formula.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate an amortization schedule independently and reconcile opening cost, current-period amortization, accumulated amortization, and closing carrying amount."

Key point:
- Schedule calculations must be reproducible and reconciled with the accounting records.
- Formula exceptions must be investigated before the period is closed.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
27. Q: How should changes in useful life, amortization method, or residual value be handled?
A: Show answer
First determine whether the change results from new information about an existing estimate, a correction of an error, or a change in accounting policy under the applicable framework. These categories may produce different accounting consequences. Obtain evidence such as a revised license term, discontinued vendor support, a new replacement plan, a technical obsolescence assessment, or an identified calculation error. Document the original assumption, new information, decision, approval, effective period, and resulting schedule impact. Do not change prior records or recalculate historical amounts merely to make the current period look reasonable. Under IFRS, IAS 8 provides relevant requirements for distinguishing changes in accounting estimates from errors and other accounting-policy matters. An IPSAS-reporting entity must verify the corresponding applicable requirements before applying this analysis.

Study instruction:
- "Classify three scenarios as an estimate change, an error correction, or a potential policy change and identify the evidence and accounting consequences."

Key point:
- A new estimate and a prior-period error are not interchangeable.
- Changes must be assessed and approved under the confirmed accounting framework.

Source links:
- IAS 8 Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and Errors — IFRS Foundation
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
28. Q: What impairment indicators should trigger a review of an intangible asset?
A: Show answer
Monitor whether the asset remains useful and whether evidence suggests its carrying amount may no longer be recoverable or supportable under the applicable framework. Potential indicators include technological obsolescence, discontinued products, loss of required rights, supplier failure, damaged or unusable software, cancelled projects, reduced service demand, significant underperformance, security restrictions that prevent use, or a decision to abandon an application. The organization should also check whether its framework requires particular assessments regardless of external indicators. The register should record the trigger, date identified, affected asset, responsible owner, initial assessment, required action, and resolution deadline. An unresolved indicator should not be dismissed simply because the software remains listed in the register or because the original project expenditure was donor-funded.

Study instruction:
- "Review a portfolio of software assets and identify evidence that would trigger an impairment assessment under the applicable framework."

Key point:
- Continued presence in the register does not prove that an asset remains recoverable.
- Impairment triggers require a documented assessment and appropriate follow-up.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Impairment
- IAS 36 Impairment of Assets — IFRS Foundation
29. Q: How should an impairment assessment and any resulting loss be documented?
A: Show answer
Follow the impairment model applicable to the organization and determine which measurement basis, assumptions, and disclosure requirements apply to the asset. For an IFRS asset within IAS 36, the assessment uses the applicable recoverable-amount requirements; non-cash-generating public-sector assets may require a different framework-specific approach. The UN's impairment guidance addresses its treatment of non-cash-generating assets, including intangible assets, but should not be assumed to govern another organization automatically. Retain the carrying amount, trigger evidence, valuation or service-potential analysis as applicable, assumptions, calculations, independent review, decision, and proposed journal. If the available evidence is insufficient, preserve the unresolved status and escalate it. Any reversal of a previously recognized loss must also satisfy the applicable criteria and approval process.

Study instruction:
- "Build an illustrative impairment assessment using the correct framework and show how assumptions, valuation support, review, and the accounting entry connect."

Key point:
- Impairment measurement depends on the asset and applicable framework.
- The assessment and any loss or reversal require supportable calculations and approvals.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Impairment
- IAS 36 Impairment of Assets — IFRS Foundation
30. Q: How should maintenance, bug fixes, upgrades, and software enhancements be classified after initial recognition?
A: Show answer
Assess subsequent expenditure according to what it does, whether it creates or improves a qualifying resource, and the requirements of the applicable framework. Routine support, minor bug fixes, maintenance, and services intended to keep existing software operating should not automatically be capitalized. A substantial upgrade or newly developed functionality may warrant further analysis if it creates an identifiable resource controlled by the organization and meets the recognition criteria. Obtain the change request, technical specification, before-and-after functionality, contract or ownership rights, detailed cost record, acceptance evidence, and explanation of future benefit or service potential where required. Separate maintenance from enhancement expenditure and reconcile costs to the related project and asset records. The approved classification must explain the economic substance and relevant evidence, not merely use the supplier's invoice terminology.

Study instruction:
- "Classify a maintenance invoice, minor bug fix, and substantial software enhancement, stating what evidence is needed before any expenditure can be capitalized."

Key point:
- Subsequent expenditure requires a separate evidence-based assessment.
- The words upgrade, enhancement, and software do not independently justify capitalization.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
31. Q: How should replacements of software components and major upgrades be reflected in asset records?
A: Show answer
Identify whether the expenditure relates to a distinct new resource, a replacement component, a service, or a modification of an existing asset. Determine whether the old component remains in use, has been replaced, has lost its value, or must be derecognized under the applicable framework. Update the register, carrying amounts, amortization calculations, and supporting records only after the required assessment and approval. Avoid capitalizing the replacement while leaving an unsupported old carrying amount in the register. Retain the link between the original asset, new deliverable, work order, cost breakdown, and disposal or replacement decision. Where the relevant records are incomplete, reconcile the technical asset inventory with the accounting register before making the adjustment.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical major software replacement from technical change request through new-cost analysis, review of the old component, and final ledger adjustment."

Key point:
- Replacement expenditure can have implications for both new recognition and the existing carrying amount.
- Related asset records must remain consistent through the replacement lifecycle.

Source links:
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
32. Q: How should donor conditions and grant restrictions be considered when accounting for software assets?
A: Show answer
Assess the accounting classification of the resource separately from donor eligibility and funding restrictions. A project budget may permit software expenditure without establishing that the expenditure qualifies as an intangible asset. Conversely, accounting recognition does not prove that the cost is allowable under the donor agreement. Review the grant agreement, approved budget, eligible-cost rules, procurement conditions, ownership or transfer clauses, permitted-use requirements, reporting obligations, and any restrictions after project closure. Record the funding source and project coding without treating donor approval as a substitute for the recognition assessment. If costs are allocated between several projects or funding sources, document the permitted allocation basis and reconcile the total amounts to prevent duplicate charging. Unclear donor conditions require review by the authorized grants or compliance owner.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the accounting-recognition decision with the donor-eligibility decision for a software purchase funded by a restricted grant."

Key point:
- Accounting recognition and donor cost eligibility are separate assessments.
- Grant terms and funding allocations must be documented and reconciled.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
33. Q: How should accounting treatment be distinguished from tax treatment for software expenditure?
A: Show answer
Maintain separate analyses for financial reporting, tax compliance, donor eligibility, and internal budget management. The accounting useful life or recognition conclusion does not automatically establish the tax treatment or deductible amount. Tax legislation may prescribe different classifications, timing, allowances, documentation, or rates. The organization's jurisdiction and legal status must be established before applying those rules. Where differences arise, document the applicable basis, calculations, supporting authority, and any required reconciliation between accounting and tax records. Coordinate material questions with the responsible tax professional and ensure that tax conclusions do not override the accounting framework. The fixed-asset or intangible-asset register may contain additional fields for tax tracking, but these must not be confused with the financial-reporting calculation.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a comparison showing how one software purchase is assessed for financial reporting, tax compliance, and donor-budget purposes."

Key point:
- Financial-reporting, tax, and donor rules serve different purposes.
- Tax consequences require the relevant law and entity-specific review.

- Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards — IAI official publications
34. Q: How should shared software costs be allocated to projects, donors, and cost centers?
A: Show answer
Define the allocation basis before distributing the expense or asset cost. Depending on the circumstances, the basis may reflect documented usage, eligible project time, licensed users, supported implementation effort, or another defensible measure. The selected method must comply with the organization's accounting policy, applicable funding agreements, and donor restrictions. It must be applied consistently and supported by source records. Reconcile all allocations to the original expenditure so that the total distributed amount equals the source total, subject to documented adjustments. Ensure one expenditure is not charged to multiple donors or capitalized in multiple asset records without a valid basis. Material changes to the allocation method require authorization and analysis of the affected periods and reports.

Study instruction:
- "Allocate an illustrative shared software cost across projects and reconcile the allocation to the source total, approved funding rules, and asset register."

Key point:
- Shared costs require a documented, supportable, and consistently applied allocation basis.
- Duplicate donor charging and duplicate capitalization must be prevented.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
35. Q: What accounting entries and posting controls are needed for intangible assets and software?
A: Show answer
Define the accounting entries and mapping rules required by the organization's confirmed framework and chart of accounts. Depending on the transaction, the workflow may involve recognizing an intangible asset, expensing services, recording a prepayment, transferring qualifying development costs, recording amortization, recognizing impairment, or derecognizing an asset. Each entry must reference the approved assessment, supporting documents, project and funding codes, reporting period, asset ID where relevant, and authorized preparer and reviewer. The workflow must distinguish an accounting journal from a payment transaction and from the underlying asset-register update. Before posting, validate account mappings, dates, amounts, debit-credit balance, and approval status. After posting, reconcile the resulting ledger balance to the approved schedule and retain evidence of any correction or reclassification.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare illustrative entries for a qualifying software asset, a SaaS prepayment, amortization, and disposal, using the organization's applicable accounting policies."

Key point:
- Journal entries must follow the confirmed framework and the approved transaction assessment.
- Posting, payment, and asset-register updates require traceable cross-references.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and journal workflows
36. Q: How should the intangible-asset register be reconciled with the general ledger and financial reports?
A: Show answer
Prepare a reconciliation that explains opening balances, additions, transfers, amortization, impairment, disposals, corrections, and closing balances by relevant asset class and account. Agree the opening balance to the prior approved closing balance, verify additions against source documents and approvals, and recalculate material movements. Compare register totals with the applicable general-ledger accounts and reporting outputs. Investigate differences arising from posting cut-off, missing journals, duplicate records, incorrect mappings, rounding, or unrecorded disposals. Each variance should include the amount, cause, proposed correction, owner, approval, and retest result. A balanced total is insufficient when underlying records remain incomplete or misclassified. Reconciliation should be independently reviewed and completed before the reporting pack is finalized, with unresolved material issues escalated rather than concealed by a manual balancing entry.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a monthly roll-forward for one asset class and trace each material movement to the ledger and supporting documentation."

Key point:
- A reconciliation must explain movements, not merely make totals agree.
- Unresolved differences require documented investigation and appropriate escalation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliations
37. Q: How should transfers, disposals, derecognition, expiry, and abandonment be controlled?
A: Show answer
The procedure should identify the event prompting the review, the authority to approve it, the accounting assessment, the required ledger entries, and the operational actions that must be completed. Obtain evidence of sale, cancellation, expiry, abandonment, loss of rights, replacement, or another relevant event. Determine whether the asset still meets the applicable recognition requirements and whether any carrying amount, amortization, impairment, or gain or loss requires treatment. Coordinate with IT to remove access and manage data, with Legal to address contractual obligations and rights, and with Grants to check applicable donor conditions. Do not derecognize an asset merely because it is fully amortized if the applicable framework and circumstances do not support that action. Reconcile the resulting register and ledger updates and retain approval and closure evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Complete a disposal checklist for an expired software license, including accounting, contract, cybersecurity, donor, and reconciliation requirements."

Key point:
- Derecognition requires evidence and authorization under the applicable framework.
- Financial closure must be coordinated with legal, operational, and data-security obligations.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
- IAS 38 Intangible Assets — IFRS Foundation
38. Q: What additional controls are needed when a license expires or a cloud software contract terminates?
A: Show answer
Before expiry or termination, confirm the contractual end date, renewal decision, outstanding invoices, data-export requirements, continuing access obligations, and any rights to retain or transfer deliverables. IT should implement approved access removal and data-handling actions, while Legal checks termination clauses and continuing rights and Finance resolves prepayments, outstanding liabilities, asset balances, and relevant accounting consequences. Grants may need to assess post-project ownership or continued-use restrictions. Record the responsible owners, planned dates, completion evidence, and unresolved matters. A license should not be marked closed merely because a calendar reminder has expired, and a financial closure should not hide continuing operational or legal obligations. Reconcile the operational software inventory with the contractual register and accounting records after the event.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sample software contract from renewal review through expiry or termination, data handling, access revocation, and accounting closure."

Key point:
- Contract expiry affects both accounting and operational controls.
- Access, data, and legal obligations must be resolved and evidenced.

39. Q: What should the period-end intangible-asset reporting pack include?
A: Show answer
The reporting pack should contain the asset-register roll-forward, additions and disposal schedules, amortization calculation, impairment review results, reconciliations to the general ledger, material accounting judgments, open exceptions, relevant donor or project information, and supporting approval evidence. Include the reporting period, schedule version, preparation date, reviewer, approval status, and source references. Distinguish draft calculations from approved figures and explain items that remain unresolved. The final report should present a traceable relationship between the accounting records and the financial statement amounts and disclosures required by the applicable framework. Use the organization's approved account mappings and reporting structure rather than copying illustrative mappings from the source document. Archive the approved output separately from the editable working files and document any post-approval corrections.

Study instruction:
- "Build a period-end reporting checklist and trace each material asset movement from the register to the financial report."

Key point:
- Period-end reports must reconcile to approved accounting records.
- Draft, approved, and subsequently corrected outputs must remain distinguishable.

40. Q: How should exceptions, errors, and overdue accounting assessments be managed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a central register for unresolved contract rights, incomplete development evidence, disputed cost allocation, missing approvals, calculation errors, unreconciled balances, overdue impairment reviews, and unauthorized changes. Every item should identify the asset or transaction, issue type, financial impact where known, risk level, responsible owner, target date, required corrective action, reviewer, and closure evidence. Distinguish a documentation gap from an accounting error and from a genuine policy interpretation question because each may require a different response. Material issues should be escalated through defined authority channels. Do not close an exception because it has been carried into the next reporting period or because the workbook status has changed. Closure requires evidence that the issue was resolved, the required records were corrected, and any necessary independent retest was completed.

Study instruction:
- "Create an exception register for three hypothetical software-accounting issues and define the evidence required for each to be closed."

Key point:
- Exception management requires an owner, deadline, corrective action, and evidence.
- Material issues must remain open until the required controls and accounting corrections are completed.

41. Q: How should audit trails, record retention, privacy, and access be managed for software-accounting evidence?
A: Show answer
Maintain records that allow reviewers to reconstruct who prepared, reviewed, approved, changed, and posted each material decision. Preserve the applicable contract version, source documents, cost calculations, recognition assessment, estimate approvals, register movements, journal references, reconciliation, and exception closure. Set retention periods using the actual legal, donor, contractual, and organizational requirements. Restrict access to confidential contracts, license keys, personal data, security-sensitive information, and commercially sensitive documents according to authorized roles. Avoid embedding passwords or authentication secrets in asset registers or broad-access workbooks. Backups should be protected and periodically tested for restoration. Where information is corrected, preserve the original record and an appropriate change history rather than silently replacing the source file. The archive should support retrieval by asset ID, transaction reference, project, period, and document type.

Study instruction:
- "Select one asset and reconstruct its full approval and accounting history from the retained records, checking access and version history."

Key point:
- Audit trails must preserve the history and evidence of material decisions.
- Privacy, retention, backup, and access requirements need explicit controls.

42. Q: How should system configuration, account mappings, and changes to the asset workflow be controlled?
A: Show answer
Document the approved configuration for asset classes, account mappings, recognition statuses, useful-life parameters, amortization rules, permissions, and reporting outputs where the organization's systems support these features. Every material change should identify the requester, business reason, affected assets and periods, relevant authority, approver, developer or implementer, test evidence, effective date, and rollback approach where appropriate. Test proposed changes in a controlled environment before release. A person who implements a critical change should not independently authorize their own work without suitable controls. After implementation, verify selected records and reconcile key totals to ensure that the system behaves as approved. Configuration establishes how a system processes information; it cannot create enforceable rights or independently determine the correct accounting treatment.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a change-control record for an amortization-parameter update, including independent testing, approval, implementation, and post-release reconciliation."

Key point:
- System configuration must follow approved accounting policy and authorization.
- Material changes require independent testing and evidence of successful implementation.

43. Q: How can spreadsheets and automation improve intangible-asset accounting without replacing professional judgment?
A: Show answer
Standardized Excel tables can hold the asset register, project-cost schedule, contract register, amortization assumptions, and reconciliation data. Power Query can import and transform structured source data, normalize identifiers, combine approved files, and flag duplicate or missing records. Excel formulas can support controlled calculations, while VBA can prepare recurring reports or record workflow events when designed and tested appropriately. The accounting criteria, useful-life decisions, impairment conclusions, and legal-rights assessments must remain governed by the applicable authority and authorized human review. Controls should capture source file, refresh date, transformation version, parameters, exception results, and approval status. Independently test the output against manually verified transactions, preserve source evidence, and prevent automated posting or status changes from bypassing required approval gates.

Study instruction:
- "Design a controlled register-import and reconciliation process, then test it against independently checked records, duplicate entries, and incomplete evidence."

Key point:
- Automation can improve consistency but cannot establish accounting validity by itself.
- Imported data, calculations, exceptions, and outputs require validation and traceable approvals.

44. Q: What testing is needed before releasing a new asset register or automated accounting workflow?
A: Show answer
Test both ordinary transactions and negative cases that must be rejected or placed on hold. Scenarios should include missing rights evidence, unauthorized approvals, duplicate asset IDs, invalid useful lives, premature amortization, unapproved thresholds, unsupported development costs, missing account mappings, inaccurate cost allocations, and unresolved reconciliation differences. Validate calculations independently and compare register, ledger, and reporting totals. Confirm that invalid workflow transitions cannot be completed merely by changing a status field. Record the test ID, scenario, input data, expected result, actual result, tester, evidence, defect, correction, and retest. Separate tests actually executed from tests that remain pending. A successful XML parse or spreadsheet calculation is not proof of complete system validation, and the organization must not claim that independent schema validation, application testing, or visual inspection occurred unless each activity was actually performed.

Study instruction:
- "Create a regression-test inventory for recognition, SaaS classification, amortization, disposal, approval permissions, and ledger reconciliation."

Key point:
- Positive and negative testing must demonstrate both valid processing and appropriate rejection of invalid cases.
- Test status and external validation limitations must be reported accurately.

45. Q: How should the organization approve, implement, and adopt the final SOP?
A: Show answer
The final procedure should identify the policy owner, independent reviewer, authorized approver, effective date, applicable entity and framework, version, and review frequency. Before adoption, resolve any organization-specific facts and policy choices, obtain the required authoritative sources, confirm contract and donor interfaces, approve roles and delegations, configure and test the workflow, reconcile sample outputs, and train the affected users. Maintain clear separation between an illustrative example, a controlled draft, an approved policy, and a live system implementation. Release should occur only after required evidence, testing, approvals, and implementation controls have been completed. A generated document does not establish organizational adoption, and a successful test in a sample workbook does not demonstrate that production controls are working. Record the final approval and confirm implementation independently.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an SOP adoption checklist covering source applicability, policy approval, system testing, training, controlled release, and post-implementation verification."

Key point:
- Document generation and organizational approval are separate events.
- Adoption requires authorized approval and evidence that operational controls are implemented.

46. Q: What should Finance do if a software invoice is available but the signed contract and evidence of rights are missing?
A: Show answer
Keep the accounting classification unresolved until the missing evidence is obtained or an authorized conclusion can be reached using sufficient alternative evidence permitted by the applicable framework. Request the executed agreement, license terms, order documentation, supplier confirmation, and details of the deliverables from Procurement and Legal. Ask IT to confirm what was supplied and how it is accessed or controlled. Finance should identify any payment, reporting, or closing deadlines that could be affected and escalate material uncertainty to the designated reviewer. Do not automatically capitalize the amount, invent contractual rights, or treat the invoice as sufficient proof that the organization controls an asset. If recognition is not supported, determine the appropriate provisional or final accounting treatment under the applicable framework and document the basis. Update the decision when reliable evidence becomes available, preserving the original assessment and change history.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a missing-contract scenario and identify evidence requests, temporary controls, approval requirements, and the conditions for final classification."

Key point:
- A supplier invoice alone may not establish the rights necessary for asset recognition.
- Missing legal and technical evidence must be resolved or formally escalated.

47. Q: What should Finance do when a development project is completed before its capitalization criteria were documented?
A: Show answer
Reconstruct the project history using dated technical assessments, development plans, approvals, contractor records, staff time records, testing evidence, and other contemporaneous documents. Determine whether the applicable recognition criteria were met and when the evidence demonstrates that they were met. Do not assume that project completion establishes that qualifying criteria existed from the beginning. Separate expenditure incurred before any qualifying date from later potentially eligible expenditure and apply the applicable framework's requirements. If the historical evidence is insufficient, retain the unresolved issue, assess its materiality, and obtain an authorized accounting determination. Document the conclusion and any required correction, including the impact on current and comparative reporting where applicable. Future projects should use mandatory phase gates so that qualifying dates and costs are supported when they arise rather than reconstructed retrospectively.

Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct a hypothetical project's timeline and identify the earliest date supported by evidence for each applicable recognition criterion."

Key point:
- The completion date is not proof that every earlier cost qualified for capitalization.
- Phase-gate records should be contemporaneous and linked to the expenditure they authorize.

48. Q: How should an organization resolve a disputed accounting treatment for SaaS customization or additional software code?
A: Show answer
Collect the executed agreement, supplier statement of work, technical description, intellectual-property provisions, delivery and acceptance records, and explanation of who controls the output. Determine whether the organization receives only access to supplier-controlled software, a distinct service, or a potentially identifiable resource such as additional code. Apply the relevant requirements and agenda decisions of the confirmed accounting framework. Under the IFRS guidance for the described cloud arrangements, customization costs do not automatically create an intangible asset; if no asset is recognized, the timing of service expense and any prepayment must also be assessed. Document the relevant facts, accounting alternatives, authoritative basis, judgment, and approval. For an IPSAS or other framework, verify whether the cited IFRS interpretation applies or whether the appropriate local guidance leads to a different conclusion.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a decision paper for a SaaS customization dispute, separating the contractual facts, technical output, rights assessment, recognition analysis, and service-timing conclusion."

Key point:
- Classification depends on the actual output and controlled rights, not the word customization.
- IFRS-specific agenda decisions must not be assumed to govern every reporting framework.

49. Q: How should management evaluate whether the intangible-assets SOP and related controls are effective?
A: Show answer
Establish measurable indicators with defined populations, calculation methods, evidence sources, frequency, owners, and escalation thresholds. Useful indicators may include the proportion of material assets with complete rights evidence, the number of unresolved recognition cases, reconciliation differences, overdue impairment reviews, expired licenses still recorded as active, changes made without complete approval, and the timeliness of period-end reporting. Evaluate trends and root causes rather than focusing only on total counts. Verify dashboard results against source records and sample transactions because automated status fields may be stale or incorrect. Management should assign corrective actions to the relevant owners and confirm that material issues are closed through independent review. Targets must be appropriate to the organization's actual risk, obligations, size, and processes rather than copied from the fictional demonstration.

Study instruction:
- "Design a control-effectiveness dashboard and independently verify each metric's population, calculation, evidence source, and escalation rule."

Key point:
- Effective monitoring measures control performance using verifiable evidence.
- Management indicators need clear definitions and documented corrective action.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 9 — Intangibles
50. Q: How should the organization monitor changes in intangible-asset standards and software-accounting guidance?
A: Show answer
Assign a responsible person to monitor official standard-setter publications, applicable national accounting guidance, authoritative interpretations, and changes in organizational policy. Record the publication date, effective date if established, affected framework, relevant transactions, implications for existing assets or contracts, and required changes to the SOP or system. Distinguish effective requirements from research projects, tentative proposals, and non-binding discussion papers. The IFRS Foundation's September 2026 IASB update reports continued discussion of potential changes to aspects of IAS 38's definition and recognition requirements using, among other things, SaaS-related scenarios. The update states that the IASB was not asked to make decisions on that discussion. It should therefore be monitored as a standard-setting development rather than treated as a newly effective accounting rule. Before changing policy or formulas, assess applicability, test proposed changes, secure approval, and communicate the effective version to all affected users.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a source-monitoring register that distinguishes currently applicable requirements from proposals and research developments and documents the approval of any required policy changes."

Key point:
- Standard-setting discussions are not automatically effective accounting requirements.
- Policy and system changes need verified authority, impact assessment, testing, and approval.

- IASB Update September 2026 — IFRS Foundation
- Intangible Assets Project — IFRS Foundation
