Inventory, Supplies and Consumables Controls
1. Q: How should an end-to-end inventory, supplies, and consumables control framework operate in an international NGO?
A: Show answer
An effective framework connects authorized acquisition, ownership and custody assessment, receipt, inspection, classification, valuation, storage, issue, distribution, transfers, returns, physical counts, discrepancy investigations, damage assessment, disposal, accounting reconciliation, and management reporting. Each transaction should have a unique reference, supporting evidence, an accountable owner, appropriate authorization, and a defined completion status. Finance and warehouse teams should coordinate so that physical movements are reflected accurately in the inventory records and, where applicable, the general ledger. The framework must also distinguish organization-owned stock from supplier-owned consignment, partner-held goods, donated goods awaiting acceptance, and supplies that qualify for immediate expense recognition. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional demonstration and must not be treated as an adopted organizational policy. Before implementation, verify the actual legal entity, applicable accounting framework, donor agreements, local requirements, approved policies, system configuration, and delegated authority.

Study instruction:
- "Map the complete inventory lifecycle and identify the responsible person, required evidence, approval, system record, and accounting consequence at each stage."

Key point:
- Inventory control links physical custody, accounting treatment, authorization, and reporting.
- The example SOP requires entity-specific review and formal approval before operational use.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
2. Q: What is the purpose of an inventory, supplies, and consumables SOP, and which processes should it cover?
A: Show answer
The SOP establishes consistent responsibilities and controls for receiving, holding, issuing, transferring, counting, valuing, and disposing of relevant items. It should specify the activities covered, the transactions that initiate the process, the completion conditions, the required records, and the interfaces with procurement, accounts payable, grants management, fixed assets, program distribution, safety, financial close, and records management. Adjacent processes should remain under their own approved procedures unless formally incorporated into the inventory SOP. For example, procurement may authorize an order, but this does not prove that the goods were delivered. Likewise, payment of a supplier invoice does not establish the quantity, condition, ownership, or inventory value of goods held. The organization should document each interface, identify the responsible process owner, and confirm that referenced procedures actually exist and are current. Unresolved boundaries should be recorded as implementation gaps rather than filled with invented requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a process-boundary diagram showing the handoffs between Procurement, Warehouse, Programs, Finance, Safety, and Records Management."

Key point:
- An SOP should define its scope and distinguish its responsibilities from those of adjacent processes.
- A handoff is controlled only when responsibility and evidence are clearly assigned.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Life Cycle
3. Q: How should an NGO determine which accounting framework and inventory valuation rules apply?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying the reporting entity, legal form, jurisdiction, reporting purpose, reporting period, and applicable accounting requirements. Determine whether the organization follows a specific Indonesian Financial Accounting Standard framework, another legally or contractually applicable framework, or a reporting framework required by its governing arrangement. Then obtain the current authoritative text and identify the provisions that apply to each inventory class. Document the selected recognition criteria, materiality policy, cost components, valuation formula, subsequent measurement requirements, rounding conventions, accounting mappings, and approval authority. Donor agreements, tax law, grant conditions, and operational rules may impose additional requirements, but they do not automatically replace financial-reporting requirements. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides a useful reference for inventory accounting and controls; however, its policies and Umoja procedures are not automatically binding on an independent NGO. The source SOP therefore correctly maintains a HOLD status until applicability and the required approvals are established.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an applicability worksheet identifying the legal entity, reporting framework, authoritative source, affected inventory classes, unresolved decisions, and approving authority."

Key point:
- Source existence does not establish applicability to a particular organization.
- No operational valuation policy should be activated while material framework decisions remain unresolved.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
- Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards — Indonesian Institute of Accountants
4. Q: What is the difference between physical possession, custody, ownership, control, and accounting recognition?
A: Show answer
Physical possession means that goods are physically present at a location. Custody means that an individual or organization is responsible for safeguarding or handling the goods. Ownership and control concern the relevant rights, obligations, substantive contractual terms, and ability to direct the use of the goods under the applicable legal and accounting framework. Accounting recognition is a separate determination based on the relevant recognition criteria. These concepts can produce different results. For example, supplier-owned consignment goods may be stored in an NGO warehouse without qualifying as the NGO's inventory asset. Similarly, an NGO may control equipment or supplies acquired through a donor-funded arrangement even when the payment was made directly by the donor. The register should separately identify the legal or beneficial owner as relevant, the custodian, the current location, the permitted use, and the accounting classification. Unsupported ownership or control conclusions must be escalated before recognition, valuation, or reporting is finalized.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a purchased item, supplier-owned consignment goods, donor-funded supplies, and partner-held stock. Document the evidence for ownership, custody, permitted use, and accounting treatment in each case."

Key point:
- Physical presence does not prove ownership or accounting recognition.
- Custody records and financial inventory records must be distinguished and reconciled appropriately.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
5. Q: How should inventory be distinguished from an immediate expense, a prepayment, property, plant and equipment, and third-party custody stock?
A: Show answer
Classification should reflect the transaction's substance, ownership or control, intended use, expected benefit, applicable recognition criteria, and approved materiality policy. Inventory may be appropriate for qualifying goods held for internal consumption, distribution, production, or another purpose covered by the applicable framework. A payment made before goods or services are received may instead create a prepayment until the relevant performance or delivery occurs. An item that meets the applicable fixed-asset recognition criteria may require property, plant and equipment treatment rather than inventory classification. Certain low-value consumables may be expensed upon acquisition or receipt where the applicable framework and a documented materiality policy support that approach. Supplier-owned or partner-owned goods held solely for custody should not automatically be recognized as owned inventory. The organization should define decision criteria for each class, document exceptions, and prevent the same stock from being recognized twice in different asset categories. Item labels or funding codes alone are insufficient to determine the accounting outcome.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample transactions into inventory, expense, prepayment, property, plant and equipment, or custody-only stock, and document the governing recognition criteria."

Key point:
- Classification must follow the applicable accounting rules and the facts of the transaction.
- An arbitrary price threshold or warehouse location is not a substitute for an approved classification policy.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
6. Q: What information should a controlled inventory item master contain?
A: Show answer
Each item should have an immutable unique identifier, item code, description, inventory class, base unit of measure, approved unit conversions, ownership basis, location, custodian, stock status, relevant batch or serial information, expiry attributes, safety requirements, and donor, project, fund, or grant restrictions. Where the item is recognized as inventory, the master should also contain the approved valuation-policy reference, currency, general-ledger mapping, reporting classifications, effective dates, and status of required approvals. Source evidence, creator, reviewer, approval date, version, and change history should be traceable. Required fields should depend on the item's characteristics. Expiry information may be essential for medicines and perishable supplies but irrelevant for some learning materials. Changes to descriptions, units, ownership, or financial mappings must not silently rewrite the meaning of historical transactions. A controlled master list should reject duplicate identifiers, unknown units, invalid classes, and missing mandatory attributes. Items that fail a critical validation should be placed on hold until the underlying data is corrected and independently verified.

Study instruction:
- "Design an item-master data dictionary and define mandatory fields, conditional fields, validation rules, ownership of changes, and effective-date requirements."

Key point:
- Reliable inventory reporting depends on consistent, validated, and historically traceable master data.
- Historical item identities and prior mappings should be preserved rather than overwritten.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory and Inventory Records
7. Q: Why must units of measure and conversion factors be controlled?
A: Show answer
Different suppliers and operational teams may describe the same stock using cartons, cases, packs, kits, bottles, or individual units. Without approved conversion factors, quantities can be overstated, understated, or recorded inconsistently between purchasing, storage, distribution, and accounting. The inventory master should designate one base unit for each item and define permitted conversions, their numerical factors, effective dates, and supporting evidence. For example, if an approved case contains ten kits, receiving three complete cases should increase the base quantity by thirty kits. An unknown or conflicting package configuration must be blocked for review rather than assigned a guessed factor. Partial packages, damaged packaging, and changes in supplier packaging also require explicit treatment. Quantity controls should compare transaction quantities before and after conversion and flag implausible values. The organization must preserve the original supplier unit and transaction details so that converted quantities can be reproduced during audit or reconciliation.

Study instruction:
- "Test a case-to-kit conversion, a missing conversion factor, a partial package, and a changed supplier pack size. Confirm that only approved conversions affect available quantities."

Key point:
- Unit conversions directly affect stock quantities and valuation calculations.
- Unknown conversion factors must fail validation instead of producing guessed results.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Acquisition and Movement
8. Q: How should goods be received, inspected, and accepted before they become available for use?
A: Show answer
Receipt should be linked to an authorized purchase order, contract, approved donation, transfer, or return record. An appropriately designated receiver should independently compare the delivered items with the authorized documentation and record quantities using approved units of measure. The inspection should cover condition, specification, packaging, batch or serial details, expiry where relevant, and any safety or temperature requirements. The receiver should distinguish accepted, rejected, damaged, uninspected, disputed, and pending quantities. The invoice may support the commercial transaction, but it does not prove that the goods physically arrived or met acceptance criteria. Where ownership, quantity, condition, donor restrictions, or supporting evidence remains unresolved, the affected goods should be recorded in a controlled pending or quarantine status. Only the quantities that have passed the defined release requirements should become available for issue. Finance should use the final acceptance evidence to support the corresponding accounting and supplier-liability records, as applicable.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sample delivery from its authorized order through quantity inspection, condition assessment, acceptance, inventory recording, invoice verification, and accounting reconciliation."

Key point:
- Receiving, commercial invoicing, and accounting recognition are related but distinct events.
- Stock should not become available until the applicable inspection and release criteria have been satisfied.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Acquisition and Inventory-In Procedures
9. Q: How should rejected, damaged, or uninspected deliveries be controlled?
A: Show answer
The warehouse should identify the affected quantities, physically segregate them where practical, and apply a status that prevents unauthorized picking or distribution. Records should identify the source transaction, supplier or donor, item, quantity, location, condition, date, responsible officer, and reason for the hold. The team should determine whether the next step is additional inspection, replacement, supplier return, donor notification, insurance referral, or approved disposal. Pending stock must not be treated as available merely because it appears in the warehouse system. Finance should assess whether the condition affects inventory recognition, the recorded cost, a potential recovery, a supplier claim, or an impairment assessment under the applicable requirements. Any subsequent release or reclassification requires evidence that the underlying issue has been resolved and that the responsible person is authorized to clear the hold.

Study instruction:
- "Create a quarantine record for a damaged delivery and identify the physical, operational, financial, and documentary checks required before the case can be closed."

Key point:
- Rejected or uninspected quantities must remain distinct from approved available stock.
- Resolving a physical delivery exception may also require accounting and supplier follow-up.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Acquisition, Write-Downs and Write-Offs
10. Q: Which costs should be included in inventory valuation?
A: Show answer
Inventory valuation should follow the applicable accounting framework and the organization's approved valuation policy. Depending on the framework and the nature of the inventory, relevant costs may include purchase price after applicable discounts or rebates and costs directly attributable to bringing goods to their present location and condition, such as qualifying freight or handling. Certain conversion costs may also be relevant for inventory produced by the organization. Costs that are unrelated to acquiring or preparing the inventory for its intended condition or use may need to be excluded or treated separately. Taxes, import charges, abnormal losses, storage, and administrative costs require specific analysis rather than blanket inclusion or exclusion. The workbook should preserve the original transaction values and separately identify cost components, currencies, conversion rates, and calculation rules. The approved policy must identify which components are eligible, how allocation is performed, which evidence is required, and how exceptions are reviewed. Illustrative cost rules should not be activated as an actual accounting policy without approval.

Study instruction:
- "Analyze a supplier invoice, freight charge, discount, tax, and storage expense. Document the proposed valuation treatment for each component under the confirmed applicable framework."

Key point:
- Inventory cost includes only components permitted by the applicable accounting requirements.
- Cost components, assumptions, and allocation methods must be documented and reproducible.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Recognition and Measurement
- IFRS IAS 2 — Inventories
11. Q: How should an NGO select and control an inventory cost formula?
A: Show answer
The organization should choose the cost formula that is permitted by the applicable framework and suitable for the nature of the inventory. Individually tracked or non-interchangeable items may require specific identification where the applicable requirements provide for it. For interchangeable inventory, a permitted FIFO or weighted-average approach may be appropriate under frameworks such as IAS 2. The choice must be approved, documented by item category or relevant class, applied consistently as required, and supported by reliable movement and cost records. The organization should define when costs enter the calculation, how returns and discounts affect cost, how rounding is performed, and how abnormal or exceptional transactions are treated. A physical picking rule such as first-expiry-first-out is an operational stock-rotation rule, not an accounting cost formula. Historical methods and parameter changes must be effective-dated, authorized, tested, and retained for audit. No candidate method in the fictional SOP should be treated as the actual organization's approved valuation policy.

Study instruction:
- "Compare permitted cost formulas for interchangeable and individually tracked goods, and document the evidence needed to justify the selected method."

Key point:
- Accounting cost formulas must be authorized and consistent with the applicable framework.
- Physical stock-rotation rules and financial cost formulas serve different purposes.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Costing
- IFRS IAS 2 — Inventories
12. Q: How should donated goods and inventory acquired through non-exchange transactions be assessed?
A: Show answer
Donated goods require an assessment of the applicable accounting framework, the terms of the donation, the date and conditions of acquisition, the organization's rights or control, and the valuation evidence available. Under the UN IPSAS inventory policy described in its Finance Manual, inventories acquired through non-exchange transactions, such as donated goods, are measured at fair value at the date of acquisition. That treatment should not be automatically imposed on an organization that follows a different applicable framework. The organization should record quantities, item descriptions, condition, valuation method, supporting market or other valuation evidence, relevant restrictions, acceptance date, and approval. Donor-imposed limitations on use must be recorded separately from valuation. A grant code or the donor's estimate of value is not, by itself, proof that the value is appropriate for financial reporting. Where the measurement basis or ownership remains uncertain, record the goods in a controlled pending status until the required decision is documented.

Study instruction:
- "Review a fictional donation of supplies and identify the evidence needed to establish ownership, acquisition date, condition, applicable valuation basis, and donor restrictions."

Key point:
- Donated goods require documented recognition and valuation analysis.
- Donor restrictions and financial valuation are distinct control questions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Acquired through Non-Exchange Transactions
13. Q: How should inventory be measured after initial recognition when its condition or recoverability changes?
A: Show answer
Subsequent measurement depends on the applicable framework and the inventory's purpose. For example, IAS 2 generally requires inventories within its scope to be measured at the lower of cost and net realisable value, while the UN IPSAS Finance Manual distinguishes inventory held for sale from inventory held for distribution at no or nominal charge or for consumption in the production of goods or services. The latter categories may be subject to a lower-of-cost-and-current-replacement-cost approach under the UN's stated policy. These approaches are not interchangeable and should not be applied without establishing framework applicability and the relevant inventory class. Slow-moving, obsolete, damaged, contaminated, or expired goods should be identified for review, with condition evidence, quantities, valuation calculations, and approval records. Any reversal of a previous write-down must meet the applicable requirements and be supported by evidence. A physical disposal event and a financial write-down are separate decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an item held for sale with an item held for distribution at no or nominal charge. Determine the applicable subsequent-measurement basis before calculating any adjustment."

Key point:
- Subsequent measurement depends on the applicable framework and the purpose of the inventory.
- A write-down should be supported by evidence and must not be confused with physical disposal.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Write-Downs
- IFRS IAS 2 — Inventories
14. Q: When may low-value consumables be expensed rather than recorded as inventory?
A: Show answer
The appropriate treatment depends on the applicable reporting requirements, the materiality of the amounts involved, the nature and quantity of the items, and the organization's approved accounting policy. The organization should not assume that every inexpensive item must be expensed immediately or that every item in a storeroom must be recognized as inventory. Where an expense-on-acquisition or expense-on-receipt approach is permitted and appropriately applied, the policy should define the relevant population, consistency requirements, operational records, and review controls. Materials that are expensed for financial-reporting purposes may still require physical custody and quantity tracking to prevent misuse, loss, unauthorized distribution, or unsafe handling. Conversely, a material stockholding may require inventory recognition even when its individual units are inexpensive. If the policy decision is unresolved, the preparer should not introduce an arbitrary monetary threshold into a spreadsheet and treat it as authoritative.

Study instruction:
- "Review materiality and recognition policies for consumables, then distinguish financial-reporting treatment from the warehouse controls still required."

Key point:
- Materiality decisions must be based on documented and applicable policies.
- Expensing an item does not eliminate the need for physical control where operational risks remain.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Recognition and Measurement
15. Q: How should storage, custody, safety, expiry, and stock rotation be controlled?
A: Show answer
Inventory should be traceable by location, ownership, responsible custodian, condition, stock status, and batch or lot where appropriate. Storage arrangements should reflect the characteristics of the items, including environmental conditions, security requirements, contamination risks, shelf life, and access restrictions. Expiry-sensitive or perishable goods should use batch-level controls and a documented rotation procedure. FEFO, or first-expiry-first-out, may be appropriate for relevant stock, but the organization should verify that the rule fits the specific products and operating requirements. Expired, recalled, damaged, contaminated, or quarantined items must be blocked from issue until the authorized safety and release conditions have been met. Monitoring should cover physical storage conditions, restricted access, stock movement, expiring items, and overdue inspections. The SOP should identify the relevant safety owner, escalation requirements, and evidence of clearance. Applicable product-specific safety laws and organizational procedures must be verified before the framework is adopted.

Study instruction:
- "Design a storage inspection checklist covering access, location, condition, batch traceability, expiry, environmental requirements, and quarantine status."

Key point:
- Availability in the system does not establish that stock is safe or suitable for use.
- Expiry and safety restrictions must be enforced in both physical handling and stock-status controls.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Life Cycle and Physical Verification
16. Q: What is the difference between FEFO stock rotation and an accounting cost formula?
A: Show answer
FEFO means first-expiry-first-out and prioritizes the physical issue of eligible batches with the earliest expiry date. It is an operational method intended to reduce avoidable expiry and support safe distribution. An accounting cost formula, such as weighted average or FIFO where permitted by the applicable framework, determines the financial cost assigned to inventories and consumption. The two methods answer different questions and should be implemented separately. A warehouse may select a batch under FEFO while the accounting system calculates its carrying cost using the approved financial method. The organization should document both methods, identify the item classes to which each applies, and test that batch selection does not accidentally change the approved accounting method. Expired or recalled goods must remain blocked regardless of the rotation rule. The inventory system should preserve batch identity, expiry dates, movement history, and valuation records so that both physical and financial controls remain auditable.

Study instruction:
- "Use a two-batch example with different expiry dates and purchase costs. Identify the physical batch to issue under FEFO and calculate financial cost using the approved accounting formula."

Key point:
- FEFO governs physical stock rotation; the cost formula governs financial valuation.
- A stock-rotation rule must not be used as a substitute for the approved accounting policy.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Consumption and Measurement
- IFRS IAS 2 — Inventories
17. Q: How should inventory issues, consumption, and program distributions be authorized and recorded?
A: Show answer
Every issue should be linked to a unique request or issue reference, item, quantity, approved unit of measure, source location, recipient or destination, purpose, project or donor classification where applicable, authorization, and dispatch evidence. The warehouse should validate available quantity, stock status, expiry, condition, ownership, and any restrictions before picking. Records should distinguish internal consumption from external distribution, transfer, return, and other movement types because their operational and accounting consequences may differ. Proof of delivery or recipient acknowledgement should be retained where required. Partial deliveries, rejected quantities, and returned goods should remain separately identifiable rather than being automatically treated as completed issues. Where the items are recognized as inventory, issue or consumption normally triggers the relevant expense, cost-of-distribution, or other prescribed accounting treatment under the applicable framework. The preparer must verify the account mapping and transaction date and reconcile the movement to the stock records and, where relevant, the general ledger.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one authorized issue from the original request through restriction validation, picking, dispatch, acknowledgement, inventory movement, and accounting review."

Key point:
- Every issue requires an authorized purpose, traceable quantity, and evidence of the relevant movement.
- Consumption and distribution must be recorded using the applicable accounting treatment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory-Out and Consumption Procedures
18. Q: How should donor, grant, project, fund, and destination restrictions be enforced for inventory?
A: Show answer
Each restricted stock class should have documented usage conditions derived from the relevant donor agreement, grant terms, approved budget, and internal authorization. Before release, the system or responsible reviewer should check that the intended destination, purpose, quantity, project, recipient, and timing are permitted. A project code is an identifier, not proof that a particular use is authorized or eligible. Where restrictions require approval for transfers between programs or geographic locations, obtain the authorization before dispatch. Conflicting or incomplete donor instructions should be escalated rather than interpreted informally by warehouse personnel. Records should demonstrate which goods were assigned, issued, transferred, returned, or disposed of under each relevant restriction. Donor reporting should reconcile opening balances, receipts, issues, transfers, and closing quantities or values where required. Financial classification and donor eligibility must remain distinct: a stock movement can be physically valid while still failing the donor's contractual conditions.

Study instruction:
- "Review a hypothetical restricted grant and test whether a requested distribution to a different project or destination is authorized by the documented terms."

Key point:
- Donor and grant restrictions require transaction-level evidence and appropriate authorization.
- Inventory movement records must support both financial accounting and donor accountability.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Life Cycle
19. Q: How should inter-location transfers and goods in transit be controlled?
A: Show answer
A transfer record should identify the originating and receiving locations, item and lot, ownership, quantities and units, approved destination, dispatch date, responsible custodians, transportation arrangements, and required authorization. The receiving location must independently confirm the quantity and condition received. Dispatch alone does not prove delivery, and a shipment must not be closed merely because the sending warehouse has recorded an issue. Quantities sent must reconcile to quantities received, documented discrepancies, authorized returns, or a supported in-transit balance. The accounting cut-off treatment must follow the applicable framework and actual contractual facts; the transfer date does not automatically establish the correct reporting date in every situation. Overdue transfers should be investigated according to risk and value, with follow-up evidence and an accountable owner. Differences involving donor-restricted stock, missing goods, damage, or suspected theft require escalation and appropriate financial and incident reviews before closure.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile one inter-location transfer from dispatch to destination confirmation, and explain how unreceived quantities remain visible until resolved."

Key point:
- Source dispatch and destination receipt are separate control events.
- In-transit stock and discrepancies require traceable records and appropriate accounting cut-off decisions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Life Cycle
20. Q: How should returned supplies be inspected and returned to available stock?
A: Show answer
A return should be linked to the original issue, delivery, or transfer and should identify the item, quantity, recipient or source location, return reason, date, condition, packaging, batch, expiry, and responsible officer. Returned stock must be inspected before release. Items may require quarantine where the temperature history, integrity, contamination risk, or storage conditions are uncertain. Depending on the inspection outcome, the stock may be released, retained under restricted use, sent for specialist assessment, returned to a supplier, written down, or disposed of. The organization should determine the financial treatment by referring to the original transaction and the applicable accounting policy. The return does not automatically justify reversing the original expense or restoring stock at an assumed value. Any valuation adjustment, donor restriction, or safety issue should be reviewed and documented before the stock record and accounting records are updated.

Study instruction:
- "Process a hypothetical return of unused program supplies and identify the evidence required for quantity verification, safety inspection, stock-status reassessment, and any accounting adjustment."

Key point:
- Returned goods must pass inspection and authorization before becoming available again.
- A physical return does not automatically reverse the original accounting entry.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Recognition and Measurement
21. Q: How should an NGO plan and perform physical inventory counts?
A: Show answer
The organization should establish a risk-based count policy that defines count frequency, population coverage, location coverage, cutoff procedures, independent supervision, recount requirements, and certification responsibilities. Counts may include scheduled cycle counts, periodic full counts, year-end counts, surprise counts, and event-triggered counts after a suspected loss or significant incident. The count population should distinguish owned inventory, custody-only goods, partner-held stock, stock in transit, donations pending acceptance, damaged goods, expired goods, and quarantined items. Count teams should be independent of the day-to-day custody function where practicable, and blind counts may be used when knowing the recorded quantity could bias the result. Movement controls should minimize unrecorded receipts and issues during the count. Original count sheets and electronic records must be preserved, with amendments traceable. Count certification should confirm the scope and completion of the exercise, not merely state that the closing quantity agrees with the system.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a count plan for a multi-location warehouse and identify the population, cutoff, team independence, count evidence, recount rules, and certification process."

Key point:
- Physical verification is a detective control that tests the accuracy and condition of stock records.
- Risk-based counting requires documented coverage, independence, and a controlled response to discrepancies.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Physical Verification
22. Q: Why should stock counters be independent of custodians, and when are blind counts appropriate?
A: Show answer
Independent counting reduces the risk that the person responsible for the stock will inadvertently confirm or conceal inaccuracies in their own records. Where staffing constraints prevent full segregation, the organization should define compensating controls, such as independent recounts, supervisor observation, variance review, or subsequent sample checks by another authorized person. A blind count withholds the recorded quantity from the counter where that approach is appropriate, encouraging an independent physical observation rather than an automatic agreement with the book balance. The count team should still have enough item-identification information to identify the correct goods. Any count sheet changes must preserve the original result and show who made the change, when, why, and who reviewed it. The custodian must not independently approve an adjustment arising from a discrepancy in stock under their sole control. Count certification and the authority to post financial adjustments should remain distinct responsibilities.

Study instruction:
- "Design compensating controls for a small field office where complete segregation is not practical, and document how independent review will be evidenced."

Key point:
- Independence improves the reliability of count evidence.
- Limited staffing requires documented compensating controls, not an assumption that segregation is unnecessary.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Physical Verification and Inventory Controls
23. Q: How should inventory count variances be investigated before any adjustment is posted?
A: Show answer
Open a uniquely identified discrepancy case that records the item, location, period, recorded quantity, physical quantity, difference, relevant unit cost or estimate, ownership, restrictions, supporting documents, responsible owner, and due date. Investigate transaction cutoff, duplicate or omitted records, unit conversions, unrecorded receipts or issues, transfers, returns, damage, stock-status errors, and possible loss or unauthorized movement. Obtain a recount or additional evidence when necessary. The explanation must be supported by documents and independent review; a verbal statement alone is not sufficient to authorize a material adjustment. Suspected theft, fraud, safety events, or donor breaches should be referred to the applicable incident process. Once the cause is understood, the appropriate authorized person determines the necessary physical, system, and accounting corrections. Closure requires the approved action, posting evidence where applicable, independent retest, and reconciliation to the inventory records and general ledger.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a hypothetical shortage by documenting the evidence reviewed, root cause, risk assessment, proposed correction, approval, posting, and independent verification."

Key point:
- A count difference is an exception requiring investigation, not an automatic adjustment.
- Variance closure must be supported by cause analysis, authorization, and reconciliation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Physical Verification and Inventory Adjustments
24. Q: What is the difference between an inventory write-down, write-off, and physical disposal?
A: Show answer
A write-down reduces the carrying amount of inventory under the applicable measurement requirements while some or all of the goods may remain physically held. A write-off removes an amount from the accounting records when the applicable recognition or measurement requirements and approved authorization support derecognition. Physical disposal is the operational act of returning, destroying, disposing of, or otherwise removing goods from custody. These events may occur together, but they are not interchangeable. A damaged item might require a write-down before any disposal is authorized, whereas stock confirmed lost may require a separately investigated accounting and incident response. The process should document the condition assessment, valuation basis, affected quantity, applicable restrictions, approval, accounting entry, physical disposition, and independent verification. The organization must not assume that approval of a write-off proves disposal has occurred or resolves a suspected theft, safety, or donor-compliance incident.

Study instruction:
- "Compare three cases: damaged stock still held, expired stock awaiting disposal, and stock confirmed missing. Identify the valuation, authorization, physical, and accounting evidence for each."

Key point:
- Write-down, write-off, and physical disposal represent different control and accounting events.
- Closure requires both the appropriate accounting evidence and the required physical or incident documentation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Written Off and Written Down
25. Q: What accounting events should be assessed for inventory, and how should their journal implications be controlled?
A: Show answer
The organization should define accounting events for qualifying inventory acquisition, receipt, consumption, distribution, transfer, return, valuation adjustment, loss, write-off, and disposal. The precise entries depend on the applicable framework, the accounting configuration, the classification decision, contractual facts, and the organization's approved policies. Where inventory is recognized as an asset, an accepted purchase may affect inventory and the relevant payable or clearing account, while consumption may transfer the carrying amount to the appropriate expense or other prescribed account. A donation may require a different entry depending on the recognition requirements and transaction facts. A transfer between locations may require a movement in inventory records without changing the organization's aggregate recognized asset, while some transfers between entities may have different consequences. The SOP should specify approved accounts, posting triggers, source references, period cutoff, review requirements, and reconciliation procedures. Automated journals must not determine accounting treatment without the required framework and authorization checks.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare illustrative journal flows for an inventory purchase, authorized issue, count shortage, and write-down. Label each entry as conditional on the applicable accounting policy."

Key point:
- Journal entries must follow the approved accounting treatment and verified transaction facts.
- Operational movements and general-ledger postings must reconcile without double-counting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Accounting Entries
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
26. Q: How should the inventory subledger be reconciled to the general ledger and operational records?
A: Show answer
Establish a recurring reconciliation between physical or stock-card quantities, movement records, valuation calculations, inventory subledger balances, relevant general-ledger accounts, and required project or donor reporting views. The reconciliation should identify the reporting period, opening balance, receipts, issues or consumption, transfers, returns, count adjustments, write-downs, write-offs, and closing balance as applicable. It should distinguish recognized owned inventory from custody-only records so that third-party goods are not incorrectly included in the owned-asset balance. Reconcile both quantity and value, investigate unmapped items and unexplained residuals, and verify that transaction cutoff is correct. All differences require a named owner, explanation, supporting evidence, and resolution or formal escalation. The person who prepares the reconciliation should not be the sole reviewer or approver of material differences. Final certification should be blocked if significant unexplained residuals remain or the source population has not been demonstrated to be complete.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an inventory-to-GL reconciliation for one reporting period and trace the opening balance, movements, adjustments, closing balance, and any remaining differences."

Key point:
- A balanced general ledger does not prove that physical stock exists or that its value is correct.
- Quantity, valuation, custody, and financial records must be reconciled with documented evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
27. Q: How can an inventory roll-forward demonstrate that opening stock, movements, and closing stock are complete?
A: Show answer
A roll-forward should explain how the closing balance is derived from the opening balance and recorded activity. At a basic quantity level, opening quantity plus accepted receipts, approved returns, and other authorized increases, less issues, consumption, transfers out, disposals, and other authorized decreases, together with approved adjustments, should reconcile to closing quantity. Each component must be defined to avoid double-counting, particularly for inter-location transfers and goods in transit. A separate value roll-forward should use the applicable cost formula and approved valuation adjustments. The report should distinguish the quantities and values for owned inventory, custody-only goods, restricted items, and other relevant categories. It must also reconcile to the original source population and preserve an explanation for retrospective corrections. A roll-forward can reveal missing transactions or inconsistencies, but it does not independently verify physical existence or ownership; those questions require separate evidence and control procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Build a quantity and value roll-forward and test whether every movement category has a documented definition, source record, approval, and reconciliation rule."

Key point:
- Closing inventory must be explainable through a complete and consistent record of movements.
- Roll-forwards support, but do not replace, physical counts and independent reconciliation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Life Cycle and Year-End Adjustments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
28. Q: How should inventory control responsibilities and approval authority be segregated?
A: Show answer
The organization should distinguish, where practicable, requesting, procurement authorization, receiving and inspection, custody, count performance, accounting preparation, independent review, adjustment approval, system administration, and post-implementation verification. Each role should have defined permissions and prohibitions. A requester should not approve their own request where independent approval is required. A receiver should not independently authorize the purchase or substitute an invoice check for physical inspection. A custodian should not solely approve a write-off or material count adjustment arising from stock under their control. Delegated authority should be verified against the current role or individual, entity, item class, transaction type, value, currency, location, scope, and effective dates. A job title does not automatically establish authority. When limited staffing requires compensating controls, the organization should document the exception, independent reviewer, duration, review frequency, and evidence of operation.

Study instruction:
- "Construct a responsibility matrix for receipt, custody, physical counting, journal preparation, approval, and system release. Identify conflicts and design documented compensating controls."

Key point:
- Authorization, custody, accounting, and independent verification should not be concentrated in one person without assessed and documented compensating controls.
- Approval authority must be verified, current, in scope, and supported by evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Control Processes
29. Q: How should inventory systems control stock statuses and transaction workflows?
A: Show answer
The system should use defined stock states such as pending receipt, received but uninspected, quarantined, available, reserved, in transit, issued, return pending inspection, damaged, expired, recalled, disposal pending, disposed, custody-only, and retired where these states fit the organization's operations. Transaction workflows should also distinguish draft, validation pending, approval pending, approved and version-locked, posted, reconciliation pending, verified, closed, held, rejected, or cancelled states as relevant. Each transition should require defined conditions and authorized users. For example, an expired item should not become available without documented clearance by a qualified authorized person, and a transfer should not be closed without receipt confirmation or a formally approved resolution. The system must preserve prior statuses, responsible users, timestamps, and evidence. A status label alone is not a control: actual permissions and business rules must prevent prohibited transactions. Where enforcement is manual, the compensating review and evidence requirements should be explicit.

Study instruction:
- "Create a stock-status transition matrix identifying permitted transitions, authorized roles, required evidence, and prohibited transitions for expired, quarantined, custody-only, and in-transit stock."

Key point:
- Stock statuses must represent genuine conditions and enforceable control requirements.
- System status changes require authorization, evidence, and a traceable history.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Processes
30. Q: How should inventory-system changes, valuation-rule updates, and data migrations be controlled?
A: Show answer
Changes to item masters, valuation methods, cost components, unit conversions, accounting mappings, status rules, and access permissions should be assessed, approved, tested, and recorded before release. Testing should cover normal transactions, exceptions, boundary conditions, historical data, and negative cases that ought to be rejected. A controlled release should preserve the approved version, identify the change owner, record the reason and effective date, confirm backup and rollback arrangements, communicate the change, and obtain the required authorization. Migration should reconcile source and target item counts, quantities, units, ownership, statuses, lot records, values, mappings, open transfers, and residual differences. Historical records and previously approved rules must remain traceable after the change. After deployment, an independent reviewer should verify production behavior and reconcile material outputs. The organization should not infer that a workbook or software configuration is valid merely because it opens, calculates, or passes a limited technical test.

Study instruction:
- "Design a release checklist and migration reconciliation covering approved changes, positive and negative tests, backup, rollback, source-to-target totals, authorization, and production verification."

Key point:
- System and policy changes require controlled authorization, testing, historical preservation, and independent verification.
- Technical file integrity does not prove that accounting rules or operational controls are correct.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls
31. Q: What evidence should be retained to support inventory transactions and audit trails?
A: Show answer
The evidence repository should link authorized orders, contracts, donation and consignment terms, delivery notes, inspection records, custody confirmations, stock movements, approvals, valuation calculations, count sheets, variance investigations, write-down and write-off assessments, disposal evidence, reconciliations, system logs, and review records. Each document or transaction should have a unique reference and sufficient metadata to establish its date, source, owner, version, approval status, and relationship to the relevant item and reporting period. Corrections must preserve the prior state and record the reason, person, timestamp, and authorization where required. A file's presence in a folder does not prove that it is complete, valid, approved, or related to the transaction under review. The organization must establish retention periods and secure disposal requirements based on verified legal, donor, contractual, safety, and internal requirements. Access to personal, beneficiary, supplier, and donor information should be limited to personnel with a legitimate need to use it.

Study instruction:
- "Select a material inventory balance and assemble the complete supporting evidence from source transaction to final reporting, including approvals, version history, reconciliation, and applicable retention rules."

Key point:
- Auditability depends on traceable, authentic, complete, and appropriately retained evidence.
- Filing a document is not a substitute for verifying its substance and validity.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Supporting Records
32. Q: How should missing evidence, conflicting inventory records, and other exceptions be handled?
A: Show answer
Every material exception should be recorded in a controlled register with a unique identifier, description, affected item and location, potential quantity or value, severity, reporting period, owner, due date, containment action, and escalation status. Examples include missing receipt evidence, incompatible donor use, unidentified quantities, unresolved stock differences, incomplete valuation data, overdue transfers, expired items, failed reconciliations, and unapproved system changes. The responsible person should obtain additional evidence, determine the underlying cause, assess financial and operational consequences, and propose a corrective action. Higher-risk events should be escalated promptly to the appropriate Finance, management, safety, compliance, or incident owner. An exception must not be marked resolved merely because it was moved to the next reporting period or excluded from a report. Closure requires documented resolution, approved corrections where applicable, and independent verification. Recurring exceptions should also be analyzed to determine whether preventive controls, training, data quality, or process design need to be improved.

Study instruction:
- "Create an exception register for missing evidence, an overdue transfer, a valuation gap, and a donor-restriction breach. Assign severity, owner, containment, deadline, escalation, and closure evidence."

Key point:
- Exceptions require explicit ownership, time-bound resolution, and documented evidence.
- High-risk incidents must be routed to their designated investigation or safety process rather than suppressed by routine inventory workflows.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Controls and Adjustments
33. Q: How should suspected theft, fraud, safeguarding incidents, contamination, or product recalls be handled?
A: Show answer
These events require prompt referral to the designated incident, security, safety, safeguarding, compliance, or investigation procedure. The inventory team should protect people, contain affected stock, preserve transaction records and other evidence, restrict unauthorized access or movements, and notify the appropriate authorized owners. Potentially affected goods should be identified by location, batch, lot, quantity, condition, and relevant transactions. The team should not destroy, modify, or silently correct evidence to make stock records appear consistent. Finance should assess the potential inventory and financial-reporting implications without taking over an investigation beyond its authority. Donor notification, regulatory reporting, insurance, police referral, or other external communication must follow the applicable legal requirements and authorized organizational process. The inventory record should retain the incident reference and show the stock's controlled disposition. Closure of the accounting entry does not prove that the underlying incident has been fully investigated or resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a response checklist for suspected theft or contaminated supplies, separating immediate containment, evidence preservation, specialist referral, financial assessment, and final closure requirements."

Key point:
- Safety and suspected misconduct require incident controls beyond routine stock adjustment.
- Accounting closure and incident closure are separate decisions that need separate evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory and Physical Verification
34. Q: What inventory indicators should management monitor, and how should these indicators trigger action?
A: Show answer
Management should monitor the percentage of owned inventory value with approved valuation and general-ledger mappings, the number of expired or quarantined units incorrectly released, open count variances by age and estimated value, unexplained inventory-to-GL residuals, overdue transfers, missing ownership or restriction data, unresolved damage and write-off cases, and repeated failed tests or emergency changes. Each indicator needs a defined population, calculation method, source, reporting frequency, owner, target where appropriate, and escalation rule. The organization should not treat a numerical KPI as evidence of compliance unless the underlying data and completion status have been validated. For example, the absence of open count variances may indicate successful resolution, but it could also mean that no count was conducted or that unresolved cases were not entered into the register. Dashboards should distinguish verified results from incomplete or held records and should support management decisions rather than replace review of material exceptions.

Study instruction:
- "Define five inventory KPIs, specify their numerator and denominator where applicable, and identify the evidence that validates each reported result."

Key point:
- KPI definitions must be consistent, reproducible, and linked to controlled evidence.
- Management indicators should trigger specific actions when risk thresholds or control failures are identified.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
35. Q: How should inventory information be presented in financial statements and management reports?
A: Show answer
Inventory reporting should follow the applicable reporting framework and provide a clear account of recognized balances and significant movements. Depending on the framework and reporting requirements, relevant information may include closing balances by inventory class, opening-to-closing reconciliations, purchases or receipts, consumption, impairment, write-downs, write-offs, and significant events or assumptions affecting valuation. Management reports may also need location-level quantities, expiry risks, restricted stock, shortages, open transfers, and unresolved exceptions. The organization must distinguish financial-reporting balances from operational custody records and from donor-specific inventories or distribution reports. The reporting population, period, currency, valuation basis, general-ledger mapping, and consolidation approach should be documented. Totals must reconcile to underlying records and the general ledger. Unverified balances or unresolved differences should remain identified rather than being incorporated into a certified figure without an approved basis. Specific presentation and disclosure requirements must be verified against the applicable accounting framework.

Study instruction:
- "Draft an inventory reporting schedule connecting opening stock, movements, valuation adjustments, closing balances, exceptions, and financial-statement disclosures where applicable."

Key point:
- Inventory disclosures must be supported by reconciled balances and the applicable reporting requirements.
- Operational dashboards and donor schedules supplement rather than automatically replace financial-statement reporting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory
36. Q: How can a weighted-average inventory calculation be checked without treating fictional figures as an actual accounting policy?
A: Show answer
The source SOP provides an illustrative calculation in which the unit cost of a fictional interchangeable item is IDR 1,037.50. At that unit cost, an issue of 80 units has a value of IDR 83,000. A subsequent book quantity of 80 units compared with a physical count of 78 units produces a shortage of two units. At the same illustrative unit cost, the shortage represents IDR 2,075, leaving an illustrative remaining stock value of IDR 80,925 after the adjustment, assuming all necessary accounting and approval requirements are satisfied. These figures demonstrate calculation mechanics only. They do not establish the actual organization's applicable valuation method, recognition policy, materiality threshold, approval, or inventory balance. Before using any calculation, verify the item population, opening quantity and value, accepted receipts, movement records, approved cost formula, decimal precision, unit conversions, and authorization for any adjustment. Independently reproduce the arithmetic and reconcile the result to the controlled source records.

Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the fictional unit-cost, issue-value, shortage-value, and adjusted-closing-value calculations, then list the evidence and approvals needed before a comparable real adjustment could be posted."

Key point:
- Mathematically correct calculations do not prove that the valuation method or underlying data is valid.
- The example's quantities and IDR amounts are fictional and must not be used as real balances or policy parameters.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Measurement
37. Q: How should consignment stock and partner-held goods be recorded and reconciled?
A: Show answer
The organization must determine whether the goods are owned or controlled by the reporting entity or are merely held on behalf of a supplier, partner, donor, or other organization. The underlying contract and actual circumstances should be examined before deciding the accounting treatment. Where goods are third-party-owned and held only for custody, a custody register may be needed to record quantities, locations, conditions, responsible people, restrictions, and movement history without including the items in the organization's recognized inventory balance. Partner-held stock may require additional reporting and confirmation procedures, depending on ownership, grant terms, and the organization's rights and obligations. Physical confirmations and movement records should be obtained at appropriate intervals. Any loss, damage, transfer, or discrepancy should be investigated and referred to the relevant party. The organization must not recognize third-party stock simply because the goods appear in a warehouse or operational spreadsheet.

Study instruction:
- "Compare supplier consignment stock, partner-held program supplies, and organization-owned stock. Document ownership evidence, custody responsibilities, reconciliation requirements, and financial reporting consequences."

Key point:
- Third-party custody must be distinguished from inventory recognized by the reporting entity.
- Ownership and custody records need separate but appropriately reconciled reporting populations.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Recognition and Control
38. Q: How should inventory restrictions and stock records be reconciled with donor and project reporting?
A: Show answer
A donor or project inventory schedule should connect authorized purchases or donations, accepted quantities, relevant valuation information, transfers, issues, distributions, returns, count adjustments, disposals, and closing balances to the applicable project or agreement. Finance should verify that stock quantities and values are linked to valid source transactions, that restrictions are still applicable, and that the reporting allocation is consistent with the organization's approved financial and donor-reporting requirements. Differences between operational distribution quantities and financial balances should be explained rather than forced to match by unsupported entries. In particular, the same cost must not be charged twice, restricted items must not be assigned to an incompatible purpose, and unused goods should not automatically be treated as consumed expenditure. The organization should maintain a record of donor approvals or changes to permitted use where required. Final reports should identify unresolved issues, the responsible owner, and the corrective action needed.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile one donor-funded inventory schedule to purchase records, warehouse movements, approved distributions, closing quantities, and the relevant project-reporting balance."

Key point:
- Donor reporting must connect physical movements, financial balances, and contractual restrictions.
- Differences require explanation and evidence, not forced agreement or duplicate charging.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Life Cycle
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
39. Q: How should missing documentation or conflicting information affect inventory processing?
A: Show answer
The organization should identify which assertion is unresolved: existence, ownership, quantity, condition, valuation, permitted use, authorization, or transaction timing. It should then obtain the relevant source evidence, compare independent records, and determine whether the item or transaction must be held, quarantined, excluded from final reporting, or escalated. For example, a supplier invoice without receiving evidence does not establish that goods arrived, while a warehouse balance without ownership documentation does not establish an asset. If records conflict, preserve both sources and document the investigation rather than silently overwriting the lower-level record. Material uncertainties should be reviewed by the responsible accounting or operational authority, with conclusions and limitations documented. A transaction must not be forced into an approved state solely to meet a reporting deadline. The organization should define escalation thresholds and specify when an unresolved issue prevents stock release, valuation, journal posting, or certification of a report.

Study instruction:
- "Analyze a case with an invoice, an incomplete delivery note, and an inconsistent stock record. Identify the unsupported assertions and establish the minimum evidence needed before processing continues."

Key point:
- Conflicting or missing evidence requires investigation rather than assumption.
- Unresolved material matters must remain visible and may require the affected process or report to be held.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Control Procedures
40. Q: How can Excel and Power Query support inventory records and reconciliations without determining the accounting policy?
A: Show answer
Excel can provide controlled structured tables for item masters, movement records, counts, valuation inputs, exceptions, reconciliations, and reporting. Power Query can import files from designated sources, normalize formats, merge approved reference lists, validate codes, detect candidate duplicates, calculate quantity roll-forwards, and generate exception populations. These tools improve consistency and reduce repetitive copy-and-paste work, but their calculations remain subject to the approved accounting and operational rules. The workbook should record source filenames, reporting periods, refresh dates, source versions, record counts, control totals, and transformation exceptions. Users should compare imported totals with original source files and test that missing or invalid fields are surfaced. The finance team should separately approve valuation methods, recognition criteria, account mappings, and adjustment rules. A successfully refreshed workbook does not prove that its data is complete, that inventory exists, or that the accounting conclusions are correct.

Study instruction:
- "Design a Power Query model that imports warehouse movements, validates item codes and units, reports candidate duplicates, and reconciles control totals to the original files."

Key point:
- Power Query supports data preparation and exception detection; it does not establish accounting validity.
- Refreshed data must be validated against sources and approved business rules.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Accounting and Controls
41. Q: How can VBA or other automation improve inventory workflows while preserving accountability?
A: Show answer
Automation may prepare movement reports, flag invalid stock statuses, create count sheets, compile unresolved discrepancies, prepare draft journals from approved calculation rules, generate recurring reports, and record workflow timestamps. Before deployment, the organization should define approved inputs, validation conditions, prohibited transitions, exception handling, user permissions, and review responsibilities. Macros should not automatically authorize write-offs, override donor restrictions, release expired goods, determine unresolved ownership, or make complex accounting judgments without the required independent decision. Important actions should produce a traceable log showing the action, user where available, date and time, transaction reference, input version, output, and status. Changes to formulas or scripts should be tested and approved before production use. Recovery procedures should address failed refreshes, missing files, incomplete output, unexpected values, and interruption during posting or export. Automation must remain subject to independent review and reconciliation with the actual system of record.

Study instruction:
- "Specify the inputs, controls, exceptions, permissions, audit-log fields, and approval gates for an automated stock reconciliation and discrepancy-reporting workflow."

Key point:
- Automation should enforce approved routine controls and surface exceptions.
- Human authorization and accounting judgment remain necessary wherever the process requires professional or delegated decisions.

42. Q: What negative and regression tests should be completed before inventory controls are released?
A: Show answer
Testing should cover both valid transactions and deliberately invalid cases. Relevant positive cases include accepted receipts, authorized issues, approved unit conversions, complete stock roll-forwards, valid transfers, count reconciliations, and valuation calculations using approved fictional or test data. Negative cases should include duplicate item identifiers, unknown units, missing ownership information, unsupported donor destinations, expired or quarantined stock presented for issue, unapproved write-offs, unconfirmed transfers, incomplete evidence, and unexplained general-ledger differences. Tests should confirm that each prohibited case is rejected for the intended control reason rather than merely failing because of an unrelated earlier error. Historical records and effective-dated changes should remain consistent after updates or migration. Test results must identify the configuration and version tested, expected outcome, actual outcome, defect, remediation, reviewer, and approval status. Local spreadsheet or document checks do not substitute for independent schema validation or actual user-interface testing where those are required by the release criteria.

Study instruction:
- "Build a regression suite covering normal transactions, prohibited cases, valuation calculations, quantity roll-forwards, reconciliation, migration, and audit-trail completeness."

Key point:
- A control should be tested for both successful permitted processing and rejection of prohibited activity.
- Technical checks and accounting-control tests provide different forms of assurance and must be reported separately.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 10 — Inventory Controls
43. Q: How should stock losses, payment issues, and accounting discrepancies be escalated across departments?
A: Show answer
The SOP should specify which team owns each type of issue and the circumstances that require cross-functional escalation. Warehouse personnel may identify a quantity difference, Finance may assess the accounting implications, Procurement may follow up with the supplier, Programs may confirm the intended distribution, and Safety or Compliance may investigate specific incidents. A case should record the evidence gathered, impact assessment, owner, deadline, decisions required, and status. Potentially unauthorized transactions, significant losses, donor breaches, unsafe goods, or unresolved material financial differences should be escalated promptly according to approved authority and incident procedures. No department should close a matter merely because another department has accepted the referral. Closure requires confirmation that the underlying issue has been addressed and that any necessary financial, operational, system, donor, or incident records have been updated. The organization should monitor overdue cases and analyze recurring root causes to improve controls.

Study instruction:
- "Create an escalation matrix that assigns stock discrepancies, donor-restriction breaches, safety incidents, supplier disputes, and unresolved accounting differences to named roles and approval levels."

Key point:
- Cross-functional cases need clear ownership and a single traceable case history.
- Referral is not resolution; the outcome and supporting evidence must be verified before closure.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Control Processes
44. Q: How should an NGO maintain continuity when an inventory custodian, reviewer, or system owner is unavailable?
A: Show answer
The organization should designate approved deputies and document their permitted authority, access, effective dates, and escalation responsibilities. Handover records should identify open transfers, pending inspections, quarantine stock, expiry concerns, uncompleted counts, open discrepancies, outstanding write-off cases, unreconciled balances, and pending approvals. Access should be updated through an authorized procedure rather than by sharing accounts or credentials. A substitute should be able to locate current procedures, source documents, relevant control logs, and approved system instructions without relying solely on the absent person's memory. Material changes, emergency actions, and approvals performed by a deputy should be recorded and independently reviewed when required. Continuity arrangements must preserve segregation of duties and must not grant broader authority than necessary. After the temporary assignment, access and delegated authority should be reviewed and removed or adjusted according to the approved process.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a handover checklist for an absent warehouse manager and finance reviewer, including open stock risks, unresolved transactions, delegated authority, access changes, and subsequent independent review."

Key point:
- Continuity depends on documented processes, current records, and authorized backup responsibilities.
- Emergency delegation must not silently bypass segregation or access controls.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Control Processes
45. Q: What is required before the inventory SOP can be formally adopted and released for operational use?
A: Show answer
The organization should complete a documented implementation and approval process. This includes confirming the legal entity and applicable reporting framework, reviewing current accounting texts and executed agreements, deciding the recognition and valuation policy for relevant inventory classes, verifying donor and supplier requirements, approving the roles and delegation matrix, confirming general-ledger mappings, and validating the operational, safety, access, retention, and incident procedures. The controlled forms, item master, units, stock statuses, movement rules, reconciliation templates, and exception registers should be established in the authorized system. Positive and negative tests should be completed, migration balances independently reconciled, responsible personnel trained, and production behavior verified. The approver should review the final version and evidence that material gaps have been resolved. The source document expressly treats the example as a controlled draft, not an adopted policy. Until the required decisions and approvals are recorded, it should remain a design reference rather than an operational instruction.

- "Complete an adoption checklist and identify the exact evidence needed to demonstrate approval of the framework, valuation rules, responsibilities, controls, system configuration, training, and production readiness."

Key point:
- Formal adoption requires verified applicability, approved policies, tested controls, and authorized release.
- A comprehensive template is not evidence that an organization has adopted or implemented its requirements.
