Table 6
Lease Accounting Controls and Procedures
1. Q: What is the purpose of a comprehensive lease accounting SOP for an international NGO?
A: Show answer
A lease accounting SOP establishes a controlled process for identifying, assessing, recognizing, measuring, recording, reviewing, reporting and monitoring lease arrangements throughout their lifecycle. For an NGO, it should cover offices, warehouses, vehicles, equipment, IT hardware and other potential arrangements that convey rights to use assets. The procedure should connect contract management with procurement, accounts payable, Treasury, the general ledger, asset accounting, grants, tax and financial reporting. Its primary control objectives are completeness of the lease population, evidence-based classification, accurate calculations, appropriate authorization, reliable reconciliations and sufficient supporting documentation. The SOP must distinguish mandatory accounting requirements from internal policy choices and operational recommendations. The supplied document is explicitly a fictional demonstration: its entity, contracts, approvals, calculations, account mappings and test results do not establish an actual organization's policy or compliance. Before adoption, management must determine the applicable reporting framework, verify current authoritative requirements and contract terms, assess local legal and tax obligations, independently validate calculations and obtain formal approval.

Study instruction:
- "Map the entire lease lifecycle from contract identification through financial reporting, including the responsible teams and evidence required at every stage."

Key point:
- The SOP governs a complete, evidence-based process rather than calculations alone.
- Fictional examples must not be treated as approved organizational policy.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
2. Q: Which accounting framework should govern an NGO's lease accounting?
A: Show answer
The organization must first establish its legal entity, reporting perimeter, statutory obligations, contractual commitments and applicable financial reporting framework. Depending on the entity and reporting requirements, the relevant framework might be IPSAS, IFRS Accounting Standards, Indonesian financial reporting standards or another authorized framework. These frameworks must not be treated as interchangeable without an applicability assessment. IPSAS 43 introduces a right-of-use model for lessee accounting and is effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2025, subject to its adoption and transition requirements. The older UN IPSAS Finance Manual contains lease guidance based on the UN's own historical policies and processes; its provisions should not automatically be treated as current IPSAS 43 requirements for a different NGO. IFRS 16 is also relevant only when IFRS applies or when its mechanics are explicitly adopted as an appropriate analytical reference. Finance should document the selected framework, applicable version, effective date, transition requirements, approval authority and consequences for recognition, measurement, presentation and disclosure. If applicability remains unresolved, actual accounting conclusions and production postings should remain on hold.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a framework decision record identifying the legal entity, reporting obligations, applicable lease standard, effective amendments and approval evidence."

Key point:
- Select and document the applicable framework before implementing lease accounting.
- A standard's existence does not establish its applicability to a particular NGO.

Source links:
- IPSASB — IPSAS 43, Leases
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
3. Q: Why must the NGO distinguish current accounting requirements from legacy lease policies in the UN IPSAS Finance Manual?
A: Show answer
The UN IPSAS Finance Manual remains useful for understanding established UN processes, lease lifecycle controls, journal workflows and financial statement disclosures. However, Chapter 7 describes a finance-versus-operating lease classification model based on an earlier IPSAS framework. IPSAS 43 subsequently introduced a right-of-use model for lessees, replacing the former risks-and-rewards model for lessee accounting. Consequently, historical classification indicators, capitalization approaches and UN-specific thresholds must not automatically be applied to an organization reporting under a different or newer framework. Finance should classify each source by authority, purpose, effective period and applicability. Use current standards for operative accounting requirements, the UN manual where its guidance genuinely applies or adds process context, and approved organizational procedures for implementation choices. Where the sources differ, document the conflict, determine which authority governs the reporting entity and obtain an appropriately authorized interpretation. A software workflow inherited from an older manual must also be reviewed rather than copied without validation.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the lease recognition and classification requirements in the applicable current standard with the relevant UN Finance Manual chapter, documenting every material difference."

Key point:
- Historical UN procedures are not automatically current universal accounting rules.
- Resolve framework conflicts before using legacy thresholds or journal models.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
- IPSASB — IPSAS 43, Leases
4. Q: Which lease arrangements should be included in the NGO's lease identification process?
A: Show answer
The identification process should examine both contracts explicitly described as leases and other arrangements that may contain a right to use an asset. Potential sources include signed contracts, amendments, purchase orders, rental agreements, facility records, warehouse arrangements, vehicle agreements, equipment hire, IT infrastructure, communications equipment and recurring vendor payments. Procurement, Facilities, Fleet, IT, programme teams, Legal and Finance may each hold evidence that is missing from the central contract register. The organization should maintain an inventory of source populations and document which records were screened, the screening criteria, the assessment result and unresolved items. Finance must not rely solely on accounts already labelled rent because embedded leases may be recorded under service, equipment, hosting or other expense categories. Conversely, recurring payments do not automatically establish a lease. Each candidate requires a documented assessment under the applicable standard. Potential arrangements with incomplete evidence should remain visible as pending rather than disappearing from the control population.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the central contract register with procurement, accounts payable, facilities, fleet and IT records, then investigate unmatched potential lease arrangements."

Key point:
- Lease completeness begins with identifying arrangements across all relevant business processes.
- Missing contracts and uncertain arrangements require documented follow-up.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
5. Q: What information should be collected when a potential lease contract is received?
A: Show answer
The contract intake record should identify the legal parties, contract reference, approval status, signature date, commencement date, asset description, location, contractual duration, payment terms, renewal and termination options, purchase options, service components, index-linked adjustments, incentives, security deposits and relevant amendments. It should also record the asset's availability for use, contractual rights, restrictions, maintenance responsibilities and any restoration or dismantling obligations. Attach the signed contract and supporting documents, then record the source owner, receipt date, document version and assessment status. The reviewer should be able to trace each conclusion back to the relevant contractual clause or other reliable evidence. Missing terms must be recorded as exceptions, with a responsible owner and due date. Procurement approval or vendor onboarding does not replace the accounting assessment. A contract may be commercially authorized but still require further analysis before classification, measurement or posting.

Study instruction:
- "Complete a lease intake checklist using an actual or training contract and identify which clauses support each required data field."

Key point:
- Contract data must be complete, version-controlled and traceable to supporting evidence.
- Commercial approval does not automatically authorize the accounting conclusion.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
6. Q: How should an NGO distinguish a lease from a service contract?
A: Show answer
The assessment should examine the substance of the arrangement rather than relying on its title or expense account. Under relevant lease standards, a contract contains a lease when it conveys the right to control the use of an identified asset for a period of time in exchange for consideration. The assessment considers whether an asset is explicitly or implicitly identified, whether the supplier has substantive rights to substitute it, whether the customer obtains substantially all economic benefits from use, and whether the customer can direct how and for what purpose the asset is used within the contractual scope. A cleaning contract that delivers a cleaning outcome using a supplier-controlled workforce and equipment may not convey control of an identified asset. A contract for exclusive use of a specified vehicle or office may require a different conclusion. Mixed arrangements may contain both lease and service components. Document the relevant facts, contractual clauses, conclusions and reasons. If evidence is insufficient, use a pending assessment status instead of forcing a conclusion.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a service contract and an asset-use contract, identifying the evidence needed to establish or reject a right to control an identified asset."

Key point:
- The economic substance and control-of-use criteria determine lease assessment.
- A contract label alone is insufficient evidence.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
7. Q: How should the NGO assess whether an arrangement contains an identified asset?
A: Show answer
An identified asset can be expressly specified in a contract or implicitly identified when made available for use. The assessment should examine asset descriptions, serial numbers, location, capacity, contractual schedules, supplier substitution rights and practical operating arrangements. A substitution clause does not necessarily prevent lease accounting if the supplier lacks a substantive economic ability to substitute the asset or would not benefit economically from doing so. When substitution is possible, Finance should determine whether the applicable standard's substantive-substitution requirements are met. The evidence should include relevant contract clauses, confirmation from the asset owner or operations team, and a reasoned assessment of actual operating conditions. For complex infrastructure, fleet, hosting or shared-equipment arrangements, obtain appropriate specialist input. If the asset cannot be identified confidently, the accounting conclusion should remain unresolved until sufficient evidence is obtained.

Study instruction:
- "Use a contract and an asset-use description to document whether an asset is specified, whether substitution rights are substantive and how the conclusion was reached."

Key point:
- Asset identification depends on enforceable terms and substantive rights, not just asset labels.
- Unresolved substitution questions require additional evidence or technical review.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
8. Q: How should an NGO assess whether it controls the use of an identified asset?
A: Show answer
Control of use generally requires the customer to obtain substantially all economic benefits from using the identified asset and to have the right to direct its use within the contract's scope. The assessment should cover permitted uses, capacity rights, exclusivity, operating decisions, restrictions imposed by the supplier and the party that determines how and for what purpose the asset is used. Protective rights intended to safeguard the supplier's asset do not necessarily prevent the customer from controlling its use. For shared office space, warehouse capacity, vehicles or equipment, Finance should establish the scope of the identified asset and the rights conveyed. Evidence may include contract clauses, operating schedules, access rights, asset allocation records and explanations from the business owner. The final conclusion should be consistent with the contract and actual operating conditions. Unclear or conflicting evidence should be escalated rather than resolved by selecting the most convenient accounting outcome.

Study instruction:
- "Document the economic benefits, decision-making rights and contractual restrictions for a potential lease and explain how they affect the assessment."

Key point:
- Control depends on the rights conveyed over the asset's use.
- Operational facts and contractual terms must support the same conclusion.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
9. Q: How should lease and non-lease components be separated?
A: Show answer
A contract may combine the right to use an asset with services such as cleaning, maintenance, security, equipment support or other operational assistance. Finance should identify the components, determine the consideration attributable to each under the applicable standard and allocation policy, and document the basis used. Contractual line items can provide useful evidence, but the stated price allocation may require further examination if it does not reflect the underlying components. Where a framework permits a practical expedient to combine components for a defined class of underlying assets, the organization must document the election and apply it consistently within the permitted scope. Payment amounts should not be separated arbitrarily merely to achieve a desired liability. Service costs may also affect expense coding, project allocation and donor reporting. The lease calculation, service expense schedule and payment reconciliation should therefore share traceable contract references and consistent component definitions.

Study instruction:
- "Take a property or equipment agreement containing rent and maintenance, identify the components, calculate the proposed allocation and obtain independent review."

Key point:
- Component allocation affects liability measurement and expense classification.
- Accounting elections must be permitted, documented and consistently applied.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
10. Q: How should the lease term be determined when renewal or termination options exist?
A: Show answer
The lease term must be determined using the applicable standard's requirements, including the enforceable period and the treatment of extension or termination options. Under IFRS 16-style mechanics, the assessment includes periods covered by an extension option when the lessee is reasonably certain to exercise it, and periods covered by a termination option when the lessee is reasonably certain not to exercise it. The evaluation should consider contractual rights, expected operational use, relocation costs, asset specialization, economic incentives, availability of replacement facilities and other relevant facts. Management should not automatically assume that the stated non-cancellable period is the complete lease term. Equally, renewal should not be included solely because it seems commercially convenient. Record the options, supporting evidence, conclusion, approving authority and reassessment triggers. Significant changes in circumstances may require reassessment under the applicable framework.

Study instruction:
- "Assess a lease with renewal and termination options, documenting the enforceable period, relevant economic incentives and rationale for the selected lease term."

Key point:
- Option judgments can materially affect lease measurement and reporting.
- Significant changes may trigger reassessment under the applicable standard.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
11. Q: Which lease exemptions and practical expedients may be available to a lessee?
A: Show answer
Available elections depend on the applicable accounting framework and its specific conditions. Under IFRS 16, a lessee may elect not to apply the recognition requirements to qualifying short-term leases and leases of low-value underlying assets. Short-term treatment generally depends on the lease term at commencement and the relevant standard's requirements; low-value eligibility must be assessed using the required basis rather than the NGO's purchase budget or donor threshold. These treatments are not automatic exemptions for every small payment or short contract. The organization should document eligible contract classes, the accounting policy election, the assessment evidence, the approval and the required expense treatment. Similar-looking arrangements must be treated consistently where the framework requires consistency. The source document's example of a ten-month printer arrangement illustrates a possible short-term candidate only under its fictional assumptions. It does not establish that the exemption applies to a real printer lease or that every framework provides identical elections.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a short-term arrangement and a low-value asset arrangement separately, listing the eligibility criteria, supporting evidence and required policy approvals."

Key point:
- Exemptions require eligibility and documented elections where applicable.
- A nominal price or a low budget value does not independently prove eligibility.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
12. Q: How should initial lease recognition differ under modern lessee accounting and older lease-classification models?
A: Show answer
Under an applicable right-of-use model such as IFRS 16 or IPSAS 43, a lessee generally recognizes a right-of-use asset and lease liability at commencement for leases within the standard's recognition requirements, subject to available exemptions and transition provisions. This replaces the former general approach under which operating lease payments were expensed over the lease term while finance leases were capitalized. The older UN IPSAS Finance Manual describes historical UN policies that classified leases as finance or operating using risks-and-rewards indicators. That historical model must not be transferred automatically into a current right-of-use accounting process. If the NGO applies another framework, Finance must follow that framework's recognition rules. The accounting policy record should state which model applies, its effective date, transition method and approval evidence. Procedures, spreadsheets, account mappings and disclosures must align with the selected model rather than combining elements from incompatible standards.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a comparison of initial lessee accounting under IPSAS 43 or IFRS 16 and the historical approach described in the UN Finance Manual."

Key point:
- The selected standard determines whether and how lease assets and liabilities are recognized.
- Do not combine old operating-lease accounting mechanically with a right-of-use model.

Source links:
- IPSASB — IPSAS 43, Leases
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
13. Q: How is the initial lease liability measured under a right-of-use model?
A: Show answer
Under IFRS 16-style lessee accounting, the initial lease liability is generally measured at the present value of lease payments that are unpaid at commencement. The payment population is determined by the applicable standard and may include fixed payments, payments in substance fixed, qualifying index- or rate-linked amounts measured on the required basis, and amounts related to specified options or residual value guarantees. The discount rate is generally the interest rate implicit in the lease when readily determinable; otherwise, the lessee's incremental borrowing rate is used under the standard. Payments made at or before commencement affect the right-of-use asset measurement rather than remaining as unpaid liability. Each cash flow should be linked to a contractual clause, amount, currency, payment date and component assessment. Finance should validate the timing conventions and present-value formula independently. The calculation must remain provisional until the contract facts, rate, framework and required approvals have been verified.

Study instruction:
- "Build a contractual cash-flow schedule and independently calculate the present value of unpaid qualifying lease payments using an approved rate and timing convention."

Key point:
- Liability measurement requires supported cash flows and an appropriate discount rate.
- The calculation must reflect the applicable standard's definitions of lease payments.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
14. Q: How should the initial right-of-use asset be measured?
A: Show answer
Under IFRS 16-style mechanics, the initial right-of-use asset generally starts with the initial lease liability and is adjusted for lease payments made at or before commencement, lease incentives received, qualifying initial direct costs and estimated costs of dismantling, removing or restoring the underlying asset or site when the applicable requirements are met. Amounts already expensed or recognized as separate assets or liabilities must not be added again without a valid basis. The asset measurement should be supported by a clear bridge showing every component and its documentary source. Finance must identify restoration obligations, incentives and prepayments during contract intake rather than discovering them after posting. The subsequent depreciation method and useful life must follow the applicable standard and approved asset policy. The accounting treatment for arrangements without consideration, including donated rights to use, may differ and should not simply be forced into a commercial lease formula without examining the relevant framework and facts.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a right-of-use asset bridge from the initial liability, listing every adjustment and the evidence supporting each amount."

Key point:
- ROU asset cost is not always equal to the initial lease liability.
- Each adjustment must be authorized, supported and protected against double counting.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
15. Q: How should the discount rate for a lease be selected and approved?
A: Show answer
The rate-selection process must follow the applicable standard's requirements. Under IFRS 16, use the interest rate implicit in the lease if it can be readily determined; otherwise, use the lessee's incremental borrowing rate at the required measurement date. An incremental borrowing rate should reflect relevant factors such as currency, term, security, economic environment and the financing characteristics required by the applicable guidance. An unrelated borrowing rate, a generic corporate rate or an unsupported management estimate is not sufficient merely because it produces a convenient answer. The rate file should record the source, observation date, currency, term, methodology, adjustments, preparer, reviewer, approval and version. Rates must be compared with the contractual cash-flow dates and lease term. Materially changed inputs require recalculation and assessment of whether reassessment or remeasurement rules apply. A stale rate must not be silently carried into a newly measured lease.

Study instruction:
- "Document an approved lease discount rate and demonstrate how currency, term, market evidence and measurement date affect its selection."

Key point:
- The chosen rate requires a documented technical basis and independent review.
- Rate changes should be treated under the applicable measurement and reassessment rules.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16 Supporting Implementation Materials
16. Q: How should payment timing and cash-flow assumptions be validated before calculating a lease liability?
A: Show answer
The payment schedule should reproduce the contractual obligations, including amounts, currency, due dates, payment frequency, payment-in-advance arrangements, incentives, escalation clauses and any other qualifying cash flows. Distinguish contractual payments from refundable deposits, service costs and payments already made. Where the contract links payments to an index or rate, apply the measurement rule required by the relevant standard. Irregular payment dates must not be forced into a regular monthly-annuity formula without a justified convention. Each row should have a unique payment reference and a link to the contract clause or supporting document. Reconcile the total undiscounted cash flows to the contractual payment schedule before discounting. Finance should independently test representative periods, unusual payment patterns and final instalments. If an invoice conflicts with the contract, determine whether it reflects an authorized amendment, separate service, error or unsupported charge before including it in the lease measurement.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a lease with irregular payments and compare the total contractual cash flows with the payment schedule used in the measurement worksheet."

Key point:
- Payment completeness and timing must be validated before present-value calculations.
- Unsupported or disputed amounts require investigation rather than automatic inclusion.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
17. Q: What journal entries are normally required when a lessee recognizes a lease under a right-of-use model?
A: Show answer
At commencement, an illustrative entry may debit the right-of-use asset and credit the lease liability, with appropriate adjustments for qualifying prepayments, incentives, direct costs and restoration obligations. Subsequent entries generally recognize depreciation of the ROU asset, interest expense on the lease liability and reduction of the liability when payments are made. Additional entries may be required for reassessment, remeasurement, modifications, impairment, termination or derecognition. These are conceptual entries, not a prescribed chart of accounts. The actual account numbers, project codes, donor codes, cost centers and presentation must follow the selected framework and the organization's approved accounting manual. Separate the measurement worksheet from the journal preparation and posting workflow. The preparer must attach the calculation and contract references; an independent reviewer should confirm the accounting conclusion, amounts, period and balance. A balanced journal does not establish that the lease was classified or measured correctly.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare illustrative commencement, monthly depreciation, interest and payment entries, then trace them to the supporting schedule and general ledger."

Key point:
- Journal entries should reflect approved measurements and the applicable accounting framework.
- Mathematical balance alone is not proof of correct lease accounting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
18. Q: How should lease liabilities and right-of-use assets be accounted for after commencement?
A: Show answer
For a lessee applying a right-of-use model, the liability is generally updated for interest, qualifying payments and applicable remeasurement events. The ROU asset is generally depreciated over the applicable period and is subject to relevant impairment and measurement requirements. Under IFRS 16-style mechanics, interest is recognized so that the outstanding liability reflects the passage of time, while payments reduce the liability after the relevant finance effect is accounted for. The ROU asset's depreciation period depends on the applicable standard, lease term, useful life and any qualifying transfer of ownership or purchase option. Maintain separate movement schedules for the asset and liability. Reconcile their opening balances, additions, depreciation, interest, cash payments, remeasurements, impairments and closing balances to the general ledger. Any difference must be explained and resolved before financial reporting is approved.

Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct a lease liability and ROU asset roll-forward for one reporting period and reconcile each movement to its journal and supporting evidence."

Key point:
- Asset depreciation and liability amortization are separate accounting processes.
- Closing balances should reconcile to approved schedules and the general ledger.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
19. Q: How should foreign-currency lease contracts be measured and monitored?
A: Show answer
For leases denominated in a currency different from the organization's functional currency, Finance should distinguish the initial measurement of contractual cash flows from subsequent foreign-currency accounting. The lease schedule should preserve the contractual currency, original amounts, payment dates, selected exchange-rate sources and the rate applied at initial recognition. Subsequently, the lease liability may be a foreign-currency monetary item subject to the applicable foreign-exchange requirements, while the right-of-use asset's treatment may differ because it is generally a non-monetary asset carried under its applicable measurement basis. The actual treatment must be confirmed under the organization's reporting framework. Do not apply a single blanket translation rule to both balances without analysis. Reconcile exchange differences, payments and closing balances and document the rates, source dates, calculations and account mapping used.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a foreign-currency lease example and explain the separate accounting considerations for the liability, ROU asset, cash payment and exchange difference."

Key point:
- Foreign-currency liability and ROU asset accounting may differ.
- Currency, rate source and reporting-framework requirements must be documented.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
20. Q: What should happen when a lease's terms, payments or duration change after commencement?
A: Show answer
Every amendment or reassessment trigger should be recorded and evaluated under the applicable standard. Relevant events include changes in lease scope, additional space or equipment, extension or termination decisions, changes in contractual payments, index-linked changes, purchase-option decisions and changes in the enforceable lease period. The accounting consequence depends on the nature of the event; it may require reassessing the lease term, remeasuring the liability, adjusting the ROU asset or accounting for a modification separately. Do not treat every invoice variance as a lease modification. Compare the signed amendment with the existing contract and determine whether the change creates enforceable rights and obligations. Preserve the original calculations and journal history, version the revised schedule, identify the effective date, document the assessment and obtain independent approval before posting the change.

Study instruction:
- "Use an amended lease contract to distinguish a contractual modification from an ordinary payment variance and identify the required accounting assessment."

Key point:
- Different types of lease changes can produce different accounting outcomes.
- Historical measurements must remain traceable after remeasurement.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
21. Q: How should index-linked rent changes and discount-rate changes be handled?
A: Show answer
Index-linked payments and discount-rate revisions should be assessed using the specific requirements of the applicable standard. A contractual rent increase linked to a consumer price index, benchmark rate or other variable may require remeasurement when the relevant event occurs. The applicable standard determines when revised cash flows are measured and whether the discount rate is updated. This is different from routinely replacing the original discount rate whenever market rates change. Finance should record the original contractual basis, index or reference rate, observation date, revised payment amount, effective date, required measurement rate and documented calculation. An independent reviewer must verify whether the trigger occurred, whether the cash flows were calculated correctly and whether the right accounting treatment was selected. Unapproved changes to rates or assumptions must not flow automatically into a posted journal.

Study instruction:
- "Model an index-linked rent increase and separately test whether a change in market borrowing rates alone triggers remeasurement under the applicable standard."

Key point:
- Remeasurement depends on the contractual event and the applicable standard.
- Changes in market rates do not automatically justify revising every existing lease liability.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
22. Q: How should lease termination, expiry and derecognition be controlled?
A: Show answer
The organization should identify lease expiry dates, termination rights, notice periods, asset return requirements, restoration obligations and any final settlement amounts. When a lease terminates early or expires, Finance must determine the required accounting under the applicable standard, including derecognition of the relevant liability and ROU asset, settlement of outstanding obligations and recognition of any resulting gain or loss where required. Do not close the register merely because a final invoice has been paid. Obtain evidence that the contractual right has ended, the asset has been returned or otherwise disposed of as required, outstanding charges have been assessed and the accounting has been completed. Facilities, Fleet, Procurement and Accounts Payable should provide closure evidence to Finance. The lease register, asset register, payment workflow and general ledger must be updated consistently while preserving the original contract and accounting history.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one expired or terminated lease from the contract notice through asset return, final invoice, journal entries and register closure."

Key point:
- Contract expiry, asset return and accounting derecognition are related but distinct controls.
- Closure requires documented evidence and reconciliation.

Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
23. Q: How should lease accounting address impairment and other specialist situations?
A: Show answer
Changes in asset use, damage, vacancy, programme closure, relocation or other adverse circumstances may indicate that the ROU asset requires impairment assessment under the applicable framework. Finance should document the triggering event, affected asset, financial implications, applicable impairment requirements and whether specialist valuation or accounting advice is needed. Other arrangements, such as subleases, lessor accounting, sale-and-leaseback transactions, unusual concession agreements and donated rights to use, may require separate technical analysis. They should not be forced into a standard lessee workflow simply because the underlying transaction relates to property or equipment. Record the issue, owner, assessment status, supporting evidence and approval outcome. If the relevant standard or contractual facts are not understood sufficiently, place the accounting conclusion on hold and refer the case to a competent specialist. The routine process can continue for unaffected leases while the specialist issue remains unresolved.

Study instruction:
- "Identify circumstances that may trigger impairment or specialist review and document the evidence needed before reaching a conclusion."

Key point:
- Lease assets may require assessments beyond routine depreciation.
- Specialist arrangements must follow their applicable standards and evidence requirements.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
24. Q: How should donated rights to use property or equipment be assessed?
A: Show answer
An NGO may receive the right to occupy premises or use equipment without payment or for a nominal amount. Finance must determine the substance of the arrangement, the rights granted, the applicable standard, whether consideration is involved, the extent of control, and whether a non-exchange transaction or other accounting requirements affect recognition and measurement. A donated right to use is not automatically equivalent to a conventional commercial lease, and the absence of a cash payment does not by itself prove that there is no accounting impact. The older UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides guidance for certain UN donated-right-to-use arrangements, including specific treatment and historical thresholds. Those policies are entity-specific and may require reassessment against current requirements before use elsewhere. Document the donor agreement, asset-use rights, valuation basis where needed, duration, restrictions, approval and relevant reporting consequences. Tax treatment and donor eligibility must be assessed separately from financial accounting recognition.

Study instruction:
- "Review a donated office-use agreement and distinguish the accounting assessment, legal rights, donor restrictions and tax questions."

Key point:
- Donated rights may have accounting implications even when cash payments are absent.
- UN-specific historical policies must not be applied automatically to another entity.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
25. Q: How should the accounting treatment of donated premises be distinguished from the treatment of an ordinary lease?
A: Show answer
An ordinary commercial lease typically involves contractual payments in exchange for a right to use an asset. A donated right-to-use arrangement may involve no consideration or only nominal consideration and may be governed by different recognition requirements depending on the reporting framework and the substance of the arrangement. Finance should establish whether the agreement grants an enforceable right, what rights and restrictions apply, whether control transfers and how the applicable standard treats the transaction. The legal ownership of the underlying asset should not be confused with the right to use it. Similarly, the label "donation" does not independently establish how revenue, an asset or any related liability should be recognized. The accounting conclusion must be supported by current authoritative guidance, contract terms and approved organizational policy. Record donor reporting and tax consequences separately and ensure that any valuation or measurement is independently reviewed.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a paid office lease with a donor-provided office-use arrangement and identify which facts could change recognition, measurement and reporting."

Key point:
- Similar operational use does not guarantee identical accounting treatment.
- Rights, consideration, framework requirements and donor terms must all be evaluated.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
26. Q: How should the organization distinguish historical capitalization thresholds from current lease recognition requirements?
A: Show answer
A threshold may arise from an accounting standard, an approved capital asset policy, an entity-specific manual or a historical implementation procedure. These sources serve different purposes. The UN Finance Manual, for example, describes historical thresholds for recognizing certain donated right-to-use revenue and expenses within the UN's reporting model. Such thresholds are not automatically general capitalization thresholds for every NGO or an exemption from lease recognition under a different standard. Finance should identify the threshold's source, scope, effective period, eligible asset class and approval. It must then determine whether the current framework actually permits the threshold to affect the accounting decision. An asset's purchase budget, the amount of a lease instalment and a capitalization threshold should not be confused. Maintain a controlled register of thresholds and elections, and reassess it whenever the framework, policy or applicable requirements change.

Study instruction:
- "Identify each threshold in a lease accounting reference and document whether it is a recognition requirement, an entity-specific policy or a historical operational parameter."

Key point:
- Thresholds must be applied only within their authorized scope.
- Historical UN-specific thresholds are not universal accounting rules.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 7 — Leases
- IPSASB — IPSAS 43, Leases
27. Q: How should lease arrangements be reconciled between the lease register, general ledger and asset register?
A: Show answer
The lease register should provide the controlled operational record of each assessed arrangement, including contract references, accounting treatment, commencement, duration, payment schedule, discount-rate information, ROU asset movements, liability movements and closure status. Its totals should reconcile to the general ledger, while relevant asset details should reconcile to the asset register. Differences may arise from new contracts not recorded, incorrect account mapping, missing depreciation, payments recorded against the wrong lease, unprocessed remeasurements, foreign-exchange movements or incorrect closure. Finance should compare both the number of records and the monetary balances. Reconciliation items should include the amount, cause, risk, responsible person, due date and evidence of resolution. No difference should be cleared solely through an unsupported journal. Reconciliation should be performed at the required reporting frequency, independently reviewed and retained in the reporting archive.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile the lease register against the general ledger and asset register, tracing a sample of differences to their root cause and approved correction."

Key point:
- Separate systems must present consistent, explainable lease information.
- Unexplained differences require investigation and documented closure.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 8 — Property, Plant and Equipment
28. Q: What information should be disclosed about lease arrangements in the financial statements?
A: Show answer
Lease disclosures depend on the applicable reporting standard and the nature of the organization's arrangements. Under relevant lease requirements, disclosures may include recognized ROU assets by underlying asset class, depreciation, lease liabilities, interest expense, cash payments, additions and other measurement movements. Additional information may be needed about material judgments, lease term assumptions, extension options, variable payments, restrictions, future commitments and the organization's exposure to lease-related cash-flow risks. An organization that acts as a lessor may have different income, asset, receivable and maturity disclosure requirements from a lessee. Donated rights to use can also require specific treatment under the relevant framework. The disclosure process should reconcile the notes to the ledger and underlying schedules, compare prior-year figures, explain material changes and document reviewer approval. Examples from the UN Finance Manual are useful only to the extent that their requirements and reporting model apply to the organization concerned.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a lease disclosure checklist using the applicable standard and reconcile each quantitative disclosure to an approved supporting schedule."

Key point:
- Disclosure requirements depend on the framework and the organization's role in the arrangement.
- Every material reported figure should be supported and reconciled.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
- IFRS Foundation — IFRS 16, Leases
29. Q: How should lease commitments and future cash payments be presented and monitored?
A: Show answer
Finance should maintain a contractual payment schedule that identifies future payments by period, currency, lease and relevant payment category. Depending on the applicable framework, required disclosures may include lease liabilities, contractual commitments or maturity analyses. The organization must determine which amounts are already recognized as liabilities and which represent additional commitments requiring separate treatment or disclosure. Undiscounted contractual payments should not be presented as though they were identical to the discounted carrying amount of a lease liability. Schedule changes should be supported by signed amendments, permitted index changes or other documented events. At closing, compare the payment schedule with the lease register, accounts payable, cash records and approved disclosure calculations. Investigate contracts missing from the schedule and payments outside the expected pattern. Document the period covered, assumptions, source records and reconciliation results so users can understand both recognized obligations and relevant future commitments.

Study instruction:
- "Compare undiscounted contractual lease payments with the recognized liability and identify the applicable disclosure requirements for each figure."

Key point:
- Contractual cash flows and recognized lease liabilities are different measurement concepts.
- Future payment disclosures must reconcile to controlled source schedules.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
30. Q: How should lease accounting be coordinated with tax, donor, grant and budget requirements?
A: Show answer
Financial accounting, tax treatment, donor eligibility and budget monitoring are separate determinations that may use different definitions and timing rules. A lease liability or ROU asset recognized for financial reporting does not automatically establish a deductible tax expense or an allowable donor charge. Likewise, the existence of a contract does not mean every payment is eligible for reimbursement under a grant. Finance should review the applicable local tax law, donor agreement, grant budget, procurement requirements and organizational accounting policy. Record any differences in the appropriate schedules and explain them in reporting or reconciliation workpapers where material. Tax withholding, indirect taxes, non-creditable taxes, restoration obligations and donor cost-allocation rules should be assessed by the responsible specialists. Require documented approval where donor authorization or a formal tax determination is needed. A lease should not be charged to a restricted grant solely because the programme uses the asset.

Study instruction:
- "Select one lease charged to a donor-funded project and prepare separate conclusions for financial accounting, tax compliance, grant eligibility and budget treatment."

Key point:
- Financial accounting recognition does not determine tax deductibility or donor eligibility.
- Each reporting obligation must be assessed against its own governing requirements.

Source links:
- Directorate General of Taxes — Income Tax on Rental of Land and/or Buildings
31. Q: How should responsibilities and segregation of duties be designed for lease accounting?
A: Show answer
The organization should distinguish contract initiation, commercial approval, lease assessment, calculation preparation, independent review, journal authorization, payment execution, disclosure preparation and records custody. The exact division depends on staffing and system access. Procurement or Legal should provide contractual evidence, the lease accountant should prepare the assessment and schedules, an independent reviewer should validate material judgments and calculations, and an authorized approver should approve the accounting outcome and posting. Treasury should execute payments under its own authorization rules. Where staffing limitations prevent full segregation, compensating controls such as independent post-payment review, documented managerial review and periodic access checks should be established. Consultants may provide technical assistance, but the organization must retain appropriate ownership and decision authority. Maintain a responsibility matrix including deputies, escalation routes and access rights. Approval evidence should identify the exact version reviewed and must not be inferred from a verbal instruction.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a RACI matrix for lease intake, assessment, measurement, approval, posting, payment and disclosure, then identify incompatible duties."

Key point:
- Preparation, review, approval and payment should not be controlled by one person without adequate safeguards.
- Delegated work does not remove organizational accountability.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
32. Q: What controls should exist over lease journals and general-ledger mappings?
A: Show answer
Establish approved account mappings for ROU assets, lease liabilities, depreciation, interest expense, payments, modifications and other relevant movements. Account descriptions, project codes, donor codes, currencies and cost centers should follow the organization's chart of accounts and reporting requirements. Journal templates must be linked to approved calculation schedules and should identify the contract, accounting period, journal reference and preparer. Before posting, verify debit-credit balance, period, account classification, project allocation, approval status and supporting documents. Automated journal preparation must not bypass authorization. A change to an account mapping or formula should be version-controlled and tested on representative cases before release. Review unexpected movements, unusual manual postings and entries made after the normal close timetable. After posting, reconcile the lease register and ledger and retain evidence of both preparation and independent review.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a lease journal from the approved measurement worksheet through the account mapping, authorization, ledger posting and final reconciliation."

Key point:
- Approved mapping and independent review reduce classification and posting errors.
- Journal automation must preserve authorization and audit evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
33. Q: How should lease invoices and payments be reconciled to contractual obligations?
A: Show answer
Each invoice should be compared with the contract, payment schedule, approved amendments, service components, tax treatment and any other relevant contractual terms. Reconcile invoiced amounts to scheduled obligations, then reconcile the amounts due to payment records and the general ledger. Differences may arise from index-linked adjustments, partial periods, separate service charges, deposits, disputed invoices, foreign-exchange movements or unauthorized billing. The source document illustrates an invoice of IDR 10,500,000 against a fictional contract amount of IDR 10,000,000, with an unsupported service line suspected. Such a difference should be investigated rather than accepted automatically. Obtain the contractual basis for the additional amount, determine whether it is a valid amendment or separate service, and secure approval before changing the accounting schedule or payment. Track unresolved items in an exception register with a responsible owner, due date and resolution evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate an invoice that exceeds a lease schedule, reconcile the difference to contract evidence and document the approved payment or correction decision."

Key point:
- An invoice does not independently authorize a change to lease accounting.
- Contractual differences must be resolved and evidenced before closure.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
34. Q: Which risks and common errors should the lease accounting control framework address?
A: Show answer
Key risks include omitted contracts, incorrect lease-versus-service assessments, unsupported renewal assumptions, invalid exemptions, inaccurate discount rates, incomplete payment schedules, duplicate service charges, incorrect journal mappings and missing disclosure information. Other errors include using historical thresholds without verifying applicability, failing to record amendments, overlooking restoration obligations, treating a paid invoice as proof of lease closure and allowing unapproved spreadsheet changes to affect posted balances. Controls should combine preventive activities, such as mandatory intake forms and approved master data, with detective checks, such as population reconciliation, independent calculation review and exception reporting. Corrective controls should assign clear owners, due dates, escalation procedures and retesting requirements. Significant risks should be prioritized by potential financial effect, compliance exposure, recurrence and reporting deadlines. Repeated differences should trigger root-cause analysis rather than repeated manual adjustments.

Study instruction:
- "Create a lease accounting risk-and-control matrix that connects each material risk to a preventive control, detective control, responsible owner and closure evidence."

Key point:
- Controls must address actual failure modes throughout the lease lifecycle.
- Repeated exceptions require root-cause correction and independent retesting.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
35. Q: How can Excel and Power Query support the lease register and contract population reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Excel can maintain structured registers for contracts, assessment outcomes, rates, payment schedules, asset movements, liabilities, journal references and review status. Power Query can import data from approved source folders and transform procurement, accounts payable, payment, asset and general-ledger files into a consistent structure. Normalization can standardize dates, currencies, supplier names, contract references and account codes. The workflow can flag missing contract IDs, possible duplicates, expired agreements, unmatched payments and unassessed arrangements. Control totals and record counts should be compared before and after every material transformation. Power Query is a data transformation tool, not an accounting authority: its output must still be assessed under the applicable framework and verified against source evidence. Source paths, refresh dates, query versions, input files and rejected records should be documented to support reproducibility and troubleshooting.

Study instruction:
- "Build a Power Query process that combines contract and ledger populations, records control totals and produces an exception list for independent review."

Key point:
- Power Query can improve data consistency and completeness testing.
- Automated transformations must remain traceable to approved source records.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
36. Q: How can Excel formulas or VBA automate lease measurement without compromising accounting control?
A: Show answer
A controlled workbook can calculate present values, payment roll-forwards, interest, depreciation, ROU movements and proposed journal entries from approved inputs. Excel formulas or VBA should implement a documented calculation method, not independently determine whether a contract is a lease or which standard applies. The workbook should identify source versions, approved discount rates, cash-flow dates, calculation conventions, rounding rules and relevant policy elections. Protect calculation cells and separate inputs from generated outputs. Before release, test representative cases, irregular payments, missing inputs, incorrect currencies, expired rates, amendments and boundary conditions. Compare outputs with independently recalculated results and preserve test evidence. Any material formula, rate or mapping change should receive review and authorization before production use. Macros must include error handling, access controls and execution logs where appropriate. The software should block or clearly flag incomplete evidence and unresolved approval gates instead of generating a misleading final journal.

Study instruction:
- "Independently recalculate several lease measurement examples and test whether the workbook rejects incomplete, inconsistent or unapproved inputs."

Key point:
- Automation improves consistency but does not establish accounting validity.
- Calculations, assumptions and changes must remain reviewable and reproducible.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
37. Q: How should the NGO control changes to its lease accounting workbook, calculation model and system configuration?
A: Show answer
Changes to workbook formulas, discount-rate logic, journal mappings, accounting policies, access roles and system integrations should be subject to formal change control. The process should record the change request, reason, affected standards or policies, source version, owner, reviewer, required testing, approval, implementation date and rollback procedure. Test the revised logic with representative existing cases and negative scenarios before implementation. Preserve the old version and its calculations so historical postings can be reconstructed. After deployment, perform a post-implementation check to confirm that the intended changes are active and that unaffected processes still operate correctly. Unauthorized edits to critical formulas or configuration should be treated as control incidents. Spreadsheet outputs should identify their version and reporting date so reviewers can establish which calculation model generated a journal or disclosure.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a change to the discount-rate formula and prepare the complete change request, test evidence, approvals, release record and rollback plan."

Key point:
- Significant calculation and configuration changes require controlled testing and approval.
- Historical outputs must remain reproducible under the version that originally generated them.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA Reference
38. Q: How should exceptions be escalated and closed in lease accounting?
A: Show answer
Every material exception should have a unique identifier, detection date, affected lease, reporting period, financial impact, risk rating, owner, due date, containment action, escalation route and status. Examples include missing contract evidence, unresolved control-of-use questions, unsupported discount rates, incomplete amendments, unapproved invoices, calculation failures, unreconciled balances and disclosure differences. The owner should investigate the root cause and propose a correction with supporting documents. A reviewer independent of the preparer should verify that the correction is appropriate and that dependent schedules, journals, reports and disclosure figures have been updated. Closing an exception requires evidence that the issue has been resolved and that required retesting has passed. A case must not be marked closed merely because it has moved into the next reporting period or because a balancing journal has been posted. Repeated exceptions should be escalated for process redesign or additional training.

Study instruction:
- "Complete an exception record from initial detection to root-cause analysis, approved correction, independent retesting and documented closure."

Key point:
- Every exception needs an accountable owner, deadline and evidence-based closure decision.
- Independent retesting helps prevent unresolved issues from recurring.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
39. Q: How should lease records, approvals and audit evidence be retained?
A: Show answer
Retain the signed contract, amendments, lease assessment, framework and policy decisions, discount-rate evidence, measurement worksheets, payment schedules, journal approvals, posting references, reconciliation records, disclosure calculations and termination evidence. Each record should have a stable identifier, responsible custodian, repository location, access classification, version, relevant reporting period and retention rule. The organization must determine the retention period from applicable law, audit requirements, donor agreements, contracts and internal policies rather than applying an invented universal duration. Access should follow least-privilege principles, and sensitive contractual or financial information should be restricted appropriately. Backups must be tested through actual recovery procedures. Legal holds and preservation requests must take precedence over routine disposal. An auditor or reviewer should be able to navigate from a reported balance to the related calculation, journal, contract and supporting approval without relying solely on an individual's memory.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one material lease balance from the financial statements to its ledger entry, calculation, contract, approvals and archive index."

Key point:
- An auditable lease process requires complete, retrievable and version-controlled evidence.
- Retention and access rules must follow applicable requirements.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
40. Q: How should an NGO test whether a lease accounting SOP is operationally ready for adoption?
A: Show answer
Readiness requires more than a complete document, a balanced sample calculation or a successful spreadsheet execution. The organization should confirm the applicable framework, verify current authorities, approve relevant policy elections, validate procedures and account mappings, assign competent owners, establish access permissions and confirm that supporting forms can be completed in practice. Walk through representative arrangements from different asset classes and test ordinary transactions, incomplete contracts, service arrangements, amendments, payment differences and termination cases. Independently recalculate material measurements, reconcile outputs to the test ledger and review disclosure results. Structural document checks should be distinguished from genuine independent schema validation, application-level opening and saving, and page-by-page visual quality assurance. Uncompleted external tests must be marked unverified rather than passed. Record defects, owners, deadlines and retesting results. Only authorized management should approve adoption after material open issues are resolved and the completed control evidence is retained.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an adoption checklist covering framework approval, source verification, user acceptance testing, calculation validation, document quality assurance, exception closure and management sign-off."

Key point:
- Readiness requires verified accounting applicability, tested procedures and authorized approval.
- Untested or unresolved controls must be disclosed rather than described as successful.

Source links:
- IPSASB — IPSAS 43, Leases
