Table 7
Receivables, Financial Assets and Impairment Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an SOP governing receivables, financial assets, and impairment?
A: Show answer
The purpose of this SOP is to establish a controlled and evidence-based process for identifying, recognizing, classifying, measuring, monitoring, impairing, writing off, recovering, and reporting receivables and other financial assets. The process should ensure that recorded balances represent valid rights, that impairment estimates reflect the applicable accounting framework, and that material judgments receive independent review and authorized approval. For an NGO, the process must also consider donor reimbursement arrangements, implementing-partner claims, staff loans, recoverable advances, supplier overpayments, and bank deposits. These balances have different underlying rights and therefore cannot automatically follow the same accounting treatment. The source document is explicitly a fictional demonstration, not an adopted policy. Its procedures, calculations, model assumptions, and approval examples must be validated before use by an actual organization.

Study instruction:
- Identify the principal stages of the receivables lifecycle and the control objective of each stage.

Key point:
- Every recorded receivable needs an identifiable basis, supporting evidence, and a responsible owner.
- Accounting recognition, impairment, collection, and write-off are separate decisions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
2. Q: How should a fictional SOP example be converted into an operational organizational policy?
A: Show answer
Begin by separating illustrative content from decisions that require actual evidence. The fictional document uses invented organizations, counterparties, currencies, dates, risk assessments, model methods, loss rates, calculations, approvals, and journal references. None of these constitutes a verified accounting conclusion or an approved organizational rule. Replace fictional information with the entity's legal details, accounting framework, approved accounting policies, contractual terms, delegated authority, genuine data, and actual reporting requirements. Obtain independent review of the proposed accounting treatment and impairment model. Validate calculations, reconcile opening balances, test exceptions, and confirm that referenced systems and forms exist. Only after the necessary review and formal authorization should the document receive an operational effective date. Retain the fictional source separately as a demonstration, rather than allowing its example values to remain in production registers or templates.

Study instruction:
- Review the document-control record and identify every fictional field that must be verified before adoption.

Key point:
- A generated procedure is not evidence of organizational approval.
- Fictional calculations and thresholds must never become production parameters by default.

Source links:
- IAI 2026 Standards Catalogue
3. Q: Which sources should govern accounting decisions for receivables and financial assets?
A: Show answer
The applicable mandatory legislation and authoritative accounting framework must be established before selecting a treatment. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides practical guidance on accounts receivable and financial instruments within its stated institutional context. IPSAS 41 is relevant when the reporting entity applies the applicable IPSAS framework and addresses classification, measurement, and forward-looking expected credit losses. Entities reporting under an Indonesian financial reporting framework must independently establish which SAK requirements and reporting tier apply. Contractual terms, donor agreements, internal accounting policies, and regulatory obligations must also be considered within their respective authority. A source that describes a particular institution's aging percentages or approval process does not automatically establish a rule for every NGO. Record the precise source, edition or effective date, applicability assessment, reviewer, and approval for each material policy decision.

Study instruction:
- Build a source register connecting each accounting question to the applicable standard, organizational policy, and contractual evidence.

Key point:
- Source relevance and applicability must be assessed separately.
- Institutional examples cannot replace an entity-specific accounting policy.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
4. Q: How should an Indonesian foundation determine which accounting framework applies to its receivables?
A: Show answer
Confirm the foundation's legal form, reporting purpose, applicable regulations, reporting period, and any obligations arising from contracts, donors, regulators, or governing documents. Determine which Indonesian financial reporting standards and reporting tier apply, including whether specific reporting or consolidation requirements affect the accounts. If the entity uses another authorized framework, document its basis and the consequences for recognition, measurement, impairment, presentation, and disclosure. Do not assume that being a foundation automatically establishes IFRS 9, IPSAS 41, or any particular simplified impairment approach as the operative requirement. The Chief Accountant or designated policy owner should prepare the analysis, obtain competent independent review, and secure approval. Until material uncertainty about the framework is resolved, affected accounting conclusions and production-model releases should remain on hold.

Study instruction:
- Document the entity's reporting framework and identify the accounting requirements applicable to the reporting period under review.

Key point:
- Framework selection precedes the choice of an impairment methodology.
- The foundation's status alone does not resolve all reporting requirements.

Source links:
- IAI Publication — Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards Effective 1 January 2025
- IAI 2026 Standards Catalogue
5. Q: How can an organization determine whether a balance is a financial asset?
A: Show answer
Assess the substance of the underlying right rather than relying on the general-ledger account name. A receivable will ordinarily be a financial asset where the organization has a contractual right to receive cash or another financial asset, subject to the applicable framework's definition and scope. Relevant evidence may include signed agreements, accepted claims, invoices, repayment clauses, approved reimbursement requests, and correspondence confirming the obligation. A right to receive goods or services is generally different from an unconditional contractual right to cash. The analysis must consider whether the right exists, whether its conditions have been satisfied, whether payment is disputed, and whether particular financial-instrument requirements apply. Document the classification conclusion and refer ambiguous cases to the accounting policy owner rather than automatically including them in an impairment calculation.

Study instruction:
- Classify sample balances by examining the enforceable right and settlement mechanism supporting each balance.

Key point:
- An account label does not prove that a balance is a financial asset.
- Contractual substance and applicable accounting requirements determine the scope assessment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Identification and Recognition of Financial Assets
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
6. Q: What is the difference between a financial receivable, a prepayment, and an advance awaiting liquidation?
A: Show answer
A financial receivable generally represents a right to receive cash or another financial asset. A supplier prepayment usually represents a right to receive goods or services, while an advance awaiting liquidation may represent an expectation that the recipient will provide supporting expenditure documentation, deliver agreed outputs, or repay an amount under specified circumstances. Those characteristics do not automatically make all three balances financial assets. A refundable contractual advance may require a different assessment from an advance that remains subject to performance and liquidation. Examine the agreement, outstanding obligations, refund clauses, evidence of delivery, and applicable recognition requirements. If the right is conditional or disputed, document the uncertainty and determine the correct classification before applying an impairment model. Non-financial balances remain subject to any relevant impairment or recoverability requirements under their applicable accounting guidance.

Study instruction:
- Compare a supplier prepayment, a refundable staff loan, and an implementing-partner advance using their contractual rights and settlement conditions.

Key point:
- Similar balances may have different accounting characteristics.
- Financial-asset impairment methods must not be applied indiscriminately to every advance.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Identification of Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
7. Q: How should the organization maintain a complete inventory of receivables and financial assets?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled register containing a unique asset ID, counterparty, originating transaction, contractual reference, recognition date, due date, currency, original and outstanding amounts, account code, project or donor dimensions, classification, collection status, and responsible owner. Include relevant staff balances, partner claims, donor reimbursement receivables, supplier recoveries, bank-related balances, and other financial assets within the verified scope. Reconcile the register to the general ledger and supporting subledgers at each reporting date. Identify missing records, duplicated assets, unlinked balances, closed items that remain open, and amounts transferred between systems without their historical evidence. Every correction should retain its reason, source, date, and approval. A complete inventory provides the population needed for aging, impairment, financial reporting, and audit testing.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile the receivable master to the general ledger and investigate unmatched or duplicated records.

Key point:
- Impairment cannot be reliably measured using an incomplete asset population.
- Unique identifiers and reconciled source records support traceability.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable Administration
8. Q: When should a receivable be recognized in the accounting records?
A: Show answer
Recognition depends on the applicable accounting requirements and the facts that establish the underlying right. The process owner must identify the originating event, verify the supporting agreement or transaction evidence, establish the amount and settlement terms, and determine when the recognition criteria are satisfied. A submitted claim is not automatically an enforceable receivable simply because staff have prepared an invoice. Similarly, donor approval may support recognition only after the relevant conditions and applicable accounting requirements have been assessed. Document the recognition date, measurement basis, relevant conditions, and supporting evidence. If approval, acceptance, performance, or contractual entitlement remains unresolved, refer the matter for a documented decision rather than allowing an automated process to create an unsupported asset.

Study instruction:
- Select sample claims and identify the evidence establishing the recognition event and the appropriate reporting period.

Key point:
- Recognition requires an appropriate basis, not merely a spreadsheet entry.
- The originating transaction and its supporting evidence must be traceable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Recognition of Accounts Receivable
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
9. Q: How should receivables be classified and measured after initial recognition?
A: Show answer
Apply the classification and subsequent-measurement requirements of the confirmed accounting framework. Depending on the nature of the asset and the applicable rules, measurement may involve nominal value, amortized cost, fair value, or another permitted basis. Consider contractual cash flows, applicable transaction costs, financing components, interest requirements, currency, and relevant settlement conditions. Do not automatically carry every balance at invoice value if the applicable standard requires a different measurement basis. Nor should an organization assume that every receivable must be discounted without assessing the governing requirements and materiality. Document the selected basis, material assumptions, calculations, and independent review. A material change in contract terms, settlement expectations, or classification may require reassessment of measurement and impairment.

Study instruction:
- Compare the measurement requirements for short-term trade receivables and a material long-term contractual loan under the confirmed framework.

Key point:
- Classification determines the applicable measurement requirements.
- Measurement assumptions must be supported and reproducible.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Recognition and Measurement of Loans and Receivables
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
10. Q: What contractual and legal evidence should support a receivable?
A: Show answer
The evidence should establish who owes the amount, why it is owed, the calculation basis, the payment obligation, the applicable due date, any conditions, and the rights available if the amount is not settled. Depending on the transaction, relevant records may include signed contracts, purchase orders, invoices, accepted deliverables, approved reimbursement claims, repayment acknowledgments, donor agreements, partner agreements, and authorized correspondence. For a disputed amount, retain both the organization's evidence and the counterparty's stated objection. A ledger balance or program manager's confirmation alone may not prove enforceability. Where legal rights are unclear, obtain appropriate Legal review and maintain the conclusion in the controlled register. Do not equate a collection expectation with a legally enforceable right without examining the facts.

Study instruction:
- Test a sample of receivables from the ledger back to the signed agreement and the evidence supporting the amount due.

Key point:
- Contractual support establishes the basis for the claim and informs recovery decisions.
- Legal uncertainty must be documented rather than silently resolved through accounting assumptions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
- Indonesian Law No. 16 of 2001 on Foundations — BPK Legal Documentation
11. Q: How should donor reimbursement receivables be assessed and controlled?
A: Show answer
A donor reimbursement balance should be supported by the governing grant agreement, eligible expenditure records, claim submission, required approvals, and evidence establishing the right to payment. Verify that the claim complies with the relevant period, budget, cost eligibility, documentation, and submission conditions. Track submitted, accepted, disputed, rejected, and overdue amounts separately because each status may have different implications for recognition, collection, and recoverability. An overdue reimbursement should trigger review of the donor's response, outstanding documentation, historical payment behavior, and any changes in funding or contractual circumstances. Program managers may provide essential context, but the impairment conclusion should follow the applicable accounting model and independently reviewed evidence. Do not assume that a donor's historical support or continuing relationship guarantees collection.

Study instruction:
- Trace one reimbursement claim from eligible expenditure through submission, acceptance, collection, and general-ledger reconciliation.

Key point:
- Donor eligibility and enforceable payment rights must be demonstrated.
- Claim status, collection risk, and accounting impairment are related but distinct assessments.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Contributions and Other Receivables
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
12. Q: How should staff loans, employee overpayments, and staff advances be distinguished?
A: Show answer
A staff loan may represent a contractual right to repayment, while an employee overpayment claim requires evidence of the amount recoverable and the applicable repayment terms. A travel or project advance awaiting receipts may instead remain part of a liquidation process and should not automatically enter a financial-asset impairment population. Examine the actual agreement, purpose, remaining obligations, refund conditions, applicable employment requirements, and enforceability of repayment. Payroll deductions must not be assumed to be permissible merely because Finance has identified a balance; Legal and HR should verify the appropriate authority and consent requirements. Track original advance issuance, supporting expenditure, repayments, disputed amounts, and the remaining recoverable balance. Refer unresolved items to the relevant staff advance, payroll, or legal process.

Study instruction:
- Classify a staff loan, an unsupported travel advance, and a payroll overpayment claim using actual evidence and settlement terms.

Key point:
- The accounting treatment depends on the substance of the staff balance.
- A recoverability assessment does not itself authorize payroll deductions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Staff Receivables and Financial Assets
13. Q: How should implementing-partner advances and refundable partner claims be treated?
A: Show answer
First determine whether the balance represents an advance awaiting liquidation, a contractual right to repayment, a reimbursement claim, or a disputed amount. Review the partner agreement, approved budget, liquidation reports, deliverables, expenditure evidence, unspent funds, repayment provisions, and any documented breach or recovery discussion. A program risk rating or late financial report may indicate a need for investigation but does not, by itself, determine the accounting loss allowance. Where a financial asset exists, assess impairment under the applicable framework. Where the balance remains a non-financial advance or contractual performance issue, apply the relevant accounting requirements for that balance instead. Keep grants-management follow-up, contractual recovery, and accounting impairment linked but separately controlled.

Study instruction:
- Inspect an overdue partner advance and explain what additional evidence is needed to establish its classification and recoverability.

Key point:
- Partner advances are not automatically financial assets.
- Programmatic risk indicators should inform investigation without replacing accounting analysis.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Financial Assets and Receivables
14. Q: How should receivable aging be calculated and maintained?
A: Show answer
Calculate aging using the contractual due date and the reporting date, subject to the applicable policy and the nature of the balance. Maintain the original due date, any legally effective revised due date, the reason for the change, the supporting agreement, and the approval. Use consistent aging buckets that allow management to identify current, recently overdue, materially overdue, and long-outstanding balances. Distinguish elapsed time since recognition from time overdue, because these measures answer different questions. For disputed, restructured, settled, or partially paid balances, preserve the history and calculate the residual exposure correctly. An undocumented due-date change must not reset aging or conceal an overdue balance. Reconcile the aging totals to the receivable register and general ledger before using the schedule for reporting or impairment.

Study instruction:
- Recalculate overdue days for a sample using the contractual due date and period-end date, then investigate any changed due dates.

Key point:
- Aging must reflect the verified settlement terms and reporting date.
- A change in due date requires evidence and must preserve historical information.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Receivable Aging and Impairment
15. Q: What evidence should be considered when evaluating credit risk and recoverability?
A: Show answer
Examine payment history, overdue days, failed payment commitments, disputes, counterparty financial information, legal developments, correspondence, subsequent receipts, collateral or guarantees where relevant, and changes in the counterparty's operating environment. For donors and implementing partners, consider acceptance status, grant conditions, continued funding arrangements, performance disputes, and any enforceable repayment obligations. Separate factual evidence from management expectations or unverified verbal statements. Record the assessment date, information sources, identified risks, estimated recovery implications, reviewer, and next collection action. Under an expected-credit-loss framework, assess relevant historical, current, and forward-looking information; under another framework, follow the applicable recognition and measurement criteria. An aging category is useful evidence but is not necessarily a complete assessment of recoverability.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an evidence-based risk assessment for one overdue claim and identify which facts remain unverified.

Key point:
- Credit-risk assessment should be supported by observable evidence and documented judgment.
- Subsequent collections and new risk information may affect the assessment when relevant to the reporting date.

Source links:
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
16. Q: What is the difference between recognizing impairment and identifying a collection problem?
A: Show answer
A collection problem is an operational indication that a counterparty has not paid or that the organization faces difficulty obtaining settlement. Impairment is an accounting assessment performed under the applicable reporting framework. A missed payment may require immediate follow-up and may also supply evidence relevant to impairment, but it does not automatically determine the precise allowance. Similarly, a balance that is not yet overdue may still be exposed to credit risk under a forward-looking model. Collection teams should document the operational facts, while the responsible accounting or credit-risk owner determines the appropriate measurement approach. The organization should not confuse a collection reminder, a disputed balance, a risk flag, an allowance, or a write-off as interchangeable states.

Study instruction:
- Compare a recently overdue but well-supported receivable with a current receivable involving serious counterparty distress.

Key point:
- Collection status informs but does not replace accounting judgment.
- The applicable framework determines how credit risk affects measurement.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Collection and Impairment Processes
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
17. Q: How do the UN IPSAS Finance Manual's impairment guidance and a forward-looking expected credit loss model differ?
A: Show answer
The UN Finance Manual's accounts-receivable chapter describes the UN Secretariat's institutional approach, including specific identification, aging-based estimation, allowance recognition, and write-off workflows under the guidance reflected in that manual. IPSAS 41 introduces a forward-looking expected-credit-loss model for financial instruments within its scope, while IFRS 9 contains a related impairment model for entities applying IFRS. These approaches should not be combined indiscriminately. In particular, historical UN aging percentages must not be copied automatically into an IFRS 9 or IPSAS 41 model, and an ECL calculation should not be imposed on an entity whose applicable framework prescribes a different approach. Establish the operative standard, scope, required methodology, and approved policy before selecting the calculation. Document any differences between a reference manual and the requirements actually applicable to the organization.

Study instruction:
- Compare the measurement logic and applicability of the UN Finance Manual's institutional approach with the relevant requirements of IPSAS 41 or IFRS 9.

Key point:
- Accounting methods must follow the framework applicable to the entity.
- UN-specific aging percentages are not universal impairment rates.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
18. Q: How should the organization select an impairment methodology for eligible financial assets?
A: Show answer
Method selection follows the applicable accounting framework and the characteristics of the financial asset. Depending on the standard, eligible balances may require an individual assessment, a collective assessment using shared risk characteristics, a provision matrix, a general expected-credit-loss model, or a permitted simplified approach. The selected method must be supported by the relevant eligibility criteria and approved policy. Record why the method applies, the asset population covered, required inputs, measurement horizon, assumptions, and approval evidence. A method used for ordinary short-term receivables may not be appropriate for a material staff loan or a disputed partner claim. Methodology selection should remain separate from the subsequent calculation so that an incorrect scope decision cannot be hidden by mathematically correct processing.

Study instruction:
- Map asset classes to the methods permitted by the confirmed framework and document the eligibility rationale for each class.

Key point:
- No impairment method is appropriate solely because it is easy to calculate.
- Scope and methodology must be approved before production calculations run.

Source links:
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
19. Q: When should receivables be assessed individually rather than collectively?
A: Show answer
Individual assessment is appropriate where the applicable framework and policy require or permit it, particularly when a balance is individually significant or has distinctive risk characteristics that a collective estimate may not adequately represent. Examples include a major disputed contractual claim, a counterparty facing severe financial distress, or a material receivable with unusual collateral or settlement terms. Collective assessment may be appropriate for balances that share relevant credit-risk characteristics and can be measured reliably using a suitable common method. The organization should document the selection criteria, the assessment performed, and the conclusion. A material asset assessed individually must not also be included in a collective population in a way that counts the same expected loss twice. If an individual assessment finds no impairment under a framework that permits subsequent collective assessment, follow that framework's rules for population assignment.

Study instruction:
- Select a portfolio of receivables and explain why specific assets require separate analysis while others may be grouped.

Key point:
- Significant or unusual risks may require individual analysis.
- Individual and collective assessment must not duplicate the same exposure.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
20. Q: How should receivable portfolios be segmented for impairment estimation?
A: Show answer
Group balances using credit-risk characteristics that are relevant to their expected collection behavior and the applicable measurement method. Potential characteristics include counterparty type, contractual terms, geographic exposure, payment history, overdue status, financing characteristics, and other evidence supported by the data. Segmentation should not rely only on department, donor, or project labels unless those attributes genuinely explain differences in risk. Test whether each segment is sufficiently homogeneous and whether historical experience is applicable to the current population. Document the segmentation logic and the rules for transferring an asset when its risk characteristics change. Separate treatment may be needed for material or unusual balances. Changes in segmentation require impact analysis and approval because regrouping may materially change estimated impairment without changing the underlying receivables.

Study instruction:
- Compare alternative segmentation schemes and justify which characteristics provide meaningful evidence about collection risk.

Key point:
- Segments should reflect relevant shared risk characteristics.
- Changes to grouping logic require testing and documented approval.

Source links:
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
21. Q: What historical data is needed to support an impairment model?
A: Show answer
The required data depends on the applicable model, but commonly includes a complete historical population, original and outstanding balances, contractual due dates, actual cash collections, recoveries, write-offs, payment patterns, default events, and relevant changes in contract or counterparty status. Each record must be linked to a source and consistent reporting date. Data owners should explain missing periods, inconsistent identifiers, changes in accounting systems, and any biases in the historical sample. Historical loss rates should not be used without checking whether the data reflects the asset classes and conditions to which the rates will be applied. The model owner must document exclusions, adjustments, data limitations, and validation. Where history is limited, the entity should determine a defensible alternative permitted by its framework and obtain appropriate review rather than inventing precision.

Study instruction:
- Trace the data supporting an impairment estimate back to source transactions and identify missing or inconsistent records.

Key point:
- Reliable model output depends on complete, relevant, and traceable input data.
- Historical experience must be assessed for relevance before being applied to current exposures.

Source links:
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
22. Q: How should forward-looking information be incorporated into an expected credit loss estimate?
A: Show answer
Where the applicable framework requires expected credit losses, the estimate should reflect relevant historical experience, current conditions, and reasonable and supportable forward-looking information. Potential indicators include changes in counterparty financial circumstances, the operating environment, payment conditions, and other factors that influence expected cash shortfalls. Forecast assumptions must be relevant to the portfolio, documented, and consistent with the information available at the reporting date. Where multiple scenarios are required or used, the basis for scenario selection and weighting must be supported. Avoid treating a general economic forecast as automatically predictive of losses on a particular NGO receivable portfolio. Record the sources, assumptions, weighting rationale, limitations, and reviewer challenge. If the data does not adequately support a proposed assumption, resolve the limitation before using the result as a final accounting estimate.

Study instruction:
- Identify forward-looking variables relevant to a particular receivable portfolio and explain how their relevance would be assessed.

Key point:
- Forward-looking inputs must be reasonable, supportable, and relevant to the exposure.
- Assumptions and scenario weights require evidence and independent review.

Source links:
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
23. Q: How should collateral, guarantees, and expected recoveries affect an impairment assessment?
A: Show answer
Assess whether the collateral, guarantee, or other recovery source is relevant under the applicable accounting framework and whether it is legally valid, enforceable, accessible, and expected to produce a measurable recovery. Examine the supporting documents, any restrictions, priority of claims, realization costs, expected timing, and uncertainty. Do not count a guarantee merely because it appears in a contract if the guarantor cannot be identified or the guarantee's effectiveness is uncertain. Avoid double-counting collateral recoveries already reflected in another part of the calculation. If recovery depends on an unresolved legal matter, record the uncertainty and obtain appropriate review. The model should distinguish gross exposure from the estimated cash shortfall after considering only the recovery elements allowed by the governing method.

Study instruction:
- Review a receivable supported by a guarantee and identify the evidence needed before including expected recovery in the estimate.

Key point:
- Recovery assumptions must be supported by enforceable rights and realistic evidence.
- Collateral or guarantees must not be double-counted or assumed effective without analysis.

Source links:
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
24. Q: What do probability of default, loss given default, and exposure at default mean in a model example?
A: Show answer
Probability of default (PD) represents an estimate of the likelihood of default over the relevant horizon. Loss given default (LGD) represents the proportion of exposure expected to be lost if default occurs, considering applicable recovery assumptions. Exposure at default (EAD) represents the relevant exposure expected to be outstanding at default. A simplified illustration may multiply these components, but this arithmetic does not by itself establish compliance with an applicable impairment standard. The definitions, measurement horizon, recovery assumptions, timing, discounting, and permitted methodology must follow the governing model. The fictional source uses PD of 20%, LGD of 15%, and EAD of IDR 10,000,000 to illustrate an IDR 300,000 result. These numbers are invented and must not be used as actual risk parameters or an approved allowance.

Study instruction:
- Recalculate the fictional PD × LGD × EAD example and identify which assumptions would require evidence in a real model.

Key point:
- Model arithmetic is not a substitute for methodology compliance and validated inputs.
- Illustrative parameters must remain clearly separated from approved organizational assumptions.

Source links:
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
25. Q: How should a provision matrix be developed and controlled for eligible receivables?
A: Show answer
Where the applicable framework permits a provision matrix or similar collective estimation method, determine appropriate risk groupings and historical loss information, then adjust the methodology for relevant current and forward-looking conditions where required. Aging buckets may be used as a segmentation factor, but rates need an evidence-based rationale. Define the source population, measurement date, treatment of partially paid balances, exclusions, recovery data, and calculation logic. Reconcile the model population with the approved receivable register and check totals after every transformation. The fictional SOP contains an illustrative calculation using IDR 100,000,000 multiplied by a combined rate of 4.5%, producing IDR 4,500,000. These values demonstrate arithmetic only; they are not approved loss rates. Any actual matrix must be calibrated, validated, approved, and reassessed according to the applicable framework and policy.

Study instruction:
- Reperform a sample matrix calculation and trace each rate and exposure amount to its approved source.

Key point:
- Aging-based estimation requires a justified methodology and reliable population data.
- Fictional rates must never be copied into a production model.

Source links:
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
26. Q: When should the time value of money be considered in measuring impairment or financial assets?
A: Show answer
The applicable measurement requirements determine when discounting is necessary and which rate or approximation is appropriate. For financial assets measured using methods that require discounted expected cash flows, estimates should reflect the required timing and discount rate. The relevant effective interest rate or other prescribed basis may depend on the financial instrument and the applicable standard. Do not introduce an arbitrary discount rate simply to make a model appear more sophisticated. Similarly, do not omit discounting where the applicable requirements demand it. Document the cash-flow assumptions, timing, rate source, calculation, and treatment of uncertainty. If the model does not incorporate discounting, state the reason, assess materiality and compliance, and obtain approval for the treatment rather than presenting an untested simplification as universally valid.

Study instruction:
- Identify whether a material long-term receivable requires discounting under the applicable framework and document the permitted basis.

Key point:
- Discounting requirements depend on the instrument and governing measurement model.
- Time-value assumptions must be supported, documented, and independently reviewed.

Source links:
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
27. Q: How should management overlays and model overrides be governed?
A: Show answer
An overlay or override may be considered when the approved model does not adequately capture a material, supportable risk or when a documented limitation requires an adjustment permitted by the applicable framework. The adjustment must identify the affected exposure, reason, calculation, supporting evidence, financial impact, effective period, owner, and required approval. Independent review should assess whether the adjustment duplicates risks already included in the model, introduces bias, or persists after its original rationale has expired. Overrides must not be used to force the allowance toward a preferred budget result or conceal an unresolved data problem. Define expiration and reassessment requirements, and retain both the original model result and the approved adjustment so the reporting outcome can be reproduced.

Study instruction:
- Review a proposed overlay and evaluate its evidence, quantified impact, duplication risk, approval, and expiry conditions.

Key point:
- An overlay requires a documented rationale and independent challenge.
- Uncontrolled overrides can undermine model reliability and reporting integrity.

Source links:
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
28. Q: How should an impairment model be validated and back-tested?
A: Show answer
Validation should test whether the model is appropriate for its intended population, whether its inputs are complete and reliable, whether the calculations follow the approved methodology, and whether its results are reasonable. Reperform selected calculations independently, test boundary conditions, review changes in estimates, and check that exceptions are handled as designed. Back-testing may compare prior estimates with actual collections, defaults, recoveries, and subsequent loss experience, with appropriate consideration of timing and changing conditions. Differences should be investigated rather than automatically interpreted as proof that the earlier estimate was unreasonable. The independent validator must not simply approve the model prepared by the same person. Record test cases, expected results, actual outcomes, defects, corrections, retesting, and release decisions. Material model changes should trigger renewed validation appropriate to their impact.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an independent sample calculation and compare a previous estimate with actual collection outcomes.

Key point:
- Validation tests both the model design and its operation.
- Identified defects require correction and documented retesting before release.

Source links:
- IPSASB Supporting Resources — IPSAS 41
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
29. Q: How should changes in the impairment allowance be reconciled between reporting periods?
A: Show answer
Prepare a roll-forward showing the opening allowance, current-period impairment charges or reversals, write-offs charged against the allowance where appropriate, recoveries or other movements required by the applicable framework, and the closing allowance. Maintain separate supporting schedules for gross receivables, allowance balances, and net carrying amounts. Each movement should be linked to the relevant calculation run, journal reference, reporting period, and approval. Distinguish changes caused by new receivables, collections, changed risk estimates, methodology changes, write-offs, and reclassifications. Investigate any unexplained residual before the period-end certification is approved. The roll-forward should reconcile to the subledger, general ledger, and financial reporting outputs without allowing unexplained adjustments to be hidden in a general balancing account.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile an opening allowance to the closing allowance and explain each material movement using supporting evidence.

Key point:
- Every allowance movement must have an identifiable cause and accounting reference.
- Closing balances must reconcile across the model, journals, ledger, and reporting schedules.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable and Allowances
30. Q: What accounting entries may arise when recognizing or changing an impairment allowance?
A: Show answer
The journal entry depends on the applicable accounting framework, the asset's classification, and the nature of the change. A typical impairment charge may debit an impairment expense and credit an allowance account. A reversal may reduce the allowance and recognize the corresponding effect according to the applicable accounting requirements. A write-off against an existing allowance may reduce gross receivables and the allowance without creating the same expense a second time, subject to the framework and the facts. These illustrations are not universal posting instructions. The preparer must use the approved chart of accounts, confirm the source calculation, identify the reporting period and relevant project dimensions, and obtain the required review. Posting should occur only after reconciliation and authorization. Separate journal rules may be required for recoveries, currency effects, modifications, or other movements.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a supported illustration of an impairment charge, an allowance reversal, and a write-off against an existing allowance.

Key point:
- Journal entries must follow the applicable accounting treatment and approved chart of accounts.
- An existing allowance must be considered to avoid duplicate expense recognition.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Allowances and Write-Offs
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
31. Q: What is the difference between an impairment allowance and an accounting write-off?
A: Show answer
An impairment allowance estimates the amount of loss recognized under the applicable measurement framework while the asset continues to be recognized. A write-off removes all or part of a receivable from the accounting records when the relevant accounting criteria and authorized process permit that action. A balance can therefore have an allowance without being written off, and a write-off requires its own analysis and evidence. The fact that a balance is old, disputed, or fully provided for does not automatically satisfy every write-off requirement. Collection, legal enforcement, and reporting obligations may continue after an accounting write-off. Record the evidence of irrecoverability, applicable policy, approval, amount, accounting entry, and remaining recovery rights. Where the framework prescribes a particular write-off threshold or condition, that rule must take precedence over a generic procedural example.

Study instruction:
- Compare a fully provided but still collectible receivable with a balance that meets the organization's authorized write-off criteria.

Key point:
- An allowance and a write-off serve different accounting purposes.
- A write-off requires appropriate evidence and authority.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Subsequent Measurement, Write-Offs and Allowances
32. Q: Why must an accounting write-off be distinguished from a legal waiver or forgiveness of a claim?
A: Show answer
An accounting write-off changes the accounting records; a legal waiver, settlement, or forgiveness may release or modify the organization's legal rights. The two actions are not interchangeable. A write-off does not automatically establish that the organization has surrendered its right to recover the amount, nor does it establish that a donor has authorized the underlying loss or that the amount is tax-deductible. Review the contractual terms, governing law, delegated authority, donor or partner restrictions, employment implications, and required legal approvals. Where a waiver is proposed, maintain a separate legal decision record with its scope, effective date, terms, and supporting approval. Record the accounting consequence only after the legal and accounting assessments have been completed as necessary. Preserve the original obligation and decision history so that subsequent reviewers can distinguish accounting treatment from the disposition of the claim.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a proposed write-off and determine whether a separate legal waiver or settlement decision is also required.

Key point:
- Accounting derecognition does not automatically release legal rights.
- Waivers and settlements require separate authority and evidence where applicable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Write-Offs and Derecognition
33. Q: How should cash received after a receivable has been written off be recorded?
A: Show answer
Identify the original asset and write-off record, establish the nature and date of the cash receipt, determine its allocation, and apply the treatment prescribed by the applicable accounting framework and approved policy. Do not create an unrelated revenue entry merely because the original balance is no longer visible in the open-receivables list. Maintain a recovery register that links the receipt to the original asset, write-off reference, amount, currency, allocation, journal, reviewer, and closure evidence. Investigate unidentified or partially allocated receipts before closing the case. Where legal rights, third-party settlements, donor conditions, or other restrictions affect the receipt, obtain the relevant review. Include post-write-off recoveries in the appropriate monitoring and reporting processes so the organization can evaluate collection effectiveness and preserve a complete audit trail.

Study instruction:
- Trace a hypothetical post-write-off receipt back to its original receivable, approval, and ledger entries.

Key point:
- Recovery must remain linked to the original asset and write-off history.
- Cash receipts require proper identification, allocation, and accounting treatment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Receivables and Write-Offs
34. Q: How should a modified, restructured, or renegotiated receivable be assessed?
A: Show answer
A change in repayment date, principal, interest, settlement terms, or other contractual conditions may affect classification, measurement, credit risk, and derecognition. Document the original terms, proposed and executed amendments, the date the change becomes effective, the legal basis, and the effect on expected cash flows. Determine whether the change constitutes a modification of the existing instrument or requires different accounting treatment under the applicable framework. Do not silently reset the age of a receivable merely because the counterparty requests new terms. Preserve the original contractual history and assess the credit risk both in accordance with the applicable measurement requirements and in light of the changed circumstances. Material restructurings require independent accounting and, where needed, Legal review before the revised treatment is posted.

Study instruction:
- Assess a hypothetical repayment extension and list the accounting, legal, aging, and impairment questions that require resolution.

Key point:
- Contract changes may affect both measurement and the organization's rights.
- Original terms and risk history must remain traceable after restructuring.

Source links:
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
35. Q: How should reporting-date cut-off and subsequent receipts be handled in receivables reporting?
A: Show answer
Establish the receivable population, contractual status, impairment estimate, and supporting evidence as of the reporting date, using the timing requirements of the applicable framework. Payments or confirmations received after period-end may provide relevant evidence about conditions existing at the reporting date, depending on their nature and the governing rules. They should not automatically be treated as evidence of conditions that existed earlier without examining the facts. Record the original reporting-date balance, subsequent event, timing, supporting evidence, evaluation, and any approved adjustment. Distinguish the date on which an event occurred from the date it became known to management. If information arrives after an initial report is prepared, document whether the accounting numbers, disclosures, or management commentary need revision and preserve the version history.

Study instruction:
- Review subsequent receipts around year-end and determine which evidence may affect the reporting-date assessment under the applicable standard.

Key point:
- Reporting-date judgments must reflect the relevant facts and timing requirements.
- Subsequent information requires assessment, not automatic adjustment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
36. Q: How should foreign-currency receivables and impairment estimates be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain the transaction currency, original amount, outstanding foreign-currency balance, contractual settlement terms, relevant exchange-rate sources, and reporting-currency equivalent. Distinguish foreign-exchange remeasurement from changes in the expected recoverability of the receivable. The applicable accounting framework determines the exchange-rate requirements and the measurement treatment of the financial asset and related allowance. Apply the approved method consistently, document the date and rate source, and review any material exchange movement. Do not attribute every increase in the reporting-currency allowance to greater credit risk when it may arise from exchange-rate changes. Reconcile the original currency amounts and reporting-currency values separately, and ensure the impairment model and general-ledger entries use consistent approved data.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile a foreign-currency receivable and distinguish exchange-rate movements from impairment-related changes.

Key point:
- Currency remeasurement and credit impairment are different sources of financial movement.
- Exchange-rate sources, calculations, and accounting entries must be traceable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Financial Assets and Receivables
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
37. Q: What financial statement disclosures may be required for receivables and financial-asset credit risk?
A: Show answer
The disclosures depend on the applicable accounting framework and materiality requirements. They may include relevant accounting policies, carrying amounts by appropriate class, allowance movements, credit-risk exposures, overdue or impaired balances, significant estimation uncertainty, and information about how financial risks are managed. The entity should determine which classes of financial instruments share sufficiently similar risk characteristics and what quantitative and qualitative disclosures are required. Reconcile disclosure figures to the approved ledger balances and impairment schedules. Explain material changes and unresolved issues without presenting an unverified estimate as final. The UN Finance Manual and IFRS 7 describe relevant financial-instrument disclosure considerations within their respective contexts, but neither should be assumed to govern every Indonesian foundation without a framework assessment.

Study instruction:
- Map material receivable and credit-risk disclosures to the source register, general ledger, and applicable reporting requirements.

Key point:
- Disclosure requirements depend on the applicable reporting framework.
- Published figures must reconcile to the underlying accounting records and supporting estimates.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
- IFRS 7 — Financial Instruments: Disclosures
38. Q: How should receivable subledgers, impairment schedules, journals, and the general ledger be reconciled?
A: Show answer
Reconcile transaction counts and monetary totals across the receivable register, supporting subledger, impairment-model population, allowance calculation, journal interface, and general ledger. Identify differences caused by timing, missing entries, duplicate records, classification changes, foreign exchange, rejected journals, or unauthorized adjustments. For each difference, record the amount, source, reason, owner, action, approval, and evidence of resolution. Separate legitimate reconciling items from errors and unresolved accounting judgments. Do not treat a balancing journal as a complete solution where supporting evidence is missing. The final reconciliation should demonstrate that gross receivables, allowance balances, and net carrying amounts agree with the relevant reporting schedules and that no asset is included twice or omitted without explanation.

Study instruction:
- Perform a three-way reconciliation between the asset register, impairment calculation, and general ledger, investigating all residuals.

Key point:
- Reconciliation is required from source transactions through to final reporting.
- Unexplained residuals must be resolved or formally escalated before release.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable and Allowances
39. Q: How should responsibilities be separated across receivable management and impairment estimation?
A: Show answer
The process should distinguish transaction origination, asset-register maintenance, model preparation, independent review, approval, system implementation, journal posting, legal assessment, and independent assurance where practicable. The originating team supplies evidence, the receivables owner maintains accurate records and collections, and the model owner prepares the calculation. An independent reviewer challenges the data, assumptions, methodology, and reconciliations before the authorized approver makes the decision. System administrators implement approved configurations but must not independently authorize accounting substance. When limited staffing prevents complete separation, document compensating controls such as independent transaction review, additional reconciliation, or periodic oversight. Internal Audit should independently assess control design and operation rather than routinely operate the management controls being audited.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a responsibility matrix identifying who prepares, reviews, approves, implements, posts, and independently tests each material activity.

Key point:
- The person preparing a material estimate should not be its sole independent reviewer.
- Delegation and compensating controls must be explicit and evidenced.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable Process
40. Q: What approvals are necessary before a material impairment adjustment or write-off is posted?
A: Show answer
The organization should follow its formally approved delegation matrix, the applicable accounting requirements, and any relevant contractual or legal restrictions. The approval package should contain the asset identification, gross amount, existing allowance, proposed adjustment, calculation, evidence, methodology, material judgments, reconciliation, accounting entry, and explanation of exceptions. The reviewer must challenge the basis and confirm that the decision is within the approver's authority. Material estimates may require accounting policy approval or specialist review. A write-off must follow the applicable write-off procedure, which may be separate from allowance approval. The system should block transactions when approval is missing or authority is expired, out of scope, or exceeded. Retain the exact version reviewed and the approval evidence so that a later change cannot be mistaken for an already approved decision.

Study instruction:
- Review a sample adjustment against the delegation matrix and trace the approval to the posted journal.

Key point:
- Material accounting decisions require a supported decision and valid approval.
- Approval is tied to the specific calculation and document version reviewed.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Financial Instruments and Write-Off Decisions
41. Q: How should system configuration, model versions, and user access be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain controlled versions of source data, calculation logic, model parameters, mapping rules, access permissions, and release records. Each material change should identify the requester, reason, affected components, risk assessment, testing, independent approval, implementer, effective date, and rollback method. Changes to impairment methodology or assumptions should trigger the level of validation required by their effect. Restrict privileged access to authorized personnel and periodically review whether access remains appropriate. System administrators should not self-authorize changes or use administrative privileges to bypass accounting approvals. Preserve the prior approved model and its outputs so the organization can reproduce previously reported estimates. If a change affects historic results, assess the appropriate accounting and correction requirements rather than silently rewriting earlier reporting records.

Study instruction:
- Inspect a model-release log and verify that each material change has a documented reason, approval, test, and rollback plan.

Key point:
- Version control preserves the ability to reproduce approved results.
- Privileged access must be restricted, reviewed, and traceable.

Source links:
- IPSASB Supporting Resources — IPSAS 41
42. Q: How should receivables and allowance balances be controlled during a system migration or portfolio transfer?
A: Show answer
Capture a complete, approved snapshot of the source portfolio at an agreed reporting date before transferring data. Reconcile record counts, gross balances, allowances, net balances, currencies, contractual terms, due dates, disputes, collection history, write-offs, and recovery links between the source and target systems. Document mapping rules, transformed fields, duplicates, missing identifiers, and historical information that cannot be migrated automatically. Any unexplained difference must be investigated and assigned an owner; a migration must not be certified as reconciled merely because the target system accepts the records. Run validation tests before release and obtain independent sign-off. Preserve source extracts and migration records so future reviewers can reconstruct opening balances and trace subsequent transactions to their original records.

Study instruction:
- Compare fictional source and target portfolio totals and identify why an unexplained migration residual must block closure.

Key point:
- Migration must preserve financial values and material historical information.
- Unreconciled differences require investigation before acceptance.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Receivable Records and Accounting
43. Q: How should disputed receivables, failed controls, and model exceptions be managed?
A: Show answer
Record each exception in a controlled register with a unique ID, asset reference, reporting period, amount, issue description, source evidence, severity, owner, deadline, referral, corrective action, and closure evidence. Disputes may require Finance, Legal, Grants, HR, or another responsible team to establish the facts. A failed model test may require suspending an affected calculation or release until the problem is resolved. Distinguish temporary containment from final resolution and confirm whether prior reporting or approvals are affected. The case should not be closed merely because the item was moved to a later reporting period. Require evidence that the cause has been corrected, relevant figures have been reconciled, and the control has been retested. Use recurring issue analysis to strengthen the procedure and prevent repeated failures.

Study instruction:
- Investigate an exception from initial identification through root-cause analysis, correction, retesting, and final approval.

Key point:
- Every material exception requires an owner, deadline, and documented resolution.
- Remediation must address root causes rather than simply remove an item from a report.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
44. Q: How should accounting impairment allowances be distinguished from tax-deductible bad-debt provisions in Indonesia?
A: Show answer
Accounting impairment and tax deductibility are separate determinations. The financial reporting treatment follows the applicable accounting framework and approved accounting policy, whereas tax treatment depends on the current tax legislation, eligible taxpayer categories, applicable conditions, and supporting evidence. Indonesian Minister of Finance Regulation PMK 74 of 2024 addresses deductible bad-debt reserves for specified types of businesses and should not be assumed to make an NGO foundation eligible simply because it records an accounting allowance. Obtain current tax advice where needed and prepare a separate tax reconciliation. Document any differences between the accounting carrying amount and the amount recognized for tax purposes, including the legal basis and required evidence. Do not treat an accounting write-off or allowance as automatic proof of tax deductibility.

Study instruction:
- Compare a proposed accounting allowance with the applicable Indonesian tax provisions and document why the tax treatment may differ.

Key point:
- An accounting allowance does not automatically qualify as a tax deduction.
- Tax eligibility must be assessed independently under current Indonesian requirements.

Source links:
- Directorate General of Taxes — Deductible Bad-Debt Reserves
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
45. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, or VBA support receivables and impairment controls?
A: Show answer
Excel can maintain structured asset registers, controlled assumptions, calculation schedules, review checklists, and reconciliations. Power Query can combine source files, standardize field formats, link master data, identify missing fields, and flag duplicate or unmatched records. VBA may support approved workflows, exception reports, controlled exports, status updates, and audit logging. These tools should assist an already approved accounting methodology rather than determine the accounting rule independently. Use separate input, processing, review, and output layers. Restrict formula and configuration changes, validate required fields, retain version information, and reconcile imported totals with the original records. Test automated results against independently calculated samples, including invalid dates, duplicate assets, missing terms, unusual balances, and failed calculations. Do not release a report merely because the macro or query completed without an error.

Study instruction:
- Design a sample workflow that imports a receivable register, checks required data, calculates approved results, and generates an independently reviewed exception report.

Key point:
- Automation improves consistency but does not establish accounting validity.
- Source reconciliation, testing, approvals, and exception handling remain necessary.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
46. Q: How should receivables documentation, confidentiality, and record retention be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain a consistent archive structure linking source agreements, receivable records, assessments, model inputs, calculation outputs, approvals, journals, reconciliation records, and subsequent recoveries. Use stable identifiers, versioned filenames, reporting dates, and an index so that each material balance can be traced without searching through unrelated files. Restrict sensitive staff, partner, donor, and contractual information to authorized users. Apply retention periods according to applicable law, donor and contractual conditions, and approved organizational requirements. Protect records from unauthorized alteration or deletion and periodically test backup restoration. Preserve prior approved versions rather than overwriting them with new calculations. Where records are missing or inaccessible, record the exception, identify the responsible owner, and determine whether the evidence gap affects the accounting conclusion or prevents report approval.

Study instruction:
- Trace a reported allowance from its source data to the final approved calculation, journal, and archived evidence package.

Key point:
- A complete audit trail must link source evidence, decisions, and final reporting.
- Access, retention, backups, and restoration need documented controls.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable Records
47. Q: How should the receivables and impairment SOP be implemented and communicated to staff?
A: Show answer
Implementation should begin with a gap assessment of existing policies, contractual records, systems, approval arrangements, and current practices. Identify process owners and map the activities that must change. Prepare the approved forms and registers, configure the system, transfer existing balances, test calculations and controls, and resolve material exceptions before operational release. Train staff according to their responsibilities, including the distinction between receivable classification, collection action, impairment estimation, write-off, and legal waiver. Require acknowledgment of the approved version and assess understanding through practical exercises. Keep old policy versions for historical reference, but clearly identify the current operative version and effective date. Establish an escalation route for unanswered questions so that staff do not substitute informal interpretations for authorized accounting decisions.

Study instruction:
- Develop an implementation checklist covering ownership, training, testing, opening-balance reconciliation, approval, and release.

Key point:
- Staff need clear responsibilities and an approved procedure before operating the process.
- Implementation must include testing, communication, and evidence of adoption.

- IPSASB Supporting Resources — IPSAS 41
48. Q: Which performance indicators can management use to monitor receivables and impairment controls?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include total gross receivables, allowance and net balances, overdue exposure, aging by portfolio, concentration by counterparty, collection rates, disputed claims, write-offs, recoveries, unresolved exceptions, and time taken to clear material discrepancies. Model monitoring may include changes in expected losses, material overlays, comparison with realized outcomes, and the proportion of balances lacking sufficient evidence. Define each indicator, calculation method, source, frequency, owner, threshold, and escalation requirement. Interpret trends in context; a rise in the allowance may result from new exposure, worsening risk, a change in assumptions, or an improved calculation methodology. Management should review the drivers and assign corrective actions where needed. Dashboard totals must reconcile to approved financial records and should never be treated as evidence of compliance solely because the report refreshed successfully.

Study instruction:
- Design a management dashboard with reproducible definitions and verify its totals against reconciled source schedules.

Key point:
- Indicators should reveal changes in exposure, collectability, and control performance.
- Automated reporting requires independent reconciliation and management review.

Source links:
- IFRS 7 — Financial Instruments: Disclosures
49. Q: How should an organization distinguish a genuine accounting error from a policy or model change?
A: Show answer
Investigate the source of the difference before selecting a correction. An accounting error may involve incorrect source data, a calculation defect, a misclassification, an omitted transaction, or a failure to apply the applicable requirement. A model change may instead reflect a revised methodology, a new assumption, updated information, or a correction to an earlier estimate. The accounting consequences depend on the nature of the item, the applicable standard, materiality, and whether the earlier reporting was erroneous. Document the previous treatment, new information, cause, financial effect, decision, and approval. Preserve the original record and do not overwrite prior results without a traceable correction. If a previously issued financial statement may be affected, obtain appropriate accounting review before determining whether adjustment, restatement, or additional disclosure is required.

Study instruction:
- Review a hypothetical change in an allowance and determine whether it arises from new information, an estimate change, a model change, or an error.

Key point:
- Different causes of change may have different accounting consequences.
- Corrective action must preserve a clear history of previous and revised conclusions.

Source links:
- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
- IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments
50. Q: What conditions must be satisfied before a receivables and impairment SOP is approved for operational use?
A: Show answer
The final release should confirm that the applicable framework and current source requirements have been assessed, the receivable population is complete, classifications and contractual rights are supported, and the impairment methodology is appropriate. Model inputs, assumptions, calculations, allowance movements, journals, and disclosure interfaces must be tested and reconciled. Write-off and waiver approvals must follow their separate required procedures. Access rights, version control, exception handling, backup, and audit-trail controls must be operational. Material issues must be resolved or formally held from release where they prevent a reliable conclusion. The responsible approver should receive the final document, testing evidence, unresolved-risk register, change history, and independent review record. The source document's own acceptance section explicitly identifies several tests as unverified or not performed; its fictional calculations and procedural examples therefore cannot, by themselves, demonstrate readiness for actual deployment.

Study instruction:
- Complete a release checklist and identify which requirements have evidence of completion and which remain blocked or unverified.

Key point:
- Operational adoption requires validated accounting treatment, tested controls, and authorized approval.
- Unverified framework assumptions, unreconciled balances, or incomplete testing must not be represented as completed controls.

- IPSAS 41 — Financial Instruments
