Table 9
Provisions and Contingent Items: Accounting, Reporting and Control Procedures
1. Q: What is the purpose of a controlled procedure for provisions, contingent liabilities and contingent assets?
A: Show answer
The procedure establishes a consistent process for identifying uncertain obligations and potential recoveries, assessing their accounting treatment, measuring amounts where appropriate, preparing disclosures, recording journals, obtaining independent approval and retaining supporting evidence. It should cover events from initial identification through assessment, reporting-date reassessment, settlement and closure. Responsibilities must be assigned to Finance, Legal, contract owners, operational teams, independent reviewers and authorized approvers. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional demonstration and has not been adopted. Its organization names, cases, amounts, conclusions and approvals must not be treated as actual evidence or operative accounting policy. An organization must validate its reporting framework, applicable requirements and internal authority before implementing the procedure.

Study instruction:
- Identify the complete lifecycle of a provision case and distinguish illustrative procedures from approved organizational policy.
Key point:
- Every case requires documented assessment, appropriate accounting treatment, authorized approval and a traceable outcome.
- A sample SOP cannot replace entity-specific framework validation and formal adoption.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets
2. Q: How should an organization determine which accounting framework governs its provisions and contingent items?
A: Show answer
Finance should document the legal entity, reporting purpose, applicable financial reporting framework, reporting period and relevant statutory or institutional requirements before making an accounting decision. An Indonesian foundation may need to apply the applicable Indonesian Financial Accounting Standards, while another organization may be subject to IPSAS, IFRS or specific institutional requirements. Donor agreements and internal policies provide additional operational requirements but do not automatically determine statutory recognition. The framework decision should identify authoritative sources, effective dates, relevant requirements, unresolved interpretations and the person authorized to approve the conclusion. The supplied SOP deliberately uses illustrative IAS 37-style logic without confirming the framework applicable to an actual entity. Do not implement its accounting rules until that uncertainty has been resolved.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a framework-applicability memorandum identifying the legal entity, reporting purpose, governing standards, effective edition and approval evidence.
Key point:
- Framework selection must precede operational accounting decisions.
- Requirements from different accounting frameworks must not be combined indiscriminately.
Source links:
- Indonesian Institute of Accountants — PSAK provisions and contingent-item guidance
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
3. Q: Which transactions and events should fall within the scope of a provisions and contingent-items procedure?
A: Show answer
The register should capture potentially relevant legal claims, disputes, contractual penalties, guarantees, indemnities, environmental restoration obligations, onerous contracts and uncertain obligations arising from past events. Potential insurance recoveries, enforceable indemnification rights and other possible inflows should be assessed separately. The procedure must also identify matters governed by more specific accounting requirements, such as employee benefits, income taxes, leases, financial instruments and impairment. A general provisions procedure should not override those specific requirements. Event categories should direct cases to the appropriate accounting assessment, legal review and operational workflow. The organization should define inclusion criteria and referral responsibilities so that an item is neither omitted because it has an unusual description nor processed twice under overlapping procedures.

Study instruction:
- Map the event categories in the SOP to the relevant accounting standards and existing legal, HR, procurement, contract-management and financial-reporting procedures.
Key point:
- Scope is determined by the substance of the event and the applicable accounting requirements.
- Specific-standard referrals prevent incorrect recognition and duplicate accounting.
Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 scope and requirements
4. Q: How should a provision be distinguished from an accrual, payable, contingent liability and contingent asset?
A: Show answer
A provision is a liability with uncertain timing or amount and is recognized when the applicable standard's recognition criteria are satisfied. An ordinary payable or accrual generally involves a more established obligation, even if its final invoice or calculation has not yet been received. A contingent liability is a possible obligation, or a present obligation that does not qualify for recognition under the applicable framework, and is generally assessed for disclosure. A contingent asset is a possible asset whose existence depends on uncertain future events; it is not automatically recognized merely because a recovery is expected. Classification must follow the substance of the circumstances, evidence and governing standard. Calling an amount a reserve, contingency or provision does not determine its accounting treatment.

Study instruction:
- Compare four realistic cases involving an invoiced payable, an accrued service, a disputed legal claim and a possible insurance recovery.
Key point:
- Classification depends on the underlying rights, obligations and recognition criteria.
- Uncertain timing or amount alone does not make every liability a provision.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Classification and recognition
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 definitions and recognition
5. Q: What criteria must be satisfied before a provision can be recognized?
A: Show answer
Under IAS 37-style requirements, a provision is recognized when three conditions are met: the entity has a present legal or constructive obligation resulting from a past event; an outflow of economic resources is probable; and the amount can be estimated reliably. The corresponding criteria under the organization's actual governing framework must be confirmed before application. Finance should document each condition separately, identify evidence supporting the assessment, explain significant judgment and obtain independent review. A case should not be recognized merely because management is concerned about a possible loss, nor should a valid obligation be omitted simply because the amount is uncertain. The recognition decision and its date must be recorded so that future reassessment can establish whether circumstances have changed.

Study instruction:
- Apply the three recognition criteria to a legal claim and document the evidence supporting or contradicting each condition.
Key point:
- Recognition requires an obligation, the required likelihood of outflow and a reliable estimate under the applicable standard.
- All criteria must be assessed; a single positive indicator is insufficient.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provision recognition criteria
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
6. Q: How should the existence of a present obligation arising from a past event be assessed?
A: Show answer
The assessment should establish what happened before or on the reporting date, which contractual or legal rights and obligations arose, and whether the entity has a current responsibility that it cannot realistically avoid under the applicable requirements. Relevant evidence may include signed contracts, statutory obligations, service-delivery records, correspondence, court documents, established organizational practices and approved commitments. A proposed future activity or an intention to spend money does not automatically create a present obligation. Equally, a lack of an invoice or formal demand does not prove that no obligation exists. Finance and Legal should document the event, the date of the event, the source of the obligation, contrary evidence and the resulting conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Construct a timeline for a contractual dispute and distinguish the past event from possible future events that have not yet occurred.
Key point:
- An obligation assessment begins with facts and enforceable or constructive responsibilities, not the expected payment alone.
- The reporting-date condition must be established before recognition is decided.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Recognition and present obligations
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
7. Q: What is the difference between a legal obligation and a constructive obligation?
A: Show answer
A legal obligation derives from a legal requirement, contract or another enforceable basis recognized by the applicable framework. A constructive obligation may arise from an entity's actions, established practices, published policies or sufficiently specific statements that create a valid expectation among affected parties that the entity will accept a responsibility. Whether a constructive obligation exists depends on the facts and the applicable accounting requirements. A general intention to assist a beneficiary or continue funding a project should not automatically be treated as a constructive obligation. The file should explain the relevant conduct or communication, the affected parties' expectations, the entity's ability to avoid the commitment and the evidence supporting the conclusion. Legal review is appropriate when enforceability or the effect of particular statements is uncertain.

Study instruction:
- Compare a contractual restoration requirement with a publicly communicated commitment and identify the evidence necessary to assess each obligation.
Key point:
- Legal and constructive obligations have different evidential bases.
- A constructive obligation requires analysis of the entity's actions and the expectations those actions create.
Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 and constructive obligations
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
8. Q: How should the probability of an outflow be assessed without relying on arbitrary thresholds?
A: Show answer
The assessment should use the probability terminology and recognition threshold specified by the applicable standard, supported by available evidence and informed judgment. Relevant considerations include legal advice, the facts of the event, claim history, contractual provisions, settlement experience, comparable cases and developments known at the reporting date. An organization should not invent a universal percentage threshold simply because it is convenient for a spreadsheet. The UN Finance Manual describes specific guidance for the United Nations, including an indicative monetary threshold for its internal process; that threshold must not be transferred automatically to another NGO. Record the reasoning, assumptions, uncertainty, reviewer and date, and explain what new evidence would change the conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Review three different legal-outcome scenarios and explain how the applicable probability terminology and evidence affect classification.
Key point:
- Use the threshold prescribed by the governing framework, not an unapproved internal percentage.
- Probability judgments require evidence, documented reasoning and periodic reassessment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Recognition and probability assessment
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Recognition criteria
9. Q: What should Finance do when an obligation exists but its amount cannot be estimated precisely?
A: Show answer
Finance should distinguish uncertainty from an absence of reliable measurement. Legal advice, comparable outcomes, contractual calculations, independent valuations, scenario analysis and reasonable estimation techniques may make a sufficiently reliable estimate possible even when the final settlement amount is unknown. Document the available range, assumptions, methodology, data limitations and sensitivity to significant factors. If the relevant standard's recognition conditions are met and a reliable estimate is possible, the absence of an exact invoice or final judgment does not automatically prevent recognition. If reliable measurement genuinely cannot be achieved, assess the treatment and disclosure required by the governing framework rather than silently omitting the matter. Obtain specialist input where internal expertise is insufficient and preserve the unresolved issue in the register.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an estimation file for a disputed claim using available legal evidence, comparable outcomes and a documented range of outcomes.
Key point:
- Estimates are permitted where supported by reasonable evidence and an appropriate method.
- An unquantified exposure requires an explicit accounting conclusion and follow-up.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Measurement and estimation
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
10. Q: When should an uncertain obligation be disclosed as a contingent liability rather than recognized as a provision?
A: Show answer
Under IAS 37-style requirements, a contingent liability includes a possible obligation whose existence depends on uncertain future events and a present obligation that does not qualify for recognition because the required outflow is not probable or reliable measurement is not possible. A contingent liability is not recognized as a liability in the statement of financial position under that framework; disclosure is generally required unless the possibility of outflow is remote. The actual entity must apply the disclosure threshold and exceptions in its own governing framework. The case file should contain the nature of the exposure, the supporting facts, the potential financial effect where reasonably estimable, the major uncertainties and the reason for the classification. Reassess the case at each reporting date.

Study instruction:
- Compare a provision with a possible obligation and a present obligation that fails a recognition criterion, documenting the relevant disclosure conclusion for each.
Key point:
- Non-recognition does not automatically mean that no disclosure is necessary.
- Contingent-liability conclusions must be updated as facts and probabilities change.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Contingent liabilities
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 disclosure requirements
11. Q: How should a possible recovery or contingent asset be assessed?
A: Show answer
Potential recoveries may arise from insurance claims, contractual indemnities, legal proceedings or other rights whose existence or realization remains uncertain. Finance should establish the underlying event, the legal or contractual basis for recovery, applicable conditions, enforceability, counterparty capacity to pay and evidence of collectability. Under IAS 37-style requirements, a contingent asset is not recognized merely because an inflow appears possible; disclosure may be appropriate when the applicable probability threshold is met, and recognition becomes appropriate when the inflow is virtually certain under that framework. The entity must confirm the corresponding rules under its actual standard. Maintain a separate recovery assessment and avoid using an uncertain recovery to conceal or reduce a provision without meeting the applicable offsetting and recognition requirements.

Study instruction:
- Assess an insurance claim separately from the obligation it may reimburse and identify the evidence required at each stage.
Key point:
- A potential recovery is not automatically a recognized asset.
- Recovery recognition and disclosure must follow the applicable criteria independently of the related obligation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Contingent assets and reimbursements
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
12. Q: How should the organization identify all events that may require provision or contingent-item assessment?
A: Show answer
Identification should not depend solely on a questionnaire sent to Finance at year-end. The case owner should use recurring information requests and targeted checks of legal correspondence, contract registers, procurement records, complaints, insurance notifications, regulator communications, board minutes, donor or partner disputes and operational incident logs. HR, Legal, Grants, Procurement, project teams and senior management should confirm whether they know of new or ongoing matters. Reconcile newly identified matters with the prior-period register so that unresolved cases are not omitted. Record the date identified, affected reporting period, owner, source document and initial assessment status. Where evidence is incomplete, preserve the case as open and assign follow-up rather than treating the absence of information as proof that no exposure exists.

Study instruction:
- Build a reporting-date completeness checklist and compare departmental confirmations with the case register and prior-period open items.
Key point:
- Complete identification requires multiple sources and responsible-owner confirmations.
- Prior-period cases must remain in the population until properly resolved.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Information requests and identification
13. Q: What information should be captured when a new provision or contingent-item case is opened?
A: Show answer
Each case should receive a unique identifier and record the event date, date reported, description, category, entity, counterparty, affected contracts, relevant projects, reporting date, currency, source references, owner, responsible legal contact and initial status. Subsequent assessment fields should capture the applicable framework, obligation analysis, probability, estimate method, range, financial-statement classification, disclosure conclusion, reviewer, approver and next review date. The case should also link to evidence, journal references, recovery assessments and any corrective actions. Define mandatory fields and validation rules so that critical information cannot be silently omitted. Do not treat opening a case as an accounting conclusion; the register is the control mechanism for ensuring that assessment happens, evidence is collected and the authorized decision is documented.

Study instruction:
- Design a case-intake form with mandatory fields, controlled status values, source-document links and a clear assessment workflow.
Key point:
- Unique case IDs and structured records support completeness and traceability.
- Intake, accounting assessment, approval and closure are separate stages.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Case information and assessment
14. Q: How should provisions be measured when the settlement amount is uncertain?
A: Show answer
The measurement should follow the best-estimate principle and other requirements of the applicable standard. The estimate should reflect the circumstances existing at the reporting date, relevant risks and uncertainties, the expected settlement method and timing, and evidence available from contracts, advisers, historical outcomes or technical analysis. A single possible obligation and a large population of similar obligations may require different estimation techniques. The method, inputs, assumptions, calculation, evidence, reviewer and version should be recorded so that another competent reviewer can reproduce the result. Finance should distinguish estimation uncertainty from unsupported speculation and should not choose a number merely to achieve a preferred surplus, deficit or budget outcome.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a provision estimate using documented assumptions and verify that every material input has an identifiable source and rationale.
Key point:
- Measurement should reflect the applicable standard and reporting-date evidence.
- A reproducible methodology is essential for review and auditability.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provision measurement
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 measurement requirements
15. Q: When should expected-value calculations be used instead of the most likely outcome?
A: Show answer
The technique should reflect the nature of the obligation and the measurement method required by the governing standard. For a large population of similar obligations, probability-weighted outcomes may provide a more representative estimate. For a single obligation, the most likely outcome may be a useful starting point, but other possible outcomes and their implications may also need consideration. A single expected-value calculation should not be applied indiscriminately to every legal claim or contract dispute. Maintain a documented link between scenarios, probabilities, assumptions, source evidence and the selected estimate. An independent reviewer should challenge whether the model reflects the actual population or case and whether extreme outcomes or asymmetric risks have been treated appropriately.

Study instruction:
- Compare a probability-weighted estimate for many similar claims with an assessment of one individually significant legal dispute.
Key point:
- Estimation techniques should fit the nature of the obligation and the applicable measurement rule.
- The most likely outcome does not automatically represent the best estimate in every case.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Measurement methods and weighted outcomes
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Estimation methods
16. Q: How should uncertainty, assumptions and sensitivity analysis be documented in a provision estimate?
A: Show answer
Each estimate should identify the main drivers of possible change, such as legal outcomes, claim value, settlement timing, remediation costs, interest or penalties. Record the base assumptions, reasonably relevant alternative scenarios, the effect of changing material inputs and the evidence supporting the selected outcome. A sensitivity analysis should explain which assumptions materially affect the value or timing, rather than simply display arbitrary high and low cases. Significant changes from the prior reporting date should have a documented explanation. If evidence is insufficient, identify the limitation and what information is needed to resolve it. Management must not treat the range of possible outcomes as permission to select the most convenient amount without support from the accounting framework and the facts.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a sensitivity schedule for a material claim and explain which assumption produces the largest change in the estimate.
Key point:
- Significant uncertainty must be visible and traceable.
- Sensitivity analysis supports judgment; it does not replace recognition criteria or independent review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Measurement uncertainty
17. Q: When should a provision be discounted to present value?
A: Show answer
Discounting should be evaluated when the effect of the time value of money is material under the applicable framework. Finance should estimate the expected timing and amount of settlement, assess materiality, select an appropriate discount rate consistent with the standard and document the rationale. The entity should not adopt the specific discount-rate source used by the United Nations unless it is genuinely applicable to its reporting requirements. The calculation should distinguish the initial measurement from subsequent changes in estimates and the unwinding of any discount. If discounting is immaterial and the standard permits a non-discounted amount, document the basis for that conclusion. Review timing assumptions at each reporting date because changes can materially affect the estimate.

Study instruction:
- Compare an undiscounted long-term obligation with its present value and document the rate, timing assumptions and materiality conclusion.
Key point:
- Discounting depends on the applicable standard and materiality of the time value of money.
- Discount rates and assumptions require documented support and periodic review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Discounting and measurement
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 measurement requirements
18. Q: How should a previously discounted provision be updated in later reporting periods?
A: Show answer
The organization should reassess the expected settlement amount, timing and discounting assumptions at each required reporting date. Where the applicable framework requires discounting, the opening amount, new provisions, utilization, remeasurement, reversals and effects of unwinding the discount should be separately identified. Unwinding generally reflects the passage of time and is accounted for according to the applicable standard and the organization's chart of accounts. Do not confuse the unwinding expense with a cash settlement or a new legal obligation. Maintain a movement schedule that can be reconciled to the general ledger and supporting valuation calculations. Changes in methodology or rate should be approved and distinguished from changes caused by the underlying case facts.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile an opening discounted provision to its closing amount and identify the effects of settlement, revised assumptions and discount unwinding.
Key point:
- Subsequent movements must be explained by identifiable causes.
- Discounting calculations and journal entries must follow the governing framework.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Adjusting and discounting provisions
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
19. Q: How should insurance recoveries and contractual indemnities be accounted for alongside a provision?
A: Show answer
First establish whether the underlying obligation qualifies for recognition and measurement. Then assess the recovery independently by reviewing the enforceable right, policy or contract terms, conditions, limits, deductibles, exclusions, counterparty capacity and evidence of collectability. A recognized provision should not automatically be reduced because an insurance claim or indemnity is expected. Under IAS 37-style requirements, reimbursement recognition is subject to specific conditions, including virtual certainty of receipt when the entity settles the obligation, and is limited according to the applicable requirements. The accounting presentation should also follow the governing framework. Keep the gross obligation and the recovery assessment separately traceable, and reassess both when new evidence becomes available.

Study instruction:
- Analyze an insured legal claim using separate files for the obligation, insurer's coverage decision, collectability and proposed financial-statement treatment.
Key point:
- An expected recovery is not automatically an offset against a provision.
- Rights, limits, collectability and recognition requirements must be evaluated separately.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Reimbursements and contingent assets
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
20. Q: How should an onerous contract be identified and assessed?
A: Show answer
Review executed contractual terms to establish the remaining obligations, unavoidable costs, remaining economic benefits and available exit or mitigation options. Under IAS 37-style requirements, an onerous contract is assessed when the unavoidable costs of meeting the contractual obligations exceed the economic benefits expected under the contract. The cost assessment must follow the applicable framework, including relevant directly attributable costs and any required impairment assessment of assets used to fulfil the contract. Do not automatically treat a negative project budget, funding shortfall or unfavorable future forecast as an onerous-contract provision. Assess the contractual substance and avoid double-counting costs already recognized elsewhere. Legal, procurement and Finance should jointly document the assessment and approve the resulting accounting conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Examine an executed service contract with remaining obligations and compare the expected benefits, unavoidable fulfillment costs and available termination options.
Key point:
- Onerous-contract accounting requires an assessment of contractual obligations and unavoidable costs.
- Future losses, poor budgets and contractual obligations are not interchangeable concepts.
Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 and onerous contracts
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provisions
21. Q: Why should future operating losses and restructuring plans be assessed separately from provisions for present obligations?
A: Show answer
A forecast loss by itself does not establish a present obligation arising from a past event. Under IAS 37-style requirements, future operating losses are not recognized as provisions simply because management expects operations to be unfavorable. Restructuring provisions require a separate assessment of the detailed plan, the obligations created and whether the necessary conditions for a constructive obligation have been met. Specific commitments, existing contracts or other events may create separate accounting consequences and should be assessed individually. The entity must verify the applicable rules before implementation. Finance should distinguish a budget forecast, an approved management plan and an obligation that meets recognition criteria, documenting the evidence supporting each conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Compare an approved budget showing future losses with a documented restructuring plan and identify which facts could create a present obligation.
Key point:
- An expected future operating loss is not automatically a provision.
- Restructuring recognition depends on the applicable criteria and supporting evidence.
Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 and future operating losses and restructuring
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
22. Q: What role should Legal play in assessing claims and disputed obligations?
A: Show answer
Legal should supply factual and legal analysis within its authority, including the status of proceedings, relevant contractual clauses, available defenses, settlement discussions, deadlines and material developments. Finance remains responsible for applying the governing accounting framework, determining the financial-reporting treatment and ensuring that the conclusion is supported by the appropriate evidence and approval. Legal advice should identify its scope, date, assumptions and limitations. Privileged or confidential materials must be stored and shared under the applicable access controls. If Legal cannot provide a final outcome prediction, Finance should still consider whether another reasonable estimation method or disclosure assessment is possible. Lack of a definitive legal answer should lead to documented follow-up, not automatic omission of the case.

Study instruction:
- Trace a legal claim from the initial notification through the request for counsel input, accounting assessment and authorized financial-reporting decision.
Key point:
- Legal advice informs accounting judgment but does not replace it.
- Confidential legal evidence must be protected while remaining available to authorized reviewers.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Corporate Guidance — Identification and assessment
23. Q: What controls are needed when a claim remains unquantified at the reporting date?
A: Show answer
An unquantified case should remain visible in the register with the reason the amount cannot yet be estimated, the information requested, the responsible owner, outstanding dependencies and next review date. Assess whether available evidence permits an estimate or range, whether specialist input is needed, and what disclosure requirements apply. Identify the impact of the uncertainty on financial-statement preparation and management decisions. The case should not be marked closed or cleared merely because Legal has not supplied a figure. Equally, an unquantified amount must not automatically be recorded using an arbitrary estimate. Maintain an explicit decision status, escalate material unresolved matters and document the authorized treatment for the reporting date.

Study instruction:
- Review an unquantified claim and produce a documented evidence-request plan, estimation assessment and reporting-date conclusion.
Key point:
- Inability to produce an exact figure does not justify omitting the assessment.
- Every unresolved case needs an owner, evidence plan and review date.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Assessment of provisions and contingent liabilities
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
24. Q: How should provision recognition, utilization, remeasurement and reversal be recorded in the general ledger?
A: Show answer
Once the authorized accounting conclusion has been reached, Finance should prepare a journal that identifies the case, reporting date, amount, currency, debit and credit accounts, relevant project or cost dimensions, source estimate, preparer, reviewer and approval reference. Recognition will ordinarily affect the applicable liability and expense or other relevant account, subject to the governing framework and transaction facts. Utilization should relate to expenditure or settlement for which the provision was established. Remeasurement reflects changes in the estimated obligation; reversal is appropriate when the recognition criteria are no longer met or the provision is no longer required under the applicable standard. Keep the original assessment and subsequent changes traceable, and reconcile postings to the case register and closing balance.

Study instruction:
- Prepare illustrative journals for initial recognition, settlement against a provision, upward remeasurement and reversal, with reviewer approval for each entry.
Key point:
- Journals must reflect approved accounting conclusions rather than automated assumptions.
- Utilization, remeasurement and reversal represent different causes of movement.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Recognition and provision movements
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and journal workflows
25. Q: How should a provision roll-forward be prepared and reconciled to the general ledger?
A: Show answer
The roll-forward should show the opening balance, new provisions, increases or decreases from remeasurement, amounts utilized, reversals, discount-related movements where applicable and closing balance. Present movements at an appropriate level of detail by case, currency, classification and reporting period. Reconcile the calculated closing register balance to the general ledger, with differences investigated and documented before final approval. The roll-forward should link to source estimates, settlement evidence, journal references and the disclosure schedule. A balanced calculation is necessary but does not prove that the underlying estimates or classification are correct. The reviewer must independently assess both mathematical accuracy and accounting judgment, and unresolved differences should remain open and escalated until properly resolved.

Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the closing balance from the opening provision and all supported movements, then reconcile the result to the ledger.
Key point:
- Every material movement must have a clear cause and supporting evidence.
- Register-to-ledger agreement does not replace an assessment of estimate reasonableness.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
26. Q: What information should be included in financial-statement disclosures for provisions and contingent liabilities?
A: Show answer
Disclosure requirements depend on the governing accounting framework and materiality. Under IAS 37-style requirements, disclosures for recognized provisions generally explain the nature of the obligation, expected timing, uncertainties affecting the amount or timing and significant assumptions, together with the required movement information. For contingent liabilities, the disclosure generally describes the nature of the exposure, an estimate of the financial effect where practicable, uncertainties about timing or amount and possible reimbursements where relevant. Where an estimate cannot be made, the reason should be addressed as required by the applicable standard. Do not publish sensitive legal information unnecessarily, but do not treat confidentiality as an automatic exemption from financial-reporting disclosure. The final disclosure must be reconciled to the case register and reviewed against the relevant standard.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a disclosure checklist and reconcile each material provision and contingent-liability case to the proposed notes.
Key point:
- Recognized provisions and unrecognized contingencies can have different disclosure requirements.
- Disclosure completeness, materiality and permitted exceptions must be assessed under the applicable framework.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37 disclosure requirements
27. Q: How should events after the reporting date affect provisions and contingent-item assessments?
A: Show answer
Determine whether information received after the reporting date provides additional evidence about a condition that existed at that date or instead relates to a new condition arising afterward. Under IAS 10-style requirements, an adjusting event may require adjustments to reported amounts, while a material non-adjusting event generally requires disclosure rather than adjustment. The entity must apply the corresponding rules in its own reporting framework. Relevant events may include court decisions, settlement agreements, new legal claims, insurer decisions or significant developments in an existing dispute. Record the event date, the condition it relates to, the evidence obtained, the accounting analysis and the authorization for any adjustment or disclosure. Do not retrospectively change an estimate solely because a later event created a new condition that did not exist at period-end.

Study instruction:
- Classify several post-period legal developments as evidence of existing conditions or as new conditions, and document the reporting consequence.
Key point:
- The timing and nature of the underlying condition determine the treatment of subsequent events.
- Material post-period developments need assessment before financial statements are authorized for issue.
Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 10 Events after the Reporting Period
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Events after the Reporting Date
28. Q: Why must all open cases be reassessed at each reporting date?
A: Show answer
The accounting conclusion for an earlier reporting period may no longer be appropriate because new evidence, legal developments, settlement negotiations, cost estimates or contractual changes alter the facts. At each required reporting date, the case owner should confirm current status and provide new evidence; Finance should reassess recognition, measurement and disclosure; Legal should update its analysis when relevant; and an independent reviewer should evaluate the revised conclusion. The process should include all previous-period open cases, not only recently reported events. Document whether the classification or estimate changed and why. A case should not retain a provision merely because one was recognized previously, nor remain a contingent liability solely because that classification appeared in the prior-year statements.

Study instruction:
- Compare the prior-period and current-period assessment for an open claim and explain every change in probability, measurement, classification or disclosure.
Key point:
- Prior-year accounting conclusions are not automatically valid for the current period.
- Reassessment must be systematic, evidence-based and documented.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Reassessment of provisions and contingencies
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
29. Q: When can a provision be utilized, reversed or closed?
A: Show answer
Utilization occurs when the obligation is settled or qualifying expenditure is incurred for the purpose for which the provision was originally established, subject to the applicable accounting rules. A provision should be reassessed when actual settlement differs from the estimate, when facts change or when the obligation no longer meets the recognition criteria. Reversal must be supported by the relevant accounting requirements and the current facts; the balance should not remain merely because nobody has processed the adjustment. Formal closure should confirm the legal or contractual status, settlement evidence, remaining exposure, recovery status, journal treatment, disclosure update and retention requirements. Where an appeal or residual claim remains possible, the case may need continued monitoring rather than complete closure.

Study instruction:
- Follow a claim through settlement and compare the final payment with the provision, unused balance, required journal and closure evidence.
Key point:
- Utilization, reversal and case closure are distinct actions.
- Closure requires evidence that accounting, legal, disclosure and monitoring obligations have been addressed.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Utilization, adjustment and reversal
30. Q: How should responsibilities be divided among case owners, Finance, Legal, reviewers and approvers?
A: Show answer
The procedure should assign responsibility for reporting an event, collecting evidence, assessing contractual or legal facts, calculating estimates, determining accounting treatment, preparing disclosures, approving conclusions, posting journals and maintaining records. Where practical, the person preparing an estimate or journal should not be the sole reviewer and approver. If staffing constraints prevent complete segregation, independent supervisory review and documented post-entry checks should compensate for the conflict. Delegations, monetary limits, substitute approvers and escalation routes must be defined. The responsibility matrix should match actual permissions and workflows rather than merely list job titles. Consultants may provide specialist advice but should not silently replace the organization's authorized decision-maker or assume responsibilities not formally delegated to them.

Study instruction:
- Create a responsibility matrix for one provision case and identify incompatible duties and the controls needed when complete separation is impractical.
Key point:
- Preparation, review, approval, posting and closure should have clear accountability.
- Any segregation-of-duties limitation requires a documented compensating control.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Roles and responsibilities
31. Q: How should approval authority and materiality thresholds be established?
A: Show answer
The organization should approve a documented delegation framework defining who can approve case assessments, estimates, journal entries, disclosures, settlements and exceptions. Monetary approval limits should align with the organization's governance arrangements, risk profile and applicable requirements. Recognition criteria in an accounting standard must not be replaced by an internal convenience threshold. If an internal reporting threshold is used to prioritize review, it should not suppress an item that must be assessed or disclosed under the applicable framework. Escalation should also consider qualitative factors, regulatory interest, reputational exposure, donor implications, precedent-setting legal questions and potential management conflicts. Evidence of approval must identify the decision version and any conditions imposed, and must remain available for subsequent review.

Study instruction:
- Compare an approved monetary delegation schedule with the applicable recognition and disclosure requirements, and identify any inappropriate threshold interaction.
Key point:
- Management approval limits do not override accounting recognition requirements.
- Materiality and risk escalation must reflect both financial and qualitative considerations.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
- IFRS Foundation — IAS 37
32. Q: What evidence should be retained to support a provision or contingent-item conclusion?
A: Show answer
Evidence should support the underlying event, obligation, probability assessment, estimate, recognition or disclosure decision, journal and approval. Relevant records may include contracts, claims, invoices, legal correspondence, counsel assessments, insurance policies, insurer responses, cost estimates, settlement agreements, calculation workbooks, reviewer comments and final disclosures. Each item should be linked to a case identifier and reporting date. Record the source, date obtained, version and responsible custodian, and preserve changes without overwriting prior approved conclusions. Privileged legal materials should be stored with restricted access; ordinary accounting records should remain accessible to authorized reviewers and auditors. Where evidence is missing, record the gap, owner and due date rather than treating the file as complete.

Study instruction:
- Select a material case and construct an evidence index linking the underlying event, accounting assessment, approval, journal and disclosure.
Key point:
- The conclusion must be reproducible from retained supporting evidence.
- Evidence gaps and access restrictions require active management and documentation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Supporting information and audit evidence
33. Q: How should donor agreements and partner contracts influence a provisions assessment?
A: Show answer
Donor agreements, implementation contracts and partnership arrangements may create obligations, reimbursement rights, indemnities, repayment requirements or dispute-resolution mechanisms. Finance should review the relevant clauses with Legal and the contract owner, identify the event that may trigger a present obligation, and document the consequences for the entity. A contractual clause alone does not settle every accounting question; recognition, measurement and disclosure must still follow the applicable framework. Donor restrictions or repayment provisions should not be assumed to create a provision without the required present obligation and other recognition conditions. Equally, contractual uncertainty should not be ignored merely because the original grant budget has closed. Maintain links between the case register, executed agreements, donor correspondence and any approved recovery or repayment decision.

Study instruction:
- Review an agreement containing an alleged ineligible expenditure or repayment clause and distinguish the contractual issue from its accounting conclusion.
Key point:
- Contract terms provide essential evidence but do not override the governing accounting framework.
- Donor and partner implications should be documented and assessed alongside legal and financial-reporting requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provisions and contingent items
34. Q: How should duplicate obligations and overlapping accounting assessments be prevented?
A: Show answer
Establish a unique case identifier and link related legal claims, contracts, counterparties, estimates, journals and disclosures. Before recognizing a provision, determine whether any amount has already been recognized as a payable, accrual, employee-benefit liability or another specific-standard liability. Review whether multiple claims represent separate obligations or different descriptions of the same underlying matter. Reconcile case-level estimates to the ledger and compare related recovery records to avoid double-counting an asset. When the same event affects several departments or projects, assign a single case owner and require agreement on the overall accounting conclusion. A matching algorithm can flag potential duplicates, but Finance must investigate the underlying facts rather than automatically deleting or consolidating items.

Study instruction:
- Test a case population containing duplicated references and overlapping accruals, and document how genuine separate obligations are distinguished from duplicate records.
Key point:
- Unique identifiers and cross-references reduce duplicate recognition and omission risks.
- Automated duplicate flags require review against the actual facts and accounting records.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
35. Q: What should happen when a case fails a mandatory control or contains incomplete information?
A: Show answer
The workflow should prevent the case from being marked approved or closed until the required fields, evidence, review and authorization are complete. Exceptions may include a missing legal assessment, an expired estimate, an unexplained register-to-ledger difference, an unresolved framework question or a proposed journal that differs from the approved amount. Record the issue, severity, affected reports, responsible owner, deadline, temporary safeguard, corrective action and independent retest. Where a reporting deadline is at risk, escalate the matter promptly to the authorized decision-maker. Do not conceal a deficiency by changing the status to complete or moving the case into the next reporting period. Any permitted exception must have a documented basis and treatment under the governing policy and reporting framework.

Study instruction:
- Create a control-exception log and test that unresolved high-risk cases cannot move to final approval without the required authorization.
Key point:
- Mandatory workflow gates prevent incomplete cases from being treated as approved.
- Every exception needs ownership, a deadline, remediation and evidence of closure.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and control processes
36. Q: How should confidential information, privileged evidence and legal holds be handled?
A: Show answer
Classify records according to confidentiality, privilege, legal-hold and retention requirements. Restrict sensitive documents to authorized personnel and avoid embedding privileged legal advice in a general-purpose workbook or widely distributed management report. Maintain an accessible evidence index that identifies the document, custodian, access restrictions, storage location and related case without unnecessarily exposing its full content. Where litigation or regulatory action requires preservation, suspend routine disposal for relevant records according to the applicable legal and organizational instructions. Review access rights after staff changes and when advisers or contractors no longer require access. Disposal should occur only under an approved retention process and after relevant legal holds have been checked.

Study instruction:
- Review one restricted legal case and verify permissions, evidence indexing, legal-hold checks and the authorized disclosure process.
Key point:
- Confidentiality controls must protect sensitive evidence without destroying auditability.
- Legal holds and applicable retention requirements take precedence over routine disposal practices.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
37. Q: How should a case management workbook or information system support the accounting procedure?
A: Show answer
A controlled system can maintain the case register, evidence references, estimate versions, review dates, approvals, journals, disclosures and exception statuses. Excel tables and validation lists can standardize identifiers, dates, currencies and allowed status values. Power Query can combine authorized source registers and identify potential duplicates or missing records. VBA or another approved workflow tool can generate review packs and status reports where the organization has designed and tested suitable controls. The technology should support, not determine, the accounting conclusion. Access must be restricted according to role, important changes must be traceable, and source data must not be overwritten without a controlled change process. Automated outputs require reconciliation to authoritative source records and review before being used for financial reporting.

Study instruction:
- Design a basic case register and test its mandatory fields, links, refresh process, approval statuses and exception reporting using controlled sample data.
Key point:
- Technology supports completeness, consistency and traceability but does not replace professional judgment.
- Workflow and data controls must be designed before automation is released.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and controlled workflows
38. Q: What tests should be performed before a provision-management workbook is used in production?
A: Show answer
Test valid and invalid inputs, mandatory fields, unique case IDs, missing evidence, calculation accuracy, duplicate detection, status transitions, role permissions, journal references, roll-forward reconciliation and disclosure outputs. Include edge cases such as negative adjustments, partial settlement, full reversal, foreign-currency items where relevant, missing approvals and changes after approval. Independently calculate representative examples and compare the results with the workbook. Verify that a refreshed dataset has the expected record count and control totals and that errors produce clear warnings rather than silently generating incorrect output. Record the test version, expected result, actual result, tester, reviewer, defects and retest evidence. Production deployment should require documented approval and an appropriate rollback or recovery plan.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a positive-and-negative test suite and demonstrate that each important failure mode produces a detectable and controlled result.
Key point:
- Independent testing is required to establish calculation and workflow reliability.
- An untested workbook must not be treated as an approved financial-reporting control.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18
39. Q: How should management monitor whether the provisions procedure is operating effectively?
A: Show answer
Management should monitor overdue assessments, completeness of departmental confirmations, open cases without current framework analysis, missing legal updates, unexplained ledger differences, estimates overdue for review, recurring estimation errors and cases lacking evidence or approval. Each indicator needs a defined population, calculation method, data source, owner, frequency and escalation rule. A dashboard should distinguish genuinely completed cases from items merely marked complete in a workbook. Review trends over several reporting periods and investigate the causes of persistent exceptions. For material issues, management should assign corrective actions with named owners, deadlines and independent verification. Monitoring should assess both processing timeliness and the quality of accounting judgments, since a fast process can still produce materially incorrect financial statements.

Study instruction:
- Define a small set of operational and accounting-quality indicators and test their results against the case register, general ledger and supporting evidence.
Key point:
- Effective monitoring measures both process completion and the quality of accounting conclusions.
- Repeated exceptions should lead to root-cause analysis and measurable corrective action.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
40. Q: How should changes in accounting standards and regulations be incorporated into the SOP?
A: Show answer
Assign a policy owner to monitor official standard-setter publications, applicable Indonesian requirements, relevant legal developments and internal policy changes. For each change, record the source, publication date, effective date, affected cases, required process or calculation changes, training implications and approval authority. Distinguish issued requirements from exposure drafts, tentative decisions and proposed amendments. For example, the IASB's September 2026 update discusses redeliberations on proposed amendments concerning provisions; it should not be treated as an effective replacement for existing IAS 37 requirements merely because the project is progressing. Before implementing any change, assess its actual applicability, test the affected procedures and data, approve the new version and communicate the effective date. Preserve prior approved versions so earlier reporting decisions can be understood using the rules applicable at the time.

Study instruction:
- Review an official standard-setter update and document whether it is a proposal, an issued requirement or an effective amendment for the entity concerned.
Key point:
- Standard changes require applicability analysis, approval, testing and communication.
- Proposed amendments must not be presented as operative accounting requirements.
Source links:
- IFRS Foundation — IASB Update, September 2026
- Indonesian Institute of Accountants — PSAK provisions and contingent-item guidance
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15
41. Q: What should an end-to-end walkthrough verify before the SOP is adopted?
A: Show answer
Select a completed case and trace it from the initial event and supporting documents through legal or contractual analysis, framework selection, probability assessment, measurement, independent review, approval, journal or disclosure preparation, reporting-date reassessment and archival. Verify that the evidence actually supports the recorded decision rather than relying solely on explanations provided by staff. Compare the source estimate, approved journal, ledger entry and financial-statement output, and document all differences. Test whether responsible roles and escalation procedures operated as intended. Any gap should be recorded with its risk, corrective action, owner and target date. Adoption should remain blocked until the organization has approved the appropriate framework, assigned responsibilities, resolved critical deficiencies and authorized the final procedure for operational use.

Study instruction:
- Perform a documented walkthrough of one material case and identify any missing evidence, unapproved steps or discrepancies between the register and the reported figures.
Key point:
- Walkthroughs test actual execution, not merely whether a procedure exists on paper.
- Formal adoption requires authorized approval and resolution of critical implementation issues.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provision and contingency procedures
42. Q: How should management ensure the procedure remains reliable after implementation?
A: Show answer
Establish a recurring review cycle covering the policy, framework applicability, delegated authority, role access, form design, estimation methodologies, disclosure checklist, training and system controls. Monitor whether open cases are reviewed on time, whether estimates are supported by current evidence, whether journal movements reconcile and whether prior findings have been resolved. When errors recur, investigate their root causes and determine whether the policy, instructions, training, data requirements or workflow controls need revision. Release changes only after appropriate review and testing, with documented version information and communication to affected users. For the first reporting cycle after implementation, arrange an independent review of the register, key accounting conclusions, ledger reconciliation and disclosures. The objective is sustained control effectiveness, not simply completion of the initial implementation checklist.

Study instruction:
- Create an annual SOP review plan and define the evidence needed to demonstrate that corrective actions and post-implementation checks have been completed.
Key point:
- The procedure should evolve through controlled, evidence-based improvement.
- Ongoing review must cover accounting judgments, data integrity, approvals and reporting outcomes.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
